Compact and Divisible E-Cash with Threshold Issuance Volume: YYYY X DOI: XXXXXXX.XXXXXXX
Alfredo Rial, Ania M. Piotrowska

Abstract
. Decentralized, offline, and privacy-preserving e-cash could fulfil the need for both scalable and byzantine fault-resistant payment systems. Existing offline anonymous e-cash schemes are unsuitable for distributed environments due to a central bank. We construct a distributed offline anonymous e-cash scheme, in which the role of the bank is performed by a quorum of authorities, and present its two instantiations. Our first scheme is compact, i.e. the cost of the issuance protocol and the size of a wallet are independent of the number of coins issued, but the cost of payment grows linearly with the number of coins spent. Our second scheme is divisible and thus the cost of payments is also independent of the number of coins spent, but the verification of deposits is more costly. We provide formal security proof of both schemes and compare the efficiency of their implementations.



1.
Introduction

At the present moment, there is a pressing need for private electronic cash (e-cash), as shown by growing interest in blockchain-based systems and Centrally-Banked Digital Currencies (CBDCs), but no privacy-enhanced and decentralized system exists that scales to the requirements of this application scenario. To address this problem, we introduce the first decentralized offline e-cash scheme with provable security and full implementation, that can efficiently support electronic payments. In contrast to the current privacy-enhanced blockchain, systems do not require a constant online presence of the payees. The issuance of coins is non-interactive, i.e., the authorities do not need to synchronise, as we do not rely on MPC protocols.
Moreover, our scheme maps to distributed payment systems such as CBDCs, a technology that urgently requires further attention in terms of privacy.
The idea of distributing issuance among a quorum of authorities has been so far explored in the context
of attribute-based credentials
(
Sonnino et al. 2019
;
Rial and Piotrowska 2022
)
and online anonymous e-cash schemes
(
Baudet et al. 2022
)
.

Contributions:

Our paper makes the following contributions:

•

We introduce a construction
\mathrm{\Pi}_{\mathrm{EC}}
for threshold issuance offline e-cash (
\mathrm{EC}
). To the best of our knowledge, this is the first offline e-cash scheme with threshold issuance. To this end, we define the system model and the security properties in the ideal-world/real-world paradigm
(
Canetti 2001
)
by proposing an ideal functionality
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{EC}}
for
\mathrm{EC}
(Sections §
3
, §
4
).

•

We propose two instantiations of
\mathrm{\Pi}_{\mathrm{EC}}
based on compact
(
Camenisch et al. 2005
)
and divisible
(
Pointcheval et al. 2017
)
e-cash (Section §
5
). Our schemes are more efficient than
(
Camenisch et al. 2005
;
Pointcheval et al. 2017
)
thanks to the use of Pointcheval-Sanders signatures in the random oracle model and to decreasing the number of coin secrets.
We formally prove that our construction
\mathrm{\Pi}_{\mathrm{EC}}
realizes
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{EC}}
when instantiated with the algorithms of our compact and divisible e-cash schemes (Sections §
D
, §
E
).

•

We provide an open-source Rust implementation of both schemes and present an extensive evaluation of their performance and trade-offs (Section §
6
). To the best of our knowledge, this is first such practical comparison.

•

We conclude by outlining how our schemes can be integrated with a blockchain-based bulletin board (Section §
7
). This would allow our scheme to fulfil the requirements for a distributed privacy-enhanced CBDC
(
Bank 2023
)
and even provide scalability via offline transactions for existing blockchain systems like ZCash
(
Sasson et al. 2014
)
.

2.
Background and Motivation

Anonymous e-cash was originally proposed by Chaum as a digital analog of regular cash, which also allows for private payments
(
Chaum 1982
)
.
Unlike physical cash, e-coins are easy to duplicate, hence e-cash schemes must prevent double-spending and typically that is done by having a centralized bank
(
Camenisch et al. 2005
;
Belenkiy et al. 2009
;
Canard and Gouget 2007
;
Canard et al. 2015b
;
Canard et al. 2015a
;
Pointcheval et al. 2017
;
Bourse et al. 2019
;
Okamoto and Ohta 1989
;
Okamoto and Ohta 1991
;
Baldimtsi et al. 2015
;
Bauer et al. 2021
)
. Thus, there has been a revival of interest in adopting privacy to decentralized blockchain systems, in which coins are authenticated by proving in ZK that they belong to a public list of valid coins maintained on the blockchain, thus they do not require a central bank to prevent double-spending
(
Miers et al. 2013
)
.

Although blockchain-based privacy-enhanced systems such as ZCash and Monero have users
(
Sasson et al. 2014
;
van Saberhagen 2013
;
Noether and Mackenzie 2016
)
, such
decentralized e-cash systems require being online to check the status of payments, which simply does not scale to the speed of transactions needed by real-world payment systems or support the reality of transactions that need to be made without internet access, so the usage of blockchains for payments remains small in proportion to traditional payments. Also, as could happen to any other low-transaction fee blockchain that advertises high throughput, the ZCash blockchain has suffered an attack of
‘spam’
transactions that have increased the blockchain size to such an extent that ZCash has suffered from what is effectively a
denial-of-service
attack that has collapsed its throughput (i.e., simply downloading the blockchain becomes nearly impossible)
(
Chawla 2022
)
. Similarly, ‘layer 2’ solutions based on blockchain technologies such as zero-knowledge roll-ups, which increase transaction speed and (in some cases) privacy, are also vulnerable to these attacks
(
Zamyatin et al. 2021
)
. By virtue of not requiring a merchant to be online all the time but only needing eventual settlement over regular epochs, our system avoids these issues while maintaining the advantages of decentralization. Hence, it is to be expected that current privacy-enhanced blockchain systems that require an online setting will evolve into decentralized offline e-cash systems similar to the one presented in this paper. While proposals exist to enable offline payments in blockchain-based cryptocurrencies
such as payment channel networks
(
Green and Miers 2017
)
,
they typically do not offer strong privacy protection as only users who share a channel can transact with each other and so users who make a payment are not anonymous, similarly as in
(
Dmitrienko et al. 2017
)
, and cashing out payments still requires online blockchain interactions. Even privacy-preserving offline payment channels effectively restrict payment transactions and the network topology of payment channels can lead participants to be effectively de-anoymized
(
Kappos et al. 2021
;
Sharma et al. 2022
)
.

On the other side of the spectrum, centralized CBDCs have gained increasing interest in over a hundred countries and will soon be deployed in Europe and China
(
Bank 2023
;
Xu 2022
)
. CBDC systems typically sacrifice user transaction privacy from the settlement layer and can lead to the possibility of dystopian surveillance of user financial transactions
(
Danezis and Meiklejohn 2016
)
. Although financial transaction data could be considered a matter of national security, MIT and the Federal Reserve of Massachusetts in the United States have begun exploring blockchain systems for its CBDC efforts without transaction unlinkability
(
Lovejoy et al. 2022
)
. In stark contrast, Switzerland’s Central Bank has put forward a centralized online privacy-preserving scheme called ‘eCash 2.0’, but offline payments would require special hardware and are currently not supported. The European Central Bank (ECB) recently published a list of requirements for the Digital Euro, which include privacy of user transactions from the settlement layer and offline transactions
(
Bank 2023
)
. Our scheme would fulfil those criteria, and we present how it could be integrated with a blockchain.
Furthermore, distributing the issuing power would be practical for emerging multi-nation economic proposals for joint issuance of currencies such as the South American joint reserve currency SUR in the ‘Banco del Sur’ recently endorsed by Brazil and Argentina
(
Marshall and Rochon 2009
)
. Even more importantly, practically preventing byzantine faults in centralized CBDCs requires it to be managed by a set of distributed parties, similar to Facebook’s Diem project’s consensus protocol based on HotStuff
(
Yin et al. 2019
)
. In this manner, our system unifies the objectives of blockchain research for decentralization while maintaining compatibility with the requirements for privacy-preserving CBDC by decentralizing e-cash.

Online vs Offline Ecash

To revisit the original e-cash proposal, the solution to double-spending is to associate each coin with a unique
serial number
, which is used to detect double-spending by dishonest users and prevent dishonest providers from depositing a payment twice
(
Chaum 1982
)
.
In
online e-cash
schemes
(
Chaum 1982
;
Sonnino et al. 2019
)
, providers are constantly connected to the bank
and can then check if a coin has been double-spent before accepting a payment. An alternative and more realistic solution space is given by
offline e-cash
schemes
(
Camenisch et al. 2005
;
Belenkiy et al. 2009
;
Canard and Gouget 2007
;
Canard et al. 2015b
;
Canard et al. 2015a
;
Pointcheval et al. 2017
;
Bourse et al. 2019
)
, which do not require a permanent online connection between a provider and the bank.
The provider can accept payment and deposit it at a later settlement stage as there is a guarantee that users who double-spend will be identified by the bank.

An important issue in the design of anonymous e-cash schemes is paying the exact amount as users cannot receive change,
since the providers are not anonymous towards the bank. If a user receives change, the user would in fact become a provider and lose anonymity.
In online e-cash, the user can contact the bank in order to exchange a coin for lower denominations in order to make the payment. In offline e-cash, contacting the bank is not allowed during the spending phase.

Transferable e-cash
schemes
(
Okamoto and Ohta 1989
;
Chaum and Pedersen 1992
;
Baldimtsi et al. 2015
)
are one solution to this problem,
allowing a user to further spent a previously received coin without interacting with the bank. Hence, providers can return the change to the users that paid.
Although transferable e-cash is appealing, state-of-the-art schemes
(
Bauer et al. 2021
)
are much less efficient than non-transferable ones.

An alternative solution to preserve user unlinkability is to use coins of the smallest denomination. However, the large number of coins that may need to be spent easily yields an inefficient scheme. To solve this problem, researchers have focused on designing offline e-cash protocols whose complexity does not depend on the number of coins withdrawn or spent.
In
anonymous compact e-cash
schemes
(
Camenisch et al. 2005
;
Belenkiy et al. 2009
)
, the cost of storing a wallet of
N
coins and the cost of withdrawing
N
coins is independent of
N
. However, the cost of spending
n\leq N
coins grows linearly with
n
.

Anonymous divisible e-cash
schemes
(
Okamoto and Ohta 1991
;
Canard and Gouget 2007
;
Canard et al. 2015b
;
Canard et al. 2015a
;
Pointcheval et al. 2017
;
Bourse et al. 2019
)
improve the efficiency of compact e-cash and allow the user to spend
n\leq N
coins with cost independent of
n
. Therefore, in the last decade, research has focused solely on divisible e-cash. However, divisible schemes achieve constant spending cost at the expense of much more expensive deposit and identification phases, and to our knowledge, efficiency analysis of compact e-cash schemes with multiple denominations has not been conducted, as well as a fair comparison between practical implementations of divisible and compact e-cash has never been done. Therefore, it is unclear which scheme is better for the use-case of offline e-cash as required by privacy-enhanced CBDCs
(
Bank 2023
)
and blockchain-based scalability.
Our analysis shows that compact e-cash with multiple denominations is preferable for small payments, which would naturally compose the majority of offline e-cash transactions in application scenarios such as a user-facing blockchain or CBDC where practical deployment concerns would necessitate distributed authorities.

In order to address the urgent scalability issues of blockchain systems and possibly dangerous centralization of CBDCs without privacy, a formal treatment of offline e-cash is needed, including a fair comparison of compact and divisible e-cash.

3.
Threshold Issuance Offline E-Cash

In this section, we introduce an offline e-cash scheme with threshold issuance (
\mathrm{EC}
). First, we outline its system model and informally define the security properties.
To define formally the security properties of
\mathrm{EC}
, we construct the ideal functionality
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{EC}}
and explain how it guarantees those properties.

3.1.
System model

An
\mathrm{EC}
scheme involves
n
authorities
(\mathcal{V}_{1},\allowbreak\ldots,\allowbreak\mathcal{V}_{n})
, any number of users
\mathcal{U}_{j}
and any number of providers
\mathcal{P}_{k}
.
The interaction between those parties takes place through a
setup
,
withdrawal
,
spend
and
deposit
phase. Users withdraw wallets containing one or more electronic coins from the authorities and spend them with providers, who then deposit them back with the authorities.

In the
setup
phase, a trusted third party
generates the public parameters
\mathit{params}
. Next, the public verification key

\mathit{pk}
is generated, alongside key pairs
(\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{V}_{i}},\allowbreak\mathit{pk}_{\mathcal{V}_{i}})_{i\in[1,n]}
for each of the authorities
\mathcal{V}_{i}
. The keys are generated in such a way that a user needs to receive a withdrawal from at least
t
authorities in order to create a wallet.
The key generation can be executed by a trusted third party or run in a distributed way
(
Kate et al. 2012
;
Groth 2021
)
.
Finally, each user
\mathcal{U}_{j}
generates a key pair
(\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}},\allowbreak\mathit{pk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}})
.

To obtain a wallet with
L
coins, where
L
is a public parameter of the scheme, a user
\mathcal{U}_{j}
engages in the
withdrawal
protocol. To this end,
\mathcal{U}_{j}
sends a request
\mathit{req}
to a set of
t
different authorities.
Each
\mathcal{V}_{i}
verifies
\mathit{req}
and using its secret key
\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{V}_{i}}
issues back a response
\mathit{res}
.

\mathcal{U}_{j}
verifies
\mathit{res}
and extracts from it a partial wallet
\mathit{W}_{i}
. Once
\mathcal{U}_{j}
has completed the protocol with at least
t
authorities,
and collected the threshold number of partial wallets,

\mathcal{U}_{j}
aggregates them to form
a single consolidated wallet
\mathit{W}
with
L
coins of the same monetary value. To
spend

V
coins with a provider
\mathcal{P}_{k}
the user generates a payment
\mathit{pay}
using her wallet
\mathit{W}
and payment information
\mathit{payinfo}
.
\mathit{payinfo}
contains the provider’s identity and other information about the payment and must be unique for each payment.
\mathcal{U}_{j}
sends
\mathit{pay}
to
\mathcal{P}_{k}
, who verifies it.
To
deposit
the payment
\mathit{pay}
, provider
\mathcal{P}_{k}
sends
\mathit{pay}
to a bulletin board
\mathrm{BB}
. Each authority reads
\mathit{pay}
from
\mathrm{BB}
, verifies it and checks it against all the payments previously written on
\mathrm{BB}
, in order to rule out double spending and double depositing.
The double spending detection mechanism reveals the public key of the user
\mathcal{U}_{j}
if the user double-spent any coin, while double depositing detection reveals that the payment is deposited twice if two payments contain the same payment information
\mathit{payinfo}
. Otherwise, the payment is deposited successfully.

3.2.
Security Properties

As defined in
(
Bourse et al. 2019
)
, secure anonymous offline e-cash schemes should satisfy four properties. We describe them informally, taking into account that, in our schemes, the bank is replaced by a number of authorities.

Traceability::

guarantees that no more coins can be deposited than those that have been withdrawn. In particular, adversarial parties are not able to forge wallets. It also guarantees that an honest authority is able to identify a user who double-spends a coin. Double-depositing is also detected by the authority.

Unlinkability::

ensures that no coalition of dishonest authorities, users and providers is able to link the withdrawal of the wallet with the spending of its coins. It also guarantees that multiple spendings performed by the same user cannot be linked with each other.

Exculpability::

requires that an honest user cannot be found guilty of double-spending.

Clearance::

ensures that only the provider that receives a payment is able to deposit it.

3.3.
Ideal Functionality

We define the security properties of offline e-cash with threshold issuance in the ideal-world/real-world paradigm. To this end, in Figure
1
we define the ideal functionality

\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{EC}}
.
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{EC}}
interacts with authorities
(\mathcal{V}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{V}_{n})
, any number of users
\mathcal{U}_{j}
and any number of providers
\mathcal{P}_{k}
.
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{EC}}
is parameterized by a threshold
t
, a universe of pseudonyms
\mathbb{U}_{p}
, a universe of wallet identifiers
\mathit{U_{w}}
, a universe of request identifiers
\mathit{U_{req}}
, a universe of payment information
\mathit{U_{info}}
, and a number
L
of coins in a wallet. In §
A
, we define the security properties of the cryptographic primitives used in our
\mathrm{EC}
schemes.

Remarks about
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{EC}}
.

When describing ideal functionalities, we use the conventions introduced in
(
Camenisch et al. 2016
)
and summarised in §
B
.

Aborts.:

When invoked by any party,
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{EC}}
first checks the correctness of the input.
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{EC}}
aborts if any of the inputs does not belong to the correct domain.
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{EC}}
also aborts if an interface is invoked at an incorrect moment in the protocol. For example, an authority
\mathcal{V}_{i}
cannot invoke the
\mathsf{ec.issue}
interface on input a request identifier
\mathit{reqid}
if that authority did not receive a request associated with
\mathit{reqid}
. Similar abortion conditions are listed when
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{EC}}
receives a message from the simulator
\mathcal{S}
.

Session identifier.:

The session identifier
\mathit{sid}
has the structure
(\mathcal{V}_{1},\allowbreak\ldots,\allowbreak\mathcal{V}_{n},\allowbreak\mathit{sid}^{\prime})
. This allows any authorities
(\mathcal{V}_{1},\allowbreak\ldots,\allowbreak\mathcal{V}_{n})
to create an instance of
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{EC}}
. After the first invocation of
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{EC}}
,
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{EC}}
implicitly checks that the session identifier in a message is equal to the one received in the first invocation.

Query identifiers.:

Before
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{EC}}
queries
\mathcal{S}
,
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{EC}}
saves its state, which is recovered when receiving a response from
\mathcal{S}
. If an interface, e.g.
\mathsf{ec.request}
, can be invoked by a party more than once,
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{EC}}
creates a query identifier
\mathit{qid}
, which allows
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{EC}}
to match a query to
\mathcal{S}
to a response from
\mathcal{S}
. Creating
\mathit{qid}
is not necessary if an interface, such as
\mathsf{ec.setup}
, can be invoked only once by each authority, and the authority identifier is revealed to
\mathcal{S}
.

(1)

On input
(\mathsf{ec.setup.ini},\allowbreak\mathit{sid})
from an authority
\mathcal{V}_{i}
:

•

Abort if
\mathit{sid}\neq(\mathcal{V}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{V}_{n},\mathit{sid}^{\prime})
, or if
\mathcal{V}_{i}\notin\mathit{sid}
, or if
n<t
, or if
(\mathit{sid},\mathcal{V}_{i},0)
is already stored.

•

Store
(\mathit{sid},\mathcal{V}_{i},0)
and send
(\mathsf{ec.setup.sim},\allowbreak\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathcal{V}_{i})
to
\mathcal{S}
.

S.

On input
(\mathsf{ec.setup.rep},\allowbreak\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathcal{V}_{i})
from
\mathcal{S}
:

•

Abort if
(\mathit{sid},\mathcal{V}_{i},0)
is not stored or if
(\mathit{sid},\mathcal{V}_{i},1)
is already stored.

•

Store
(\mathit{sid},\mathcal{V}_{i},1)
and send
(\mathsf{ec.setup.end},\allowbreak\mathit{sid})
to
\mathcal{V}_{i}
.

(2)

On input
(\mathsf{ec.register.ini},\allowbreak\mathit{sid})
from user
\mathcal{U}_{j}
or provider
\mathcal{P}_{k}
:

•

Abort if there is a tuple
(\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathcal{U}_{j},\allowbreak 0)
stored.

•

Store
(\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathcal{U}_{j},\allowbreak 0)
and send
(\mathsf{ec.register.sim},\allowbreak\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathcal{U}_{j})
to
\mathcal{S}
.

S.

On input
(\mathsf{ec.register.rep},\allowbreak\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathcal{U}_{j})
from the simulator
\mathcal{S}
:

•

Abort if
(\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathcal{U}_{j},\allowbreak 0)
is not stored or if
(\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathcal{U}_{j},\allowbreak 1)
is stored.

•

Store
(\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathcal{U}_{j},\allowbreak 1)
and send
(\mathsf{ec.register.end},\allowbreak\mathit{sid})
to
\mathcal{U}_{j}
.

(3)

On input
(\mathsf{ec.request.ini},\allowbreak\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathcal{V}_{i},\allowbreak\mathit{reqid},\allowbreak\mathit{wn})
from user
\mathcal{U}_{j}
:

•

Abort if
\mathit{sid}\allowbreak\neq\allowbreak(\mathcal{V}_{1},\allowbreak\ldots,\allowbreak\mathcal{V}_{n},\allowbreak\mathit{sid}^{\prime})
, or if
\mathcal{V}_{i}\allowbreak\notin\allowbreak\mathit{sid}
, or if
n<t
, or if
\mathit{reqid}\allowbreak\notin\allowbreak\mathit{U_{req}}
.

•

Abort if
(\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathcal{U}_{j},\allowbreak 1)
is not stored, or if
(\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathcal{U}^{\prime}_{j},\allowbreak\mathcal{V}^{\prime}_{i},\allowbreak\mathit{reqid}^{\prime},\allowbreak\mathit{wn},\allowbreak\mathrm{user})
stored such that
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}_{j}\allowbreak=\allowbreak\mathcal{U}_{j}
,
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}_{i}\allowbreak=\allowbreak\mathcal{V}_{i}
and
\mathit{reqid}^{\prime}=\mathit{reqid}
.

•

Store
(\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathcal{U}_{j},\allowbreak\mathcal{V}_{i},\allowbreak\mathit{reqid},\allowbreak\mathit{wn},\allowbreak\mathrm{user})
.

•

If
(\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathcal{U}^{\prime}_{j},\allowbreak\mathit{wct},\allowbreak\mathsf{DB})
such that
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}_{j}\allowbreak=\allowbreak\mathcal{U}_{j}
is not stored, store
(\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathcal{U}_{j},\allowbreak\mathit{wct},\allowbreak\mathsf{DB})
, where
\mathit{wct}
is a counter of the number of wallets of
\mathcal{U}_{j}
initialized to
1
, and
\mathsf{DB}
is a (initially empty) database.

•

If
\mathit{wn}\allowbreak\notin\allowbreak[1,\allowbreak\mathit{wct}]
, set
\mathit{wct}\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak\mathit{wct}+\allowbreak 1
, set
\mathit{wn}\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak\mathit{wct}
, pick random
\mathit{wid}\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak\mathit{U_{w}}
, and store an entry
[\mathit{wn},\allowbreak\mathit{wid},0,\allowbreak\emptyset]
in
\mathsf{DB}
. Update
\mathit{wct}
and
\mathsf{DB}
in the tuple
(\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathcal{U}_{j},\allowbreak\mathit{wct},\allowbreak\mathsf{DB})
.

•

Create a fresh
\mathit{qid}
and store
(\mathit{qid},\allowbreak\mathcal{U}_{j},\allowbreak\mathcal{V}_{i},\allowbreak\mathit{reqid},\allowbreak\mathit{wn})
.

•

If
\mathcal{V}_{i}
is honest, send
(\mathsf{ec.request.sim},\allowbreak\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{qid},\allowbreak\mathcal{U}_{j},\allowbreak\mathcal{V}_{i})
to the simulator
\mathcal{S}
, else pick
\mathit{wid}
from the entry
[\mathit{wn}^{\prime},\allowbreak\mathit{wid},0,\allowbreak\emptyset]\allowbreak\in\allowbreak\mathsf{DB}
such that
\mathit{wn}^{\prime}\allowbreak=\allowbreak\mathit{wn}
and send
(\mathsf{ec.request.sim},\allowbreak\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{qid},\allowbreak\mathcal{U}_{j},\allowbreak\mathcal{V}_{i},\allowbreak\mathit{wid})
to the simulator
\mathcal{S}
.

S.

On input
(\mathsf{ec.request.rep},\allowbreak\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{qid})
from the simulator
\mathcal{S}
:

•

Abort if
(\mathit{qid}^{\prime},\allowbreak\mathcal{U}_{j},\allowbreak\mathcal{V}_{i},\mathit{reqid},\allowbreak\mathit{wn})
such that
\mathit{qid}^{\prime}\allowbreak=\allowbreak\mathit{qid}
is not stored, or if
(\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathcal{V}_{i},\allowbreak 1)
is not stored.

•

Store
(\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathcal{U}_{j},\allowbreak\mathcal{V}_{i},\allowbreak\mathit{reqid},\allowbreak\mathit{wn},\allowbreak\mathrm{authority})
, delete
(\mathit{qid},\allowbreak\mathcal{U}_{j},\allowbreak\mathcal{V}_{i},\allowbreak\mathit{reqid},\allowbreak\mathit{wn})
, and send
(\mathsf{ec.request.end},\allowbreak\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathcal{U}_{j},\allowbreak\mathit{reqid})
to
\mathcal{V}_{i}
.

(4)

On input
(\mathsf{ec.issue.ini},\allowbreak\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathcal{U}_{j},\allowbreak\mathit{reqid})
from an authority
\mathcal{V}_{i}
:

•

Abort if a tuple
(\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathcal{U}^{\prime}_{j},\allowbreak\mathcal{V}^{\prime}_{i},\allowbreak\mathit{reqid}^{\prime},\allowbreak\mathit{wn},\allowbreak\mathrm{authority})
such that
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}_{j}\allowbreak=\allowbreak\mathcal{U}_{j}
,
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}_{i}\allowbreak=\allowbreak\mathcal{V}_{i}
and
\mathit{reqid}^{\prime}\allowbreak=\allowbreak\mathit{reqid}
is not stored.

•

Create a fresh
\mathit{qid}
, store
(\mathit{qid},\allowbreak\mathcal{U}_{j},\allowbreak\mathcal{V}_{i},\allowbreak\mathit{reqid},\allowbreak\mathit{wn},\allowbreak\mathrm{issue})
and delete
(\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathcal{U}_{j},\allowbreak\mathcal{V}_{i},\allowbreak\mathit{reqid},\allowbreak\mathit{wn},\allowbreak\mathrm{authority})
.

•

Send
(\mathsf{ec.issue.sim},\allowbreak\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{qid},\allowbreak\mathcal{V}_{i},\allowbreak\mathcal{U}_{j})
to the simulator
\mathcal{S}
.

S.

On input
(\mathsf{ec.issue.rep},\allowbreak\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{qid})
from the simulator
\mathcal{S}
:

•

Abort if
(\mathit{qid}^{\prime},\allowbreak\mathcal{U}_{j},\allowbreak\mathcal{V}_{i},\allowbreak\mathit{reqid},\allowbreak\mathit{wn},\allowbreak\mathrm{issue})
such that
\mathit{qid}^{\prime}=\mathit{qid}
is not stored.

•

If
\mathcal{U}_{j}
or
\mathcal{V}_{i}
are honest, take the tuple
(\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathcal{U}^{\prime}_{j},\allowbreak\mathit{wct},\allowbreak\mathsf{DB})
such that
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}_{j}\allowbreak=\allowbreak\mathcal{U}_{j}
, and replace the entry
[\mathit{wn}^{\prime},\allowbreak\mathit{wid},\allowbreak l,\allowbreak\mathbb{V}]
in
\mathsf{DB}
such that
\mathit{wn}^{\prime}=\mathit{wn}
by
[\mathit{wn}^{\prime},\allowbreak\mathit{wid},\allowbreak l,\allowbreak\mathbb{V}\cup\{\mathcal{V}_{i}\}]
.

•

Delete
(\mathit{qid},\allowbreak\mathcal{U}_{j},\allowbreak\mathcal{V}_{i},\allowbreak\mathit{reqid},\allowbreak\mathit{wn},\allowbreak\mathrm{issue})
, and delete
(\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathcal{U}^{\prime}_{j},\allowbreak\mathcal{V}^{\prime}_{i},\allowbreak\mathit{reqid}^{\prime},\allowbreak\mathit{wn},\allowbreak\mathrm{user})
such that
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}_{j}\allowbreak=\allowbreak\mathcal{U}_{j}
,
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}_{i}\allowbreak=\allowbreak\mathcal{V}_{i}
and
\mathit{reqid}^{\prime}=\mathit{reqid}
.

•

Send
(\mathsf{ec.issue.end},\allowbreak\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{reqid},\allowbreak\mathcal{V}_{i})
to
\mathcal{U}_{j}
.

(5)

On input
(\mathsf{ec.spend.ini},\allowbreak\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{wn},\allowbreak V,\allowbreak\mathit{\mathbb{D}},\allowbreak\mathit{payinfo},\allowbreak\mathit{P},\allowbreak\mathcal{P}_{k})
from
\mathcal{U}_{j}
:

•

Abort if
\mathit{P}\allowbreak\notin\allowbreak\mathbb{U}_{p}
, or if
\mathit{payinfo}\allowbreak\notin\allowbreak\mathit{U_{info}}
, or if
\mathit{payinfo}
does not contain
\mathcal{P}_{k}
, or if
(\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathcal{U}^{\prime}_{j},\allowbreak\mathit{wct},\allowbreak\mathsf{DB})
such that
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}_{j}\allowbreak=\allowbreak\mathcal{U}_{j}
is not stored.

•

Abort if there is not an entry
[\mathit{wn}^{\prime},\allowbreak\mathit{wid},\allowbreak l,\allowbreak\mathbb{V}]\allowbreak\in\allowbreak\mathsf{DB}
such that
\mathit{wn}^{\prime}\allowbreak=\allowbreak\mathit{wn}
. Else, proceed as follows:

–

Abort if
\mathcal{U}_{j}
is honest and either
V\notin[1,L]
or
l\allowbreak+\allowbreak V\allowbreak>\allowbreak L
.

–

Abort if
\mathcal{U}_{j}
is corrupt and
\mathit{\mathbb{D}}\notin[1,L]
. Else overwrite
V\leftarrow|\mathit{\mathbb{D}}|
.

–

Abort if
|\mathbb{V}|\allowbreak<\allowbreak t^{\prime}
, where
t^{\prime}\allowbreak=\allowbreak t
, if
\mathcal{U}_{j}
is honest, or
t^{\prime}\allowbreak=\allowbreak t-\allowbreak\tilde{t}
, if
\mathcal{U}_{j}
is corrupt and there are
\tilde{t}
corrupt authorities.

•

If
\mathcal{U}_{j}
is honest, update
[\mathit{wn},\allowbreak\mathit{wid},\allowbreak l,\allowbreak\mathbb{V}]
to
[\mathit{wn},\allowbreak\mathit{wid},\allowbreak l+V,\allowbreak\mathbb{V}]
.

•

Create a fresh
\mathit{qid}
and store
(\mathit{qid},\allowbreak\mathcal{U}_{j},\allowbreak\mathit{wn},\allowbreak V,\allowbreak\mathit{\mathbb{D}},\allowbreak\mathit{payinfo},\allowbreak\mathit{P},\allowbreak\mathcal{P}_{k})
.

•

Send
(\mathsf{ec.spend.sim},\allowbreak\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{qid})
to
\mathcal{S}
.

S.

On input
(\mathsf{ec.spend.rep},\allowbreak\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{qid})
from
\mathcal{S}
:

•

Abort if
(\mathit{qid}^{\prime},\allowbreak\mathcal{U}_{j},\allowbreak\mathit{wn},\allowbreak V,\allowbreak\mathit{\mathbb{D}},\allowbreak\mathit{payinfo},\allowbreak\mathit{P},\allowbreak\mathcal{P}_{k})
such that
\mathit{qid}^{\prime}\allowbreak=\allowbreak\mathit{qid}
is not stored and if
(\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{k},\allowbreak 1)
such that
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{k}\allowbreak=\allowbreak\mathcal{P}_{k}
is not stored.

•

Create a random unique payment identifier
\mathit{payid}
and store
(\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{payid},\allowbreak\mathcal{U}_{j},\allowbreak\mathit{wn},\allowbreak V,\allowbreak\mathit{\mathbb{D}},\allowbreak\mathit{payinfo},\allowbreak\mathit{P},\allowbreak\mathcal{P}_{k},\allowbreak 0)
.

•

Delete
(\mathit{qid},\allowbreak\mathcal{U}_{j},\allowbreak\mathit{wn},\allowbreak V,\allowbreak\mathit{\mathbb{D}},\allowbreak\mathit{payinfo},\allowbreak\mathit{P},\allowbreak\mathcal{P}_{k})
.

•

Send
(\mathsf{ec.spend.end},\allowbreak\mathit{payid},\allowbreak V,\allowbreak\mathit{payinfo},\allowbreak\mathit{P})
to
\mathcal{P}_{k}
.

(6)

On input
(\mathsf{ec.deposit.ini},\allowbreak\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{payid})
from a provider
\mathcal{P}_{k}
:

•

Abort if a tuple
(\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{payid}^{\prime},\allowbreak\mathcal{U}_{j},\allowbreak\mathit{wn},\allowbreak V,\allowbreak\mathit{\mathbb{D}},\allowbreak\mathit{payinfo},\allowbreak\mathit{P},\allowbreak\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{k},\allowbreak b)
such that
\mathit{payid}^{\prime}\allowbreak=\allowbreak\mathit{payid}
,
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{k}\allowbreak=\allowbreak\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{k}
and
b\allowbreak=\allowbreak 0
is not stored.

•

Create a fresh
\mathit{qid}
and store
(\mathit{qid},\allowbreak\mathit{payid},\allowbreak\mathcal{P}_{k})
.

•

If
\mathcal{U}_{j}
is corrupt and there is at least one corrupt authority, send
(\mathsf{ec.deposit.sim},\allowbreak\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{qid},\allowbreak\mathcal{U}_{j},\allowbreak\mathit{wn},\allowbreak V,\allowbreak\mathit{\mathbb{D}},\allowbreak\mathit{payinfo},\allowbreak\mathit{P},\allowbreak\mathcal{P}_{k})
to
\mathcal{S}
. Else if
\mathcal{U}_{j}
is honest and there is at least one corrupt authority, send
(\mathsf{ec.deposit.sim},\allowbreak\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{qid},\allowbreak V,\allowbreak\mathit{payinfo})
to
\mathcal{S}
. Else, send
(\mathsf{ec.deposit.sim},\allowbreak\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{qid})
to
\mathcal{S}
.

S.

On input
(\mathsf{ec.deposit.rep},\allowbreak\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{qid})
from
\mathcal{S}
:

•

Abort if a tuple
(\mathit{qid}^{\prime},\allowbreak\mathit{payid},\allowbreak\mathcal{P}_{k})
such that
\mathit{qid}^{\prime}\allowbreak=\allowbreak\mathit{qid}
is not stored.

•

Update the stored tuple
(\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{payid}^{\prime},\allowbreak\mathcal{U}_{j},\allowbreak\mathit{wn},\allowbreak V,\allowbreak\mathit{\mathbb{D}},\allowbreak\mathit{payinfo},\allowbreak\mathit{P},\allowbreak\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{k},\allowbreak b)
such that
\mathit{payid}^{\prime}\allowbreak=\allowbreak\mathit{payid}
,
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{k}\allowbreak=\allowbreak\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{k}
and
b\allowbreak=\allowbreak 0
to contain
b=1
.

•

Delete
(\mathit{qid},\allowbreak\mathit{payid},\allowbreak\mathcal{P}_{k})
.

•

Send
(\mathsf{ec.deposit.end},\allowbreak\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{payid})
to
\mathcal{P}_{k}
.

(7)

On input
(\mathsf{ec.depvf.ini},\allowbreak\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{\mathbb{U}},\allowbreak\mathit{payinfo})
from an authority
\mathcal{V}_{i}
:

•

Abort if
(\mathit{sid},\mathcal{V}^{\prime}_{i},1)
such that
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}_{i}\allowbreak=\allowbreak\mathcal{V}_{i}
is not stored or if there exists
\mathcal{U}_{j}\allowbreak\in\allowbreak\mathit{\mathbb{U}}
such that a tuple
(\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathcal{U}_{j},\allowbreak 1)
is not stored.

•

Create a fresh
\mathit{qid}
and store
(\mathit{qid},\allowbreak\mathit{\mathbb{U}},\allowbreak\mathit{payinfo},\allowbreak\mathcal{V}_{i})
.

•

Send
(\mathsf{ec.depvf.sim},\allowbreak\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{qid},\allowbreak\mathit{\mathbb{U}}^{\prime},\allowbreak\mathit{d})
to
\mathcal{S}
.
\mathit{\mathbb{U}}^{\prime}
is the set of users
\mathcal{U}_{j}\allowbreak\in\allowbreak\mathit{\mathbb{U}}
such that
\mathcal{U}_{j}
did not send a request to
\mathcal{V}_{i}
and
\mathcal{U}_{j}
was not received previously as input by
\mathcal{V}_{i}
in another set
\mathit{\mathbb{U}}
.
\mathit{d}
is the number of payments deposited since
\mathsf{ec.depvf}
was last invoked by
\mathcal{V}_{i}
.

S.

On input
(\mathsf{ec.depvf.rep},\allowbreak\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{qid})
from
\mathcal{S}
:

•

Abort if
(\mathit{qid}^{\prime},\allowbreak\mathit{\mathbb{U}},\allowbreak\mathit{payinfo},\allowbreak\mathcal{V}_{i})
such that
\mathit{qid}^{\prime}\allowbreak=\allowbreak\mathit{qid}
is not stored.

•

If there is no tuple
(\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{payid},\allowbreak\mathcal{U}_{j},\allowbreak\mathit{wn},\allowbreak V,\allowbreak\mathit{\mathbb{D}},\allowbreak\mathit{payinfo}^{\prime},\allowbreak\mathit{P},\allowbreak\mathcal{P}_{k},\allowbreak b)
such that
\mathit{payinfo}^{\prime}=\mathit{payinfo}
and
b=1
, set
c\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak 0
.

•

If there are
K>1
tuples
(\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{payid},\allowbreak\mathcal{U}_{j},\allowbreak\mathit{wn},\allowbreak V,\allowbreak\mathit{\mathbb{D}},\allowbreak\mathit{payinfo}^{\prime},\allowbreak\mathit{P},\allowbreak\mathcal{P}_{k},\allowbreak b)
such that
\mathit{payinfo}^{\prime}=\mathit{payinfo}
and
b=1
, set
c\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak(\mathcal{P}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{P}_{K})
, where
(\mathcal{P}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{P}_{K})
are the provider identities such that either
\mathcal{P}_{k}
is not included in
\mathit{payinfo}
, or there are two or more tuples deposited by
\mathcal{P}_{k}
.

•

If there is 1 tuple
(\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{payid},\allowbreak\mathcal{U}_{j},\allowbreak\mathit{wn},\allowbreak V,\allowbreak\mathit{\mathbb{D}},\allowbreak\mathit{payinfo}^{\prime},\allowbreak\mathit{P},\allowbreak\mathcal{P}_{k},\allowbreak b)
such that
\mathit{payinfo}^{\prime}=\mathit{payinfo}
and
b=1
, proceed as follows:

–

If
\mathcal{P}_{k}
is not in
\mathit{payinfo}
, output
c\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak\mathcal{P}_{k}
.

–

Else, if
\mathcal{U}_{j}
is honest, set
c\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak 1
.

–

Else, if
\mathcal{U}_{j}
is corrupt, check if there are other tuples
(\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{payid},\allowbreak\mathcal{U}^{\prime}_{j},\allowbreak\mathit{wn}^{\prime},\allowbreak V,\allowbreak\mathit{\mathbb{D}}^{\prime},\allowbreak\mathit{payinfo},\allowbreak\mathit{P},\allowbreak\mathcal{P}_{k},\allowbreak b)
such that
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}_{j}\allowbreak=\allowbreak\mathcal{U}_{j}
,
\mathit{wn}^{\prime}\allowbreak=\allowbreak\mathit{wn}^{\prime}
and
b\allowbreak=\allowbreak 1
. For all such tuples, check if
\mathit{\mathbb{D}}^{\prime}\cap\mathit{\mathbb{D}}=\emptyset
. If that is the case for all tuples, set
c\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak 1
. Else, if
\mathcal{U}_{j}\allowbreak\in\allowbreak\mathit{\mathbb{U}}
, set
c\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak\mathcal{U}_{j}
, else set
c\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak\bot
.

•

Delete
(\mathit{qid},\allowbreak\mathit{\mathbb{U}},\allowbreak\mathit{payinfo},\allowbreak\mathcal{V}_{i})
.

•

Send
(\mathsf{ec.depvf.end},\allowbreak\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{payinfo},\allowbreak c)
to
\mathcal{V}_{i}
.

Figure 1.
Ideal Functionality
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{EC}}

Description of
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{EC}}
.

In the following, we explain how each of the interfaces of
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{EC}}
works:

1

An authority
\mathcal{V}_{i}
uses the
\mathsf{ec.setup}
interface to set up
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{EC}}
.
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{EC}}
stores the fact that
\mathcal{V}_{i}
has run the setup interface and enforces that each authority runs the setup interface only once. The simulator
\mathcal{S}
is allowed to learn that
\mathcal{V}_{i}
has run the setup interface.
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{EC}}
allows each authority
\mathcal{V}_{i}
to run the setup interface independently of other authorities, i.e. the execution of the setup interface for one authority can be finalized without the involvement of other authorities. Therefore,
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{EC}}
is realizable by protocols where authorities run the setup interface independently of each other. For example, protocols where each authority creates its own keys, or protocols where authorities obtain their keys from a trusted third party that generates them.
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{EC}}
can be modified so that it is realizable by protocols in which the setup interface requires interaction between authorities, e.g. protocols that use a distributed key generation protocol as a building block.

2

A user
\mathcal{U}_{j}
or a provider
\mathcal{P}_{k}
use the
\mathsf{ec.register}
interface to register.
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{EC}}
stores the fact that
\mathcal{U}_{j}
or
\mathcal{P}_{k}
has registered, and enforces that each user or provider runs the registration interface only once. The simulator
\mathcal{S}
is allowed to learn that
\mathcal{U}_{j}
or
\mathcal{P}_{k}
has run the setup interface.

3

A user
\mathcal{U}_{j}
runs the
\mathsf{ec.request}
interface given an authority identifier
\mathcal{V}_{i}
, a request identifier
\mathit{reqid}
and a wallet number
\mathit{wn}
. The request identifier
\mathit{reqid}
is used to bind a request to its subsequent issuance, while the wallet number
\mathit{wn}
is used to associate the request to a wallet.
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{EC}}
checks that the user has run the registration interface, and that there is not a request pending from
\mathcal{U}_{j}
to
\mathcal{V}_{i}
with the same identifier. Then, if a database for
\mathcal{U}_{j}
is not stored,
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{EC}}
stores a tuple
(\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathcal{U}_{j},\allowbreak\mathit{wct},\allowbreak\mathsf{DB})
, where
\mathit{wct}
is a counter of the number of wallets of
\mathcal{U}_{j}
initialized to
1
, and
\mathsf{DB}
is a (initially empty) database.
\mathsf{DB}
has entries of the form
[\mathit{wn},\allowbreak\mathit{wid},\allowbreak l,\allowbreak\mathbb{V}]
, where
\mathit{wn}
is the wallet number,
\mathit{wid}
is a wallet identifier,
l
is the number of coins spent from the wallet
\mathit{wn}
, and
\mathbb{V}
is the set of authorities that issued requests for the creation of this wallet.
If
\mathit{wn}
received as input is such that
\mathit{wn}\allowbreak\notin\allowbreak[1,\mathit{wct}]
, then
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{EC}}
increments
\mathit{wct}
, sets
\mathit{wn}\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak\mathit{wct}
, picks a random wallet identifier
\mathit{wid}
and stores a new entry
[\mathit{wn},\allowbreak\mathit{wid},\allowbreak 0,\allowbreak\emptyset]
in the database for
\mathcal{U}_{j}
. The reason for creating
\mathit{wid}
is that, in our e-cash schemes, requests for the same wallet can be linked to each other if authorities communicate with each other. Therefore, when
\mathcal{V}_{i}
is dishonest,
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{EC}}
leaks
\mathit{wid}
to the simulator
\mathcal{S}
.
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{EC}}
does not leak
\mathit{wn}
because dishonest authorities do not necessarily learn how many wallets a user requests.

After being prompted by the simulator
\mathcal{S}
,
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{EC}}
checks that
\mathcal{V}_{i}
has run the setup interface, records that
\mathcal{U}_{j}
sends a request to
\mathcal{V}_{i}
with identifier
\mathit{reqid}
for wallet
\mathit{wn}
, and sends
\mathcal{U}_{j}
and
\mathit{reqid}
to
\mathcal{V}_{i}
.

4

An authority
\mathcal{V}_{i}
runs the
\mathsf{ec.issue}
interface given a user identifier
\mathcal{U}_{j}
and a request identifier
\mathit{reqid}
.
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{EC}}
checks whether there is a request identifier
\mathit{reqid}
pending for a request from
\mathcal{U}_{j}
to
\mathcal{V}_{i}
. In that case, after being prompted by the simulator
\mathcal{S}
,
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{EC}}
records that
\mathcal{V}_{i}
has run the issuance for the request
\mathit{reqid}
. Concretely,
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{EC}}
updates the entry
[\mathit{wn},\allowbreak\mathit{wid},\allowbreak l,\allowbreak\mathbb{V}]
in
\mathsf{DB}
such that
\mathit{wn}
is the wallet number associated with
\mathit{reqid}
to contain
\mathcal{V}_{i}
in the set
\mathbb{V}
. Finally,
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{EC}}
informs
\mathcal{U}_{j}
that
\mathcal{V}_{i}
has run the issuance for the request
\mathit{reqid}
.

5

A user
\mathcal{U}_{j}
runs the
\mathsf{ec.spend}
interface given a wallet number
\mathit{wn}
, a number
V
of coins to be spent, a set of coin indices
\mathit{\mathbb{D}}
, payment information
\mathit{payinfo}
, a pseudonym
\mathit{P}
and a provider identifier
\mathcal{P}_{k}
.
The payment information
\mathit{payinfo}
should be unique for each spending, but
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{EC}}
checks that later when verifying a deposit. If
\mathcal{U}_{j}
is honest,
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{EC}}
checks that there are enough non-spent coins related to
\mathit{wn}
(
l+V\leq L
, where
l
is in the entry
[\mathit{wn},\allowbreak\mathit{wid},\allowbreak l,\allowbreak\mathbb{V}]\allowbreak\in\allowbreak\mathsf{DB})
and in that case adds
V
to the number of spent coins. In contrast, if
\mathcal{U}_{j}
is corrupt,
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{EC}}
records that the coins with indices
\mathit{\mathbb{D}}
are spent, regardless of whether they were spent before or not.
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{EC}}
also checks that enough authorities have issued the wallet
\mathit{wn}
to
\mathcal{U}_{j}
. In that case, after being prompted by the simulator,
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{EC}}
checks that
\mathcal{P}_{k}
has run the registration interface and creates a payment identifier
\mathit{payid}
to store the information related to this spending. Finally
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{EC}}
sends
\mathit{payid}
,
V
,
\mathit{payinfo}
and
\mathit{P}
to
\mathcal{P}_{k}
. We remark that
\mathcal{P}_{k}
does not learn
\mathcal{U}_{j}
, and
\mathit{P}
can be different for each spending so that spendings by
\mathcal{U}_{j}
are unlinkable to each other and to the request phase.

6

A provider
\mathcal{P}_{k}
runs the
\mathsf{ec.deposit}
interface on input a payment identifier
\mathit{payid}
. If there is a payment with identifier
\mathit{payid}
related to
\mathcal{P}_{k}
that is not deposited,
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{EC}}
proceeds to deposit it. If no authorities are corrupt,
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{EC}}
does not leak to
\mathcal{S}
any information about the deposited payment. However, if at least one authority is corrupt and the user that computed the payment is corrupt,
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{EC}}
leaks the full information about the payment. If at least one authority is corrupt but the user that computed the payment is honest, the authority leaks the number
V
of coins spent and the payment information
\mathit{payinfo}
. After being prompted by the simulator,
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{EC}}
marks that payment as deposited and informs
\mathcal{P}_{k}
that the payment has been deposited.

7

An honest authority
\mathcal{V}_{i}
runs the
\mathsf{ec.depvf}
interface given a list of user identifiers
\mathit{\mathbb{U}}
and payment information
\mathit{payinfo}
.
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{EC}}
checks that
\mathcal{V}_{i}
has run the setup interface and that all the users in
\mathit{\mathbb{U}}
have run the registration interface.
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{EC}}
leaks to the simulator the identities of those users in
\mathit{\mathbb{U}}
that were unknown by
\mathcal{V}_{i}
. This is done because, in our protocol,
\mathcal{V}_{i}
needs to retrieve the public key for that user, and the adversary learns that.
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{EC}}
also leaks the number of deposits that were made since the last time
\mathcal{V}_{i}
run the deposit verification interface. This is done because, in our protocol, the authority needs to read the new deposits from the bulletin board, and the adversary learns that.

After being prompted by the simulator,
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{EC}}
checks the deposited payment with payment information
\mathit{payinfo}
. If no such deposited payment exists,
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{EC}}
sets
c\leftarrow 0
. If there is more than one deposited payment with
\mathit{payinfo}
,
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{EC}}
sets
c
to contain the identifiers of the provider(s) that deposited those payments more than once, or just once if the identity of the provider is not in
\mathit{payinfo}
. If there is 1 such tuple,
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{EC}}
sets
c
to the identity of the provider that deposited the payment if that identity is not in
\mathit{payinfo}
. Else,
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{EC}}
sets
c\leftarrow 1
if the user that made a payment with
\mathit{payinfo}
is honest, which means that there has not been a double spending. If the user is corrupt,
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{EC}}
checks whether there are deposited payments where the coins spent in the payment related with
\mathit{payinfo}
have also been spent. If that is not the case,
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{EC}}
sets
c\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak 1
. Else, if the user identifier is in
\mathit{\mathbb{U}}
,
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{EC}}
sets
c
to the identifier of the user that double spent, and otherwise sets
c\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak\bot
, which indicates that double spending has been detected but the user has not been identified.
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{EC}}
sends
c
along with
\mathit{payinfo}
to
\mathcal{V}_{i}
.

Security properties.

We now argue that
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{EC}}
guarantees the security properties
defined in §
3.2
.

Traceability.:

In the
\mathsf{ec.spend}
interface, when a user wishes to spend coins in the wallet
\mathit{wn}
,
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{EC}}
checks that the user has been issued that wallet by at least
t-\tilde{t}
authorities. This guarantees that users cannot forge wallets. Moreover, in the
\mathsf{ec.depvf}
interface,
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{EC}}
guarantees that, if coins were double spent, the user is identified whenever the user identifier is included in the set
\mathit{\mathbb{U}}
.
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{EC}}
also finds providers guilty of wrongly depositing a payment, either when they deposit a payment with the same
\mathit{payinfo}
more than once, or when they deposit a payment with
\mathit{payinfo}
that does not contain the provider’s identity.

Unlinkability.:

In the
\mathsf{ec.spend}
interface, the user identity is not revealed to the provider. The provider only receives a pseudonym, which can be different at each spending. This guarantees that payments from the same user cannot be linked with each other or to withdrawals by that user.

Exculpability.:

\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{EC}}
never finds an honest user guilty of double spending. Therefore, any protocol that realizes
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{EC}}
must guarantee that.

Clearance.:

In the
\mathsf{ec.depvf}
interface,
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{EC}}
never accepts a deposit as valid if the identity of the provider that made the deposit is not contained in
\mathit{payinfo}
.

We remark that
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{EC}}
does not take into account the case where
t
or more authorities are corrupt. We will analyze the security of construction
\mathrm{\Pi}_{\mathrm{EC}}
under the assumption that at most
t-1
authorities are corrupt.

4.
Construction
\mathrm{\Pi}_{\mathrm{EC}}

(1)

On input
(\mathsf{ec.setup.ini},\allowbreak\mathit{sid})
,
\mathcal{V}_{i}
does the following:

•

Abort if
\mathit{sid}\neq(\mathcal{V}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{V}_{n},\mathit{sid}^{\prime})
, or if
\mathcal{V}_{i}\allowbreak\notin\allowbreak\mathit{sid}
, or if
n<t
.

•

Abort if
(\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{params},\allowbreak\mathit{pk},\allowbreak\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{V}_{i}},\allowbreak\mathit{pk}_{\mathcal{V}_{i}})
is already stored.

•

Send
(\mathsf{kg.getkey.ini},\allowbreak\mathit{sid})
to
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{KG}}
. In its first invocation,
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{KG}}
runs
\mathit{params}\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\mathit{k}},\allowbreak L)
and
(\mathit{pk},\allowbreak\langle\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{V}_{i}},\allowbreak\mathit{pk}_{\mathcal{V}_{i}}\rangle_{i\in[1,n]})\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak\mathsf{KeyGenV}(\mathit{params},\allowbreak t,\allowbreak n)
.
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{KG}}
sends
(\mathsf{kg.getkey.end},\allowbreak\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{params},\allowbreak\mathit{pk},\allowbreak\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{V}_{i}},\allowbreak\mathit{pk}_{\mathcal{V}_{i}})
to
\mathcal{V}_{i}
.

•

Store
(\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{params},\allowbreak\mathit{pk},\allowbreak\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{V}_{i}},\allowbreak\mathit{pk}_{\mathcal{V}_{i}})
and output
(\mathsf{ec.setup.end},\mathit{sid})
.

(2)

On input
(\mathsf{ec.register.ini},\mathit{sid})
,
\mathcal{U}_{j}
(or
\mathcal{P}_{k}
) does the following:

•

Abort if
(\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}},\allowbreak\mathit{pk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}})
is already stored.

•

Send
(\mathsf{kg.retrieve.ini},\allowbreak\mathit{sid})
to
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{KG}}
.
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{KG}}
sends
(\mathsf{kg.retrieve.end},\allowbreak\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{v})
. If
\mathit{v}=(\mathit{params},\allowbreak\mathit{pk},\allowbreak\langle\mathit{pk}_{\mathcal{V}_{i}}\rangle_{i\in[1,n]})
, store
(\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{params},\allowbreak\mathit{pk},\allowbreak\langle\mathit{pk}_{\mathcal{V}_{i}}\rangle_{i\in[1,n]})
, else abort.

•

Run
(\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}},\allowbreak\mathit{pk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}})\leftarrow\mathsf{KeyGenU}(\mathit{params})
.

•

Set
\mathit{sid}_{\mathrm{REG}}\leftarrow(\mathcal{U}_{j},\mathit{sid}^{\prime})
and send
(\mathsf{reg.register.ini},\allowbreak\mathit{sid}_{\mathrm{REG}},\allowbreak\mathit{pk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}})
to
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{REG}}
.
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{REG}}
sends
(\mathsf{reg.register.end},\allowbreak\mathit{sid}_{\mathrm{REG}})
to
\mathcal{U}_{j}
.

•

Store
(\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}},\allowbreak\mathit{pk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}})
and output
(\mathsf{ec.register.end},\allowbreak\mathit{sid})
.

(3)

On input
(\mathsf{ec.request.ini},\allowbreak\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathcal{V}_{i},\allowbreak\mathit{reqid},\allowbreak\mathit{wn})
,
\mathcal{U}_{j}
and
\mathcal{V}_{i}
do:

•

\mathcal{U}_{j}
aborts if
\mathit{sid}\allowbreak\neq\allowbreak(\mathcal{V}_{1},\allowbreak\ldots,\allowbreak\mathcal{V}_{n},\allowbreak\mathit{sid}^{\prime})
, or if
\mathcal{V}_{i}\allowbreak\notin\allowbreak\mathit{sid}
, or if
n\allowbreak<\allowbreak t
, or if
\mathit{reqid}\allowbreak\notin\allowbreak\mathit{U_{req}}
, or if
(\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}},\allowbreak\mathit{pk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}})
is not stored, or if there is
(\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathcal{V}^{\prime}_{i},\allowbreak\mathit{reqid}^{\prime},\allowbreak\mathit{wn})
such that
\mathit{reqid}^{\prime}\allowbreak=\allowbreak\mathit{reqid}
and
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}_{i}\allowbreak=\allowbreak\mathcal{V}_{i}
.

•

If there is not a tuple
(\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{wct})
,
\mathcal{U}_{j}
stores
(\mathit{sid},\allowbreak 0)
.

•

If
\mathit{wn}\allowbreak\notin\allowbreak[1,\allowbreak\mathit{wct}]
,
\mathcal{U}_{j}
sets
\mathit{wct}\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak\mathit{wct}\allowbreak+\allowbreak 1
, sets
\mathit{wn}\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak\mathit{wct}
, runs
(\mathit{req},\allowbreak\mathit{reqinfo})\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak\mathsf{Request}(\mathit{params},\allowbreak\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}})
, stores
(\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{wn},\allowbreak\mathit{req},\allowbreak\mathit{reqinfo})
and updates
(\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{wct})
.
\mathcal{U}_{j}
stores
(\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathcal{V}_{i},\allowbreak\mathit{reqid},\allowbreak\mathit{wn})
.

•

\mathcal{U}_{j}
sets
\mathit{sid}_{\mathrm{SMT}}\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak(\mathcal{U}_{j},\mathcal{V}_{i},\mathit{sid}^{\prime})
and sends
(\mathsf{smt.send.ini},\allowbreak\mathit{sid}_{\mathrm{SMT}},\allowbreak\langle\mathit{reqid},\allowbreak\mathit{req}\rangle)
to
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{SMT}}
.

•

\mathcal{V}_{i}
receives
(\mathsf{smt.send.end},\allowbreak\mathit{sid}_{\mathrm{SMT}},\allowbreak\langle\mathit{reqid},\allowbreak\mathit{req}\rangle)
from
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{SMT}}
.

•

\mathcal{V}_{i}
aborts if
(\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{params},\allowbreak\mathit{pk},\allowbreak\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{V}_{i}},\allowbreak\mathit{pk}_{\mathcal{V}_{i}})
is not stored.

•

\mathcal{V}_{i}
aborts if there is a tuple
(\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{reqid}^{\prime},\allowbreak\mathit{req},\allowbreak\mathcal{U}^{\prime}_{j})
stored such that
\mathit{reqid}^{\prime}\allowbreak=\allowbreak\mathit{reqid}
and
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}_{j}\allowbreak=\allowbreak\mathcal{U}_{j}
.

•

\mathcal{V}_{i}
parses
\mathit{sid}_{\mathrm{SMT}}
as
(\mathcal{U}_{j},\allowbreak\mathcal{V}_{i},\allowbreak\mathit{sid}^{\prime})
. If
(\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathcal{U}^{\prime}_{j},\mathit{pk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}})
such that
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}_{j}=\mathcal{U}_{j}
is not stored,
\mathcal{V}_{i}
does the following:

–

\mathcal{V}_{i}
sets
\mathit{sid}_{\mathrm{REG}}\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak(\mathcal{U}_{j},\allowbreak\mathit{sid}^{\prime})
and sends
(\mathsf{reg.retrieve.ini},\allowbreak\mathit{sid}_{\mathrm{REG}})
to
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{REG}}
.
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{REG}}
sends
(\mathsf{reg.retrieve.end},\allowbreak\mathit{sid}_{\mathrm{REG}},\allowbreak\mathit{pk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}})
to
\mathcal{V}_{i}
.

–

If
\mathit{pk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}}=\bot
,
\mathcal{V}_{i}
aborts, else
\mathcal{V}_{i}
stores
(\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathcal{U}_{j},\mathit{pk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}})
.

•

\mathcal{V}_{i}
runs
b\leftarrow\mathsf{RequestVf}(\mathit{params},\allowbreak\mathit{req},\allowbreak\mathit{pk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}})
. If
b\allowbreak=\allowbreak 0
,
\mathcal{V}_{i}
aborts, else
\mathcal{V}_{i}
stores
(\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{reqid},\allowbreak\mathit{req},\allowbreak\mathcal{U}_{j})
.

•

\mathcal{V}_{i}
outputs
(\mathsf{ec.request.end},\mathit{sid},\mathcal{U}_{j},\mathit{reqid})
.

(4)

On input
(\mathsf{ec.issue.ini},\mathit{sid},\mathcal{U}_{j},\mathit{reqid})
,
\mathcal{V}_{i}
and
\mathcal{U}_{j}
do the following:

•

\mathcal{V}_{i}
aborts if
(\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{reqid}^{\prime},\allowbreak\mathit{req},\allowbreak\mathcal{U}^{\prime}_{j})
such that
\mathit{reqid}^{\prime}\allowbreak=\allowbreak\mathit{reqid}
and
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}_{j}\allowbreak=\allowbreak\mathcal{U}_{j}
is not stored.

•

\mathcal{V}_{i}
runs
\mathit{res}\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak\mathsf{Issue}(\mathit{params},\allowbreak\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{V}_{i}},\allowbreak\mathit{req})
.

•

\mathcal{V}_{i}
deletes
(\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{reqid},\allowbreak\mathit{req},\allowbreak\mathcal{U}_{j})
, sets
\mathit{sid}_{\mathrm{SMT}}\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak(\mathcal{V}_{i},\allowbreak\mathcal{U}_{j},\allowbreak\mathit{sid}^{\prime})
and sends
(\mathsf{smt.send.ini},\allowbreak\mathit{sid}_{\mathrm{SMT}},\allowbreak\langle\mathit{reqid},\allowbreak\mathit{res}\rangle)
to
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{SMT}}
.

•

\mathcal{U}_{j}
receives
(\mathsf{smt.send.end},\allowbreak\mathit{sid}_{\mathrm{SMT}},\allowbreak\langle\mathit{reqid},\allowbreak\mathit{res}\rangle)
from
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{SMT}}
.

•

\mathcal{U}_{j}
parses
\mathit{sid}_{\mathrm{SMT}}
as
(\mathcal{V}_{i},\allowbreak\mathcal{U}_{j},\allowbreak\mathit{sid}^{\prime})
.
\mathcal{U}_{j}
aborts if a tuple
(\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathcal{V}^{\prime}_{i},\allowbreak\mathit{reqid}^{\prime},\allowbreak\mathit{wn})
such that
\mathit{reqid}^{\prime}\allowbreak=\allowbreak\mathit{reqid}
and
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}_{i}\allowbreak=\allowbreak\mathcal{V}^{\prime}_{i}
is not stored. Else
\mathcal{U}_{j}
takes the stored tuple
(\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{wn}^{\prime},\allowbreak\mathit{req},\allowbreak\mathit{reqinfo})
such that
\mathit{wn}^{\prime}=\mathit{wn}
and runs
\mathit{W}_{i}\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak\mathsf{IssueVf}(\mathit{params},\allowbreak\mathit{pk}_{\mathcal{V}_{i}},\allowbreak\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}},\allowbreak\mathit{res},\allowbreak\mathit{reqinfo})
. If
\mathit{W}_{i}\allowbreak=\allowbreak 0
,
\mathcal{U}_{j}
aborts.

•

If there is not a tuple
(\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{wn}^{\prime},\allowbreak\mathit{\mathbb{S}},\allowbreak\mathit{\mathbb{W}})
such that
\mathit{wn}^{\prime}\allowbreak=\allowbreak\mathit{wn}
,
\mathcal{U}_{j}
stores
(\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{wn},\allowbreak\emptyset,\allowbreak\emptyset)
.

•

\mathcal{U}_{j}
updates
(\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{wn},\allowbreak\mathit{\mathbb{S}},\allowbreak\mathit{\mathbb{W}})
to
(\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{wn},\allowbreak\mathit{\mathbb{S}}\cup\{i\},\allowbreak\mathit{\mathbb{W}}\cup\{\mathit{W}_{i}\})
.

•

If
(\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{wn}^{\prime},\allowbreak\mathit{W})
such that
\mathit{wn}^{\prime}\allowbreak=\allowbreak\mathit{wn}
is not stored, and if
|\mathit{\mathbb{S}}|\allowbreak\geq\allowbreak t
in the tuple
(\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{wn}^{\prime},\allowbreak\mathit{\mathbb{S}},\allowbreak\mathit{\mathbb{W}})
such that
\mathit{wn}^{\prime}\allowbreak=\allowbreak\mathit{wn}
, then
\mathcal{U}_{j}
runs
\mathit{W}\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak\mathsf{AggrWallet}(\mathit{pk},\allowbreak\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}},\allowbreak\mathit{\mathbb{S}},\allowbreak\mathit{\mathbb{W}})
and stores
(\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{wn},\allowbreak\mathit{W})
.

•

\mathcal{U}_{j}
deletes
(\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathcal{V}_{i},\allowbreak\mathit{reqid},\allowbreak\mathit{wn})
.

•

Output
(\mathsf{ec.issue.end},\allowbreak\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{reqid},\allowbreak\mathcal{V}_{i})
.

(5)

On input
(\mathsf{ec.spend.ini},\allowbreak\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{wn},\allowbreak V,\allowbreak\mathit{\mathbb{D}},\allowbreak\mathit{payinfo},\allowbreak\mathit{P},\mathcal{P}_{k})
,
\mathcal{U}_{j}
and
\mathcal{V}_{i}
do:

•

\mathcal{U}_{j}
aborts if
\mathit{P}\allowbreak\notin\allowbreak\mathbb{U}_{p}
, or if
\mathit{payinfo}\allowbreak\notin\allowbreak\mathit{U_{info}}
, or if
\mathit{payinfo}
does not contain
\mathcal{P}_{k}
, or if
(\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{wn}^{\prime},\allowbreak\mathit{W})
such that
\mathit{wn}^{\prime}\allowbreak=\allowbreak\mathit{wn}
is not stored, or if
V\allowbreak\notin\allowbreak[1,L]
.

•

\mathcal{U}_{j}
runs
b\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak\mathsf{Spend}(\mathit{pk},\allowbreak\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}},\allowbreak\mathit{W},\allowbreak\mathit{payinfo},\allowbreak V)
. If
b\allowbreak=\allowbreak 0
,
\mathcal{U}_{j}
aborts. Else
\mathcal{U}_{j}
parses
b
as
(\mathit{W}^{\prime},\allowbreak\mathit{pay})
and updates the stored tuple
(\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{wn},\allowbreak\mathit{W})
to
(\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{wn},\allowbreak\mathit{W}^{\prime})
.

•

\mathcal{U}_{j}
sends
(\mathsf{nym.send.ini},\allowbreak\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\langle\mathit{pay},\mathit{payinfo}\rangle,\allowbreak\mathit{P},\allowbreak\mathcal{P}_{k})
to
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{NYM}}
.

•

\mathcal{P}_{k}
receives
(\mathsf{nym.send.end},\allowbreak\mathit{sid},\langle\mathit{pay},\mathit{payinfo}\rangle,\allowbreak\mathit{P})
from
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{NYM}}
.

•

\mathcal{P}_{k}
aborts if
(\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{params},\allowbreak\mathit{pk},\allowbreak\langle\mathit{pk}_{\mathcal{V}_{i}}\rangle_{i\in[1,n]})
is not stored, or if
\mathit{payinfo}
does not contain
\mathcal{P}_{k}
.

•

\mathcal{P}_{k}
runs
b\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak\mathsf{SpendVf}(\mathit{pk},\allowbreak\mathit{pay},\allowbreak\mathit{payinfo})
. If
b\allowbreak=\allowbreak 0
,
\mathcal{P}_{k}
aborts, else
\mathcal{P}_{k}
sets
V\leftarrow b
, creates a random unique payment identifier
\mathit{payid}
and stores
(\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{payid},\allowbreak\mathit{pay},\allowbreak\mathit{payinfo},\allowbreak V,\allowbreak 0)
.

•

\mathcal{P}_{k}
outputs
(\mathsf{ec.spend.end},\allowbreak\mathit{payid},\allowbreak V,\allowbreak\mathit{payinfo},\allowbreak\mathit{P})
.

(6)

On input
(\mathsf{ec.deposit.ini},\allowbreak\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{payid})
,
\mathcal{P}_{k}
does the following:

•

Abort if a tuple
(\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{payid}^{\prime},\allowbreak\mathit{pay},\allowbreak\mathit{payinfo},\allowbreak V,\allowbreak b)
such that
\mathit{payid}^{\prime}\allowbreak=\allowbreak\mathit{payid}
and
b\allowbreak=\allowbreak 0
is not stored.

•

Send
(\mathsf{bb.write.ini},\allowbreak\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\langle\mathit{pay},\allowbreak\mathit{payinfo}\rangle)
to the functionality
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{BB}}
.
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{BB}}
sends
(\mathsf{bb.write.end},\allowbreak\mathit{sid})
to
\mathcal{P}_{k}
.

•

Update the tuple
(\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{payid},\allowbreak\mathit{pay},\allowbreak\mathit{payinfo},\allowbreak V,\allowbreak b)
so that
b\allowbreak=\allowbreak 1
.

•

Output
(\mathsf{ec.deposit.end},\allowbreak\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{payid})
.

(7)

On input
(\mathsf{ec.depvf.ini},\allowbreak\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{\mathbb{U}},\allowbreak\mathit{payinfo})
from an authority
\mathcal{V}_{i}
:

•

Abort if
(\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{params},\allowbreak\mathit{pk},\allowbreak\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{V}_{i}},\allowbreak\mathit{pk}_{\mathcal{V}_{i}})
is not stored.

•

For all
\mathcal{U}_{j}\allowbreak\in\allowbreak\mathit{\mathbb{U}}
, if
(\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathcal{U}^{\prime}_{j},\mathit{pk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}})
such that
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}_{j}\allowbreak=\allowbreak\mathcal{U}_{j}
is not stored, do the following:

–

Set
\mathit{sid}_{\mathrm{REG}}\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak(\mathcal{U}_{j},\allowbreak\mathit{sid}^{\prime})
and send
(\mathsf{reg.retrieve.ini},\allowbreak\mathit{sid}_{\mathrm{REG}})
to
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{REG}}
.
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{REG}}
sends back
(\mathsf{reg.retrieve.end},\allowbreak\mathit{sid}_{\mathrm{REG}},\allowbreak\mathit{pk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}})
.

–

If
\mathit{pk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}}\allowbreak=\allowbreak\bot
, abort, else store
(\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathcal{U}_{j},\mathit{pk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}})
.

•

Include in
\mathit{PK}
the public keys
\mathit{pk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}}
in all the stored tuples
(\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathcal{U}_{j},\allowbreak\mathit{pk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}})
such that
\mathcal{U}_{j}\allowbreak\in\allowbreak\mathit{\mathbb{U}}
.

•

If
(\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{i})
is not stored, set
\mathit{i}\allowbreak=\allowbreak 1
and store
(\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{i})
.

•

While
\mathit{m}^{\prime}\neq\bot
, do the following:

–

Send
(\mathsf{bb.read.ini},\allowbreak\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{i})
to
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{BB}}
.
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{BB}}
sends back
(\mathsf{bb.read.end},\allowbreak\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{m}^{\prime})
. If
\mathit{m}^{\prime}\allowbreak=\allowbreak(\mathcal{P}_{k},\mathit{pay},\allowbreak\mathit{payinfo})
, Store
(\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathcal{P}_{k},\mathit{pay},\allowbreak\mathit{payinfo})
.

–

Increment
\mathit{i}
.

•

Update
\mathit{i}
in the tuple
(\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{i})
.

•

Find all the stored tuples
(\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathcal{P}_{k},\mathit{pay},\allowbreak\mathit{payinfo}^{\prime})
such that
\mathit{payinfo}^{\prime}\allowbreak=\allowbreak\mathit{payinfo}
and do the following:

–

If there is no tuple such that
\mathit{payinfo}^{\prime}\allowbreak=\allowbreak\mathit{payinfo}
, set
c\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak 0
.

–

If there are
K\allowbreak>\allowbreak 1
tuples such that
\mathit{payinfo}^{\prime}\allowbreak=\allowbreak\mathit{payinfo}
, set
c\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak(\mathcal{P}_{1},\allowbreak\ldots,\allowbreak\mathcal{P}_{K})
, where
(\mathcal{P}_{1},\allowbreak\ldots,\allowbreak\mathcal{P}_{K})
are providers that deposited payments with
\mathit{payinfo}
more than once, or that deposited a payment such that
\mathcal{P}_{k}
is not included in
\mathit{payinfo}
.

–

If there is one tuple such that
\mathit{payinfo}^{\prime}\allowbreak=\allowbreak\mathit{payinfo}
, output
c\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak\mathcal{P}_{k}
if the identity of the provider is not in
\mathit{payinfo}
. Else, for all the remaining tuples
(\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathcal{P}^{\prime}_{k},\mathit{pay}^{\prime},\allowbreak\mathit{payinfo}^{\prime})
, run the algorithm
c\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak\mathsf{Identify}(\mathit{params},\allowbreak\mathit{PK},\allowbreak\mathit{pay},\allowbreak\mathit{pay}^{\prime},\allowbreak\mathit{payinfo},\allowbreak\mathit{payinfo}^{\prime})
until
c\allowbreak\neq\allowbreak 1
. If
c\allowbreak=\allowbreak 1
for all tuples, set
c\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak 1
.

•

Output
(\mathsf{ec.depvf.end},\allowbreak\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{payinfo},\allowbreak c)
.

Figure 2.
Construction
\Pi_{\mathrm{EC}}

In Figure
2
, we describe our construction
\mathrm{\Pi}_{\mathrm{EC}}
for
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{EC}}
.

\mathrm{\Pi}_{\mathrm{EC}}
uses
the ideal functionalities
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{SMT}}
for secure message transmission,
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{NYM}}
for a pseudonymous channel,
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{KG}}
for key generation,
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{REG}}
for registration and
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{BB}}
for an authenticated bulletin board, which are described in
C
.
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{SMT}}
is used for the communication channel between users and authorities in the
\mathsf{ec.request}
and
\mathsf{ec.issue}
interfaces, while
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{NYM}}
is used for the communication channel between users and providers in the
\mathsf{ec.spend}
interface.
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{KG}}
runs algorithms
\mathsf{Setup}
and
\mathsf{KeyGenV}
, and is used in the
\mathsf{ec.setup}
and
\mathsf{ec.register}
interfaces to generate and distribute both the parameters of the scheme and the keys of the authorities.
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{REG}}
is used in the
\mathsf{ec.register}
interface to register the user public keys, and in the
\mathsf{ec.issue}
and
\mathsf{ec.depvf}
interfaces to give those keys to authorities.
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{BB}}
is used in the
\mathsf{ec.deposit}
interface to deposit payments, and in the
\mathsf{ec.depvf}
interface to let authorities retrieve the deposited payments. We remark that
\mathrm{\Pi}_{\mathrm{EC}}
also uses a functionality for random oracle
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{RO}}
as in
(
Rial and Piotrowska 2022
)
to model the random oracle queries done in the algorithms used as a building block. However, this is omitted in the description of
\mathrm{\Pi}_{\mathrm{EC}}
.

Additionally,
\mathrm{\Pi}_{\mathrm{EC}}
uses the algorithms defined below. We define these algorithms to simplify the description of
\mathrm{\Pi}_{\mathrm{EC}}
. In §
5
, we instantiate them for both our compact and divisible e-cash schemes.

\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\mathit{k}},L)
.:

It computes the system parameters
\mathit{params}
on input the security parameter
1^{\mathit{k}}
and
the number of coins
L
in a full wallet. These parameters are publicly available.

\mathsf{KeyGenV}(\mathit{params},t,n)
.:

Given
\mathit{params}
, the threshold
t
, and the number of authorities
n
, output the public verification key
\mathit{pk}
and the key pairs
(\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{V}_{i}},\allowbreak\mathit{pk}_{\mathcal{V}_{i}})_{i\in[1,n]}
for each of the authorities.

\mathsf{KeyGenU}(\mathit{params})
.:

It is run by each user
\mathcal{U}_{j}
to generate
a secret key and a public key
(\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}},\allowbreak\mathit{pk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}})
.

\mathsf{Request}(\mathit{params},\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}})
.:

On input
\mathit{params}
and the secret key
\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}}
of the user
\mathcal{U}_{j}
, output a request
\mathit{req}
and request information
\mathit{reqinfo}
.

\mathsf{RequestVf}(\mathit{params},\mathit{req},\mathit{pk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}})
.:

Given
\mathit{params}
, a request
\mathit{req}
and the public key
\mathit{pk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}}
of the user
\mathcal{U}_{j}
, output
1
if the request is valid and
0
otherwise.

\mathsf{Issue}(\mathit{params},\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{V}_{i}},\mathit{req})
.:

On input
\mathit{params}
, the secret key of authority
\mathcal{V}_{i}
and a request
\mathit{req}
, output a response
\mathit{res}
.

\mathsf{IssueVf}(\mathit{params},\mathit{pk}_{\mathcal{V}_{i}},\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}},\mathit{res},\mathit{reqinfo})
.:

Given
\mathit{params}
, the public key
\mathit{pk}_{\mathcal{V}_{i}}
of authority
\mathcal{V}_{i}
, the secret key
\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}}
of user
\mathcal{U}_{j}
, a response
\mathit{res}
and request information
\mathit{reqinfo}
, output a partial wallet
\mathit{W}_{i}
if the response
\mathit{res}
is correct and is associated to a request with request information
\mathit{reqinfo}
, else output
0
.

\mathsf{AggrWallet}(\mathit{pk},\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}},\mathit{\mathbb{S}},\langle\mathit{W}_{i}\rangle_{i\in\mathit{\mathbb{S}}})
.:

Given the public key
\mathit{pk}
, the secret key
\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}}
, a set of indices
\mathit{\mathbb{S}}\in[1,n]
and partial wallets
\langle\mathit{W}_{i}\rangle_{i\in\mathit{\mathbb{S}}}
, output a wallet
\mathit{W}
if
|\mathit{\mathbb{S}}|\geq t
, else output
0
.

\mathsf{Spend}(\mathit{pk},\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}},\mathit{W},\mathit{payinfo},V)
.:

Given the public key
\mathit{pk}
, the secret key
\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}}
, a wallet
\mathit{W}
, payment information
\mathit{payinfo}
, and a number of coins
V
, outputs an updated wallet
\mathit{W}^{\prime}
and a payment
\mathit{pay}
if there are
V
non-spent coins in
\mathit{W}
or
0
otherwise.

\mathsf{SpendVf}(\mathit{pk},\mathit{pay},\mathit{payinfo})
.:

Given the public key
\mathit{pk}
, a payment
\mathit{pay}
, and payment information
\mathit{payinfo}
,
output the number
V
of coins received
if the payment is correct, or
0
otherwise.

\mathsf{Identify}(\mathit{params},\mathit{PK},\mathit{pay}_{1},\mathit{pay}_{2},\mathit{payinfo}_{1},\mathit{payinfo}_{2})
.:

Given
\mathit{params}
, a list of user public keys
\mathit{PK}
, and two payments
\mathit{pay}_{1}
and
\mathit{pay}_{2}
with respective payment information
\mathit{payinfo}_{1}
and
\mathit{payinfo}_{2}
:

•:

Output
1
if
\mathit{pay}_{1}
and
\mathit{pay}_{2}
are payments where different coins were used.

•:

Else, output
\mathit{payinfo}_{1}
if
\mathit{payinfo}_{1}\allowbreak=\allowbreak\mathit{payinfo}_{2}
, which indicates that the payment has been double deposited.

•:

Else, output the public key
\mathit{pk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}}\allowbreak\in\allowbreak\mathit{PK}
of the user
\mathcal{U}_{j}
that double spent a coin in payments
\mathit{pay}_{1}
and
\mathit{pay}_{2}
.

•:

Else, output
\bot
.

Remark about
\Pi_{\mathrm{EC}}
.

In the
\mathsf{ec.register}
interface, providers generate their own key pair, although it is not used later in the protocol. This is done to simplify the description of the protocol by making users and providers call the same
\mathsf{ec.register}
interface. Nevertheless, when the protocol is instantiated by replacing the ideal functionalities used as building blocks with concrete protocols that realize them, providers will need to generate their own keys.

5.
Instantiation of
\mathrm{\Pi}_{\mathrm{EC}}

5.1.
Threshold Issuance Compact Ecash

Our compact
\mathrm{EC}
scheme is based on the scheme proposed in
(
Camenisch et al. 2005
)
.
In order to provide threshold issuance, we use the Coconut protocol
(
Sonnino et al. 2019
)
with the modifications in
(
Rial and Piotrowska 2022
)
. We also make some changes in the scheme in
(
Camenisch et al. 2005
)
to improve efficiency (see
5.1.2
).

5.1.1.
High-level Overview

In
(
Camenisch et al. 2005
)
, a central bank plays the role of the authority. In the setup phase, the bank generates a key pair for a signature scheme, and each of the users generates a key pair.

Withdrawal Phase.:

A wallet of
L
coins is a signature under the bank’s public key on a user secret key
\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}}
and two random values
\mathit{v}
and
\mathit{t}
. The user
\mathcal{U}_{j}
obtains the signature from the bank on
(\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}},\allowbreak\mathit{v},\allowbreak\mathit{t})
through a blind signature protocol. The bank does not learn any of the signed values, but learns the user public key
\mathit{pk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}}
associated with
\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}}
.

Spending Phase.:

In order to spend coin
l\in[0,L-1]
,
\mathcal{U}_{j}
proves in zero-knowledge (ZK) possession of a signature on
(\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}},\allowbreak\mathit{v},\allowbreak\mathit{t})
. Additionally,
\mathcal{U}_{j}
generates a serial number
S
and a double-spending tag
T
, which are used to detect and identify double-spenders.
S
and
T
are computed by evaluating the pseudorandom function (PF) in §
A.6
on input
l
. Concretely,
S
is the output of the PF
f_{\mathit{g},\mathit{v}}
on input
l
.

T
is computed on input
\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}}
, the output of the PF
f_{\mathit{g},\mathit{t}}
on input
l
, and
R\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak H(\mathit{payinfo})
.
\mathit{payinfo}
is given by the provider and should be unique for each payment.

\mathcal{U}_{j}
also proves in ZK that
S
and
T
are correctly computed.

Deposit phase.:

The provider sends to the bank the payment received from the user. To check whether the coin has been double-spent, the bank compares the serial number
S
with the serial numbers of previously received coins. If there is a match, but the payment information
\mathit{payinfo}
is the same in both coins, then the bank finds that the provider has deposited the coin twice. Else, the bank identifies the user that double-spent the coin by using the double-spending tags of both coins.

5.1.2.
Our extensions

In our compact
\mathrm{EC}
scheme, the signature scheme is instantiated with PS signatures, which are described in §
A.5
. In the setup phase, the secret keys
\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{V}_{i}}
for each of the authorities
(\mathcal{V}_{1},\allowbreak\ldots,\allowbreak\mathcal{V}_{n})
are generated by evaluating random polynomials of degree
t-1
on input
[1,n]
, while the verification key
\mathit{pk}
used to verify wallets corresponds to a secret key that would be given by the evaluation of those polynomials on input
0
. In the withdrawal phase, the user runs a blind signature protocol with at least
t
authorities. After obtaining at least
t
valid signatures, the user uses Lagrange interpolation to obtain a signature verifiable with
\mathit{pk}
.

A wallet is a signature on
(\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}},\allowbreak\mathit{v})
. In comparison to
(
Camenisch et al. 2005
)
, we remove the secret
\mathit{t}
. Thanks to this change, the size of the wallet is smaller, and the ZK proofs used in both the withdrawal and spending phase are more efficient in comparison to
(
Camenisch et al. 2005
)
. To make this change possible, we modify the way the serial number
S
and double spending tag
T
are computed. Concretely,
S
is the output of the PF
f_{\delta,\mathit{v}}
on input
l
, and the computation of
T
uses the evaluation of the PF
f_{\mathit{g},\mathit{v}}
on input
l
, i.e., we use a new generator
\delta
for the computation of the serial numbers. This change allows us to use the same secret
\mathit{v}
as the index of both PFs without compromising the security of our scheme. In §
F
, we quantify the cost reduction attained by removing
\mathit{t}
.
We further improve the efficiency of the withdrawal phase, by removing the need for the bank to contribute randomness to create
\mathit{v}
in the blind signature protocol in
(
Camenisch et al. 2005
)
. In our protocol, the user picks
\mathit{v}
and as discussed in §
5.1.4
, this change does not compromise the security of our scheme. We also improve efficiency by using one ZK proof
\pi_{v}
to spend
V
coins, instead of repeating
V
times the spending protocol for one coin.

5.1.3.
Construction

The algorithms of our compact
\mathrm{EC}
scheme are defined below. In §
A
, we describe the cryptographic primitives used by the algorithms.

\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\mathit{k}},L)
.:

Execute the following steps:

•:

Run
(\mathit{p},\mathbb{G},\mathbb{\tilde{G}},\allowbreak{\mathbb{G}_{t}},\mathit{e},\mathit{g},\mathit{\tilde{g}})\leftarrow\mathcal{G}(1^{\mathit{k}})
.

•:

Pick
3
random generators
(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\delta)\leftarrow\mathbb{G}
.

•:

Output
\mathit{params}\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak(\mathit{p},\allowbreak\mathbb{G},\allowbreak\mathbb{\tilde{G}},\allowbreak{\mathbb{G}_{t}},\allowbreak\mathit{e},\allowbreak\mathit{g},\allowbreak\mathit{\tilde{g}},\allowbreak\gamma_{1},\allowbreak\gamma_{2},\allowbreak\delta,\allowbreak L)
.

\mathsf{KeyGenV}(\mathit{params},t,n)
.:

Execute the following steps:

•:

Choose
(1+2)
polynomials
(v,w_{1},w_{2})
of degree
(t-1)
with random coefficients in
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
.

•:

Set
(x,y_{1},y_{2})\leftarrow(v(0),w_{1}(0),w_{2}(0))
.

•:

For
i=1
to
n
, set the secret key
\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{V}_{i}}
of each authority
\mathcal{V}_{i}
as
\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{V}_{i}}\allowbreak=\allowbreak(x_{i},\allowbreak y_{i,1},\allowbreak y_{i,2})\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak(v(i),\allowbreak w_{1}(i),\allowbreak w_{2}(i))
.

•:

For
i=1
to
n
, set the verification key
\mathit{pk}_{\mathcal{V}_{i}}
of each authority
\mathcal{V}_{i}
as
\mathit{pk}_{\mathcal{V}_{i}}\allowbreak=\allowbreak(\tilde{\alpha}_{i},\allowbreak\beta_{i,1},\allowbreak\tilde{\beta}_{i,1},\allowbreak\beta_{i,2},\allowbreak\tilde{\beta}_{i,2})\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak(\mathit{\tilde{g}}^{x_{i}},\allowbreak\mathit{g}^{y_{i,1}},\allowbreak\mathit{\tilde{g}}^{y_{i,1}},\allowbreak\mathit{g}^{y_{i,2}},\allowbreak\mathit{\tilde{g}}^{y_{i,2}})
.

•:

Set the verification key
\mathit{pk}=(\mathit{params},\allowbreak\tilde{\alpha},\allowbreak\beta_{1},\allowbreak\tilde{\beta}_{1},\allowbreak\beta_{2},\tilde{\beta}_{2})\leftarrow\allowbreak(\mathit{params},\allowbreak\mathit{\tilde{g}}^{x},\allowbreak\mathit{g}^{y_{1}},\allowbreak\mathit{\tilde{g}}^{y_{1}},\allowbreak\mathit{g}^{y_{2}},\allowbreak\mathit{\tilde{g}}^{y_{2}})
.

•:

Output
(\mathit{pk},\langle\mathit{pk}_{\mathcal{V}_{i}},\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{V}_{i}}\rangle_{i=1}^{n})
.

\mathsf{KeyGenU}(\mathit{params})
.:

Execute the following steps:

•:

Pick random
\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}}\leftarrow\mathbb{Z}_{p}
and compute
