\mathit{pk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}}\leftarrow\mathit{g}^{\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}}}
.

•:

Output
(\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}},\allowbreak\mathit{pk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}})
.

\mathsf{Request}(\mathit{params},\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}})
.:

Execute the following steps:

•:

Pick random
\mathit{v}\leftarrow\mathbb{Z}_{p}
and set
(\mathit{m}_{1},\allowbreak\mathit{m}_{2})\allowbreak=(\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}},\allowbreak\mathit{v})
.

•:

Pick random
o\leftarrow\mathbb{Z}_{p}
and compute
\mathit{com}=\mathit{g}^{o}\prod_{j=1}^{2}\gamma_{j}^{\mathit{m}_{j}}
.

•:

Compute
\mathit{h}\leftarrow H(\mathit{com})
, where
H
is a hash function modeled as a random oracle.

•:

Compute commitments to each of the messages. For
j=1
to
2
, pick random
o_{j}\leftarrow\mathbb{Z}_{p}
and set
\mathit{com}_{j}=\mathit{g}^{o_{j}}\mathit{h}^{\mathit{m}_{j}}
.

•:

Compute a ZK argument of knowledge
\pi_{s}
via the Fiat-Shamir heuristic for the following relation:

\displaystyle\pi_{s}=

\displaystyle\mathrm{NIZK}\{(\mathit{m}_{1},\mathit{m}_{2},o,o_{1},o_{2}):\mathit{com}=\mathit{g}^{o}\prod_{j=1}^{2}\gamma_{j}^{\mathit{m}_{j}}\ \land\

\displaystyle\mathit{pk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}}\leftarrow\mathit{g}^{\mathit{m}_{1}}\land\ \{\mathit{com}_{j}=\mathit{g}^{o_{j}}\mathit{h}^{\mathit{m}_{j}}\}_{\forall j\in[1,2]}\}

•:

Set
\mathit{reqinfo}\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak(\mathit{h},\allowbreak o_{1},\allowbreak o_{2},\allowbreak\mathit{v})
and
\mathit{req}\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak(\mathit{h},\allowbreak\mathit{com},\allowbreak\mathit{com}_{1},\allowbreak\mathit{com}_{2},\allowbreak\pi_{s})
.

•:

Output
\mathit{req}
and
\mathit{reqinfo}
.

\mathsf{RequestVf}(\mathit{params},\mathit{req},\mathit{pk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}})
.:

Execute the following steps:

•:

Parse
\mathit{req}
as
(\mathit{h},\allowbreak\mathit{com},\allowbreak\mathit{com}_{1},\allowbreak\mathit{com}_{2},\allowbreak\pi_{s})
.

•:

Compute
\mathit{h}^{\prime}\leftarrow H(\mathit{com})
and output
0
if
\mathit{h}\neq\mathit{h}^{\prime}
.

•:

Verify the ZK argument
\pi_{s}
by using the tuple
(\mathit{params},\allowbreak\mathit{h},\allowbreak\mathit{com},\allowbreak\mathit{com}_{1},\allowbreak\mathit{com}_{2},\allowbreak\mathit{pk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}})
. Output
0
if the proof
\pi_{s}
is not correct, else output
1
.

\mathsf{Issue}(\mathit{params},\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{V}_{i}},\mathit{req})
.:

Execute the following steps:

•:

Parse
\mathit{req}
as
(\mathit{h},\allowbreak\mathit{com},\allowbreak\mathit{com}_{1},\allowbreak\mathit{com}_{2},\allowbreak\pi_{s})
and
\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{V}_{i}}
as
(x_{i},\allowbreak y_{i,1},\allowbreak y_{i,2})
.

•:

Compute
c=\mathit{h}^{x_{i}}\prod_{j=1}^{2}\mathit{com}_{j}^{y_{i,j}}
.

•:

Set the blinded signature share
\hat{\sigma}_{i}\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak(\mathit{h},\allowbreak c)
.

•:

Output
\mathit{res}\leftarrow\hat{\sigma}_{i}
.

\mathsf{IssueVf}(\mathit{params},\mathit{pk}_{\mathcal{V}_{i}},\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}},\mathit{res},\mathit{reqinfo})
.:

Do the following:

•:

Parse
\mathit{reqinfo}
as
(\mathit{h}^{\prime},\allowbreak o_{1},\allowbreak o_{2},\allowbreak\mathit{v})
,
\mathit{res}
as
\hat{\sigma}_{i}\allowbreak=\allowbreak(\mathit{h},\allowbreak c)
, and
\mathit{pk}_{\mathcal{V}_{i}}
as
(\tilde{\alpha}_{i},\allowbreak\beta_{i,1},\allowbreak\tilde{\beta}_{i,1},\allowbreak\beta_{i,2},\allowbreak\tilde{\beta}_{i,2})
. Output
0
if
\mathit{h}\allowbreak\neq\allowbreak\mathit{h}^{\prime}
.

•:

Compute
\sigma_{i}\allowbreak=\allowbreak(\mathit{h},\allowbreak s)\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak(\mathit{h},\allowbreak c\prod_{j=1}^{2}\beta_{i,j}^{-o_{j}})
.

•:

Set
(\mathit{m}_{1},\mathit{m}_{2})\leftarrow(\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}},\mathit{v})
. Output
0
if
\mathit{e}(\mathit{h},\tilde{\alpha}_{i}\prod_{j=1}^{2}\tilde{\beta}_{i,j}^{\mathit{m}_{j}})\allowbreak=\allowbreak\mathit{e}(s,\mathit{\tilde{g}})
does not hold.

•:

Output
\mathit{W}_{i}\leftarrow(i,\sigma_{i},\mathit{v})
.

\mathsf{AggrWallet}(\mathit{pk},\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}},\mathit{\mathbb{S}},\langle\mathit{W}_{i}\rangle_{i\in\mathit{\mathbb{S}}})
.:

Execute the following steps:

•:

If
|\mathit{\mathbb{S}}|\neq t
, output
0
.

•:

For all
i\in\mathit{\mathbb{S}}
, evaluate at 0 the Lagrange basis polynomials
l_{i}=[\prod_{j\in\mathit{\mathbb{S}},j\neq i}(0-j)][\prod_{j\in\mathit{\mathbb{S}},j\neq i}(i-j)]^{-1}\ \mathrm{mod}\ \mathit{p}

•:

For all
i\in\mathit{\mathbb{S}}
, parse
\mathit{W}_{i}
as
(i,\sigma_{i},\mathit{v})
and
\sigma_{i}
as
(\mathit{h},\allowbreak s_{i})
.

•:

Compute the signature
\sigma=(\mathit{h},s)\leftarrow(\mathit{h},\allowbreak\prod_{i\in\mathit{\mathbb{S}}}s_{i}^{l_{i}})
.

•:

Parse
\mathit{pk}
as
(\mathit{params},\allowbreak\tilde{\alpha},\allowbreak\beta_{1},\allowbreak\tilde{\beta}_{1},\beta_{2},\tilde{\beta}_{2})
.

•:

Set
(\mathit{m}_{1},\allowbreak\mathit{m}_{2})\allowbreak=\allowbreak(\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}},\allowbreak\mathit{v})
and output
0
if
\mathit{e}(\mathit{h},\tilde{\alpha}\prod_{j=1}^{2}\tilde{\beta}_{j}^{\mathit{m}_{j}})=\mathit{e}(s,\mathit{\tilde{g}})
does not hold, else output
\mathit{W}\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak(\sigma,\allowbreak\mathit{v},l)
, where
l
is a counter from
0
to
L-1
initialized to
0
.

\mathsf{Spend}(\mathit{pk},\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}},\mathit{W},\mathit{payinfo},V)
.:

Execute the following steps:

•:

Parse
\mathit{W}
as
(\sigma,\allowbreak\mathit{v},\allowbreak l)
. If
l+V-1\geq L
, output
0
.

•:

Parse
\sigma
as
(\mathit{h},\allowbreak s)
and
\mathit{pk}
as
(\mathit{params},\allowbreak\tilde{\alpha},\allowbreak\beta_{1},\allowbreak\tilde{\beta}_{1},\beta_{2},\tilde{\beta}_{2})
.

•:

Pick random
r\leftarrow\mathbb{Z}_{p}
and
r^{\prime}\leftarrow\mathbb{Z}_{p}
.

•:

Compute
\sigma^{\prime}=(\mathit{h}^{\prime},s^{\prime})\leftarrow(\mathit{h}^{r^{\prime}},s^{r^{\prime}}(\mathit{h}^{\prime})^{r})
and
\kappa\leftarrow\tilde{\alpha}\tilde{\beta}_{1}^{\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}}}\tilde{\beta}_{2}^{\mathit{v}}\mathit{\tilde{g}}^{r}
.

•:

Pick random
o_{c}\leftarrow\mathbb{Z}_{p}
and compute the commitment
C\leftarrow\mathit{g}^{o_{c}}\gamma_{1}^{\mathit{v}}
.

•:

For
k\in[0,V-1]
, compute
R_{k}\leftarrow H^{\prime}(\mathit{payinfo},k)
, where
\mathit{payinfo}
must contain the identifier of the merchant, and
H^{\prime}
is a collision-resistant hash function.

•:

For
k\in[0,V-1]
, set
l_{k}\leftarrow l+k
, pick random
o_{a_{k}}
and compute
A_{k}=\mathit{g}^{o_{a_{k}}}\gamma_{1}^{l_{k}}
.

•:

For
k\in[0,V-1]
, compute the serial numbers
S_{k}\leftarrow f_{\delta,\mathit{v}}(l_{k})=\delta^{1/(\mathit{v}+l_{k}+1)}
and also compute the double spending tags
T_{k}\leftarrow\mathit{g}^{\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}}}(f_{\mathit{g},\mathit{v}}(l_{k}))^{R_{k}}=\mathit{g}^{\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}}+R_{k}/(\mathit{v}+l_{k}+1)}
.

•:

For
k\in[0,V-1]
, compute the values
\mu_{k}\leftarrow 1/(\mathit{v}+l_{k}+1)
and
o_{\mu_{k}}\leftarrow-(o_{a_{k}}+o_{c})\mu_{k}
.

•:

Compute a ZK argument of knowledge
\pi_{v}
via the Fiat-Shamir heuristic for the following relation:

\displaystyle\pi_{v}=

\displaystyle\mathrm{NIZK}\{(\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}},\mathit{v},r,o_{c},\langle l_{k},o_{a_{k}},\mu_{k},o_{\mu_{k}}\rangle_{k=0}^{V-1}):

\displaystyle\kappa=\tilde{\alpha}\tilde{\beta}_{1}^{\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}}}\tilde{\beta}_{2}^{\mathit{v}}\mathit{\tilde{g}}^{r}\ \land\ C=\mathit{g}^{o_{c}}\gamma_{1}^{\mathit{v}}\ \land\

\displaystyle\langle A_{k}=\mathit{g}^{o_{a_{k}}}\gamma_{1}^{l_{k}}\ \land\ l_{k}\in[0,L-1]\ \land\

\displaystyle S_{k}=\delta^{\mu_{k}}\ \land\ \gamma_{1}=(A_{k}C\gamma_{1})^{\mu_{k}}\mathit{g}^{o_{\mu_{k}}}\ \land\

\displaystyle T_{k}=\mathit{g}^{\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}}}(\mathit{g}^{R_{k}})^{\mu_{k}}\ \rangle_{k\in[0,V-1]}\}

In §
F
, we explain this ZK proof and show how to prove the statement
l_{k}\in[0,L-1]
.

•:

Output a payment
\mathit{pay}\leftarrow(\kappa,\sigma^{\prime},\langle S_{k},T_{k},A_{k}\rangle_{k\in[0,V-1]},V,C,\allowbreak\pi_{v})
and an updated wallet
\mathit{W}^{\prime}\leftarrow(\sigma,\allowbreak\mathit{v},\allowbreak l+V)
.

\mathsf{SpendVf}(\mathit{pk},\mathit{pay},\mathit{payinfo})
.:

Execute the following steps:

•:

Parse
\mathit{pk}
as
(\mathit{params},\allowbreak\tilde{\alpha},\allowbreak\beta_{1},\allowbreak\tilde{\beta}_{1},\beta_{2},\tilde{\beta}_{2})
.

•:

Parse
\mathit{pay}
as
(\kappa,\sigma^{\prime},\langle S_{k},T_{k},A_{k}\rangle_{k\in[0,V-1]},V,C,\pi_{v})
.

•:

Parse
\sigma^{\prime}
as
(\mathit{h}^{\prime},\allowbreak s^{\prime})
and output
0
if
\mathit{h}^{\prime}=1
or if
\mathit{e}(\mathit{h}^{\prime},\kappa)\allowbreak=\allowbreak\mathit{e}(s^{\prime},\mathit{\tilde{g}})
does not hold.

•:

Output
0
if not all the serial numbers
\langle S_{k}\rangle_{k\in[0,V-1]}
are different from each other.

•:

For
k\in[0,V-1]
, compute
R_{k}\leftarrow H^{\prime}(\mathit{payinfo},k)
.

•:

Verify
\pi_{v}
by using
\mathit{payinfo}
,
\mathit{pk}
,
\langle S_{k},T_{k},A_{k},R_{k}\rangle_{k\in[0,V-1]}
,
C
and
\kappa
. Output
0
if the proof is not correct, else output
V
.

\mathsf{Identify}(\mathit{params},\mathit{PK},\mathit{pay}_{1},\mathit{pay}_{2},\mathit{payinfo}_{1},\mathit{payinfo}_{2})
.:

Do:

•:

Parse
\mathit{pay}_{1}
as
(\kappa_{1},\sigma^{\prime}_{1},\langle S_{k,1},T_{k,1},A_{k,1}\rangle_{k\in[0,V_{1}-1]},V_{1},C_{1},\pi_{v,1})
and
\mathit{pay}_{2}
as
(\kappa_{2},\sigma^{\prime}_{2},\langle S_{k,2},T_{k,2},A_{k,2}\rangle_{k\in[0,V_{2}-1]},V_{2},C_{2},\pi_{v,2})
.

•:

For
k\in[0,V_{1}-1]
, for
j\in[0,V_{2}-1]
, check whether
S_{k,1}\allowbreak=\allowbreak S_{j,2}
. If the equality never holds, output
1
.

•:

Else, output
\mathit{payinfo}_{1}
if
\mathit{payinfo}_{1}=\mathit{payinfo}_{2}
.

•:

Else, for
k\in[0,V_{1}-1]
and
j\in[0,V_{2}-1]
such that
S_{k,1}=S_{j,2}
, compute

\mathit{pk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}}\leftarrow(T_{j,2}^{R_{k,1}}/T_{k,1}^{R_{j,2}})^{(R_{k,1}-R_{j,2})^{-1}}

If
\mathit{pk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}}\in\mathit{PK}
output
\mathit{pk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}}
, else output
\bot
.

5.1.4.
Security Analysis of Compact E-Cash

In §
D
, we prove formally that
\mathrm{\Pi}_{\mathrm{EC}}
, when instantiated with the algorithms of our compact
\mathrm{EC}
scheme, realizes
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{EC}}
. In this section, we give intuition on why our scheme is secure.

Unlinkability.:

In the withdrawal phase, a corrupt authority does not learn the user secrets
(\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}},\allowbreak\mathit{v})
thanks to the hiding property of the Pedersen commitment scheme and to the ZK property of the argument
\pi_{s}
. In the spend phase, a corrupt provider does not learn anything from a payment beyond the number of coins spent. To prove that, several properties are used. First, we use the ZK property of the argument
\pi_{v}
. Second, to prove that
C
and
A_{k}
do not reveal any information about
\mathit{v}
or
l_{k}
, we use the hiding property of the Pedersen commitment scheme. Third, to prove that
S_{k}
and
T_{k}
do not reveal any information about
\mathit{pk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}}
,
\mathit{v}
or
l_{k}
, we use the pseudorandomness property of the PF, along with the XDH assumption.
1
1

1

In contrast to
(
Camenisch et al. 2005
)
, the XDH assumption is needed in our scheme because we use the same coin secret
\mathit{v}
to compute
S_{k}
and
T_{k}
.
Finally, as in the modified version of Coconut in
(
Rial and Piotrowska 2022
)
, the PS signature is “randomized” in a way that enables us to prove signature possession without revealing any information about the original signature or the signed messages.

Traceability, Exculpability and Clearance.:

To prove that a user cannot spend coins that she has not withdrawn before, we use several properties of our building blocks. The weak simulation extractability property allows us to extract the witnesses from the ZK arguments
\pi_{s}
. Thanks to that extraction, we can use the binding property of the commitment scheme
\mathit{com}
included in a request message to ensure that different commitments
\mathit{com}
and
\mathit{com}^{\prime}
commit to different tuples
(\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}},\allowbreak\mathit{v})
. This is required for the unforgeability of PS signatures in the RO model. We remark that
\mathit{com}
is the input to the random oracle and that it is necessary to ensure that a different generator
\mathit{h}
is created to sign different message tuples. The binding property of
\mathit{com}
guarantees that. Second, we use the weak simulation extractability property of arguments
\pi_{v}
to extract the witnesses. Thanks to that, in the spending phase, we can extract a signature on
(\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}},\allowbreak\mathit{v})
from a payment message and show that, if a user did not withdraw at least
t
signatures from
t
different authorities, then the user can be used to break the existential unforgeability property of PS signatures in the RO model.

Therefore, we know that, if more coins are deposited than those being withdrawn, it is the case that a user has double-spent coins or that a provider has double-deposited coins. In the deposit phase, an authority checks that payments that are deposited have different serial numbers. We show that the extractability of
\pi_{v}
, along with the discrete logarithm assumption, guarantees that the serial numbers and double spending tags are correctly computed. Therefore, if two payments have at least one common serial number, there are three possibilities:

(1):

Double depositing,

(2):

double spending,

(3):

none of the former.

Double depositing can be punished by checking whether two payments are associated with the same payment information
\mathit{payinfo}
, which contains the identifier of the provider. We recall that
\mathit{payinfo}
is signed in
\pi_{v}
. Moreover, our construction
\mathrm{\Pi}_{\mathrm{EC}}
uses an authenticated bulletin board. Thus, an authority can check that the provider that deposits a payment is the same whose identity is in
\mathit{payinfo}
. The latter guarantees the clearance property. If double depositing did not happen because the payment information
\mathit{payinfo}
and
\mathit{payinfo}^{\prime}
is different in those payments, the authority can retrieve the public key of the user who double spent a coin through the computation described in the algorithm
\mathsf{Identify}
. This computation requires that
R_{k}\leftarrow H^{\prime}(\mathit{payinfo},k)
is different from
R^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}}\leftarrow H^{\prime}(\mathit{payinfo}^{\prime},k^{\prime})
. We show that, if the hash function
H^{\prime}
is collision-resistant, the double spender can always be identified. In our scheme, a corrupt user is able to compute two payments where there is no double spending, yet two serial numbers are equal. The reason is that, unlike in the scheme in
(
Camenisch et al. 2005
)
, the user picks the coin secret
\mathit{v}
on its own. When a corrupt user does that, our security analysis guarantees that, under the hardness of the discrete logarithm assumption, algorithm
\mathsf{Identify}
will never identify an honest user as the double spender. This guarantees the exculpability property. Moreover, we also show that, under the discrete logarithm assumption, a corrupt user cannot compute a payment with a serial number that is equal to a serial number in a payment computed by an honest user. Consequently, when
\mathsf{Identify}
detects that two serial numbers are equal, but is unable to find the public key of the user who double spent (i.e.
\mathsf{Identify}
outputs
\bot
), we are in a case in which in fact there is no double-spending.

5.2.
Threshold Issuance Divisible E-Cash

In our compact
\mathrm{EC}
scheme in §
5.1
, the cost of the spending phase grows linearly with the number of coins spent. In divisible e-cash, the cost of the spending phase is independent of the number of coins spent. To make that possible, the main change in comparison to compact e-cash is that the serial numbers of coins are generated during the deposit phase, rather than the spending phase.

Our divisible
\mathrm{EC}
scheme is based on the work by Pointcheval et al.
(
Pointcheval et al. 2017
)
, which
proposes a scheme in the standard model with Groth-Sahai proofs
(
Groth and Sahai 2008
)
. We modify that scheme as follows. In
(
Pointcheval et al. 2017
)
, a wallet is a signature on two group elements
(U_{1},U_{2})=(u_{1}^{\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}}},\allowbreak u_{2}^{\mathit{v}})
We replace the signature scheme used in
(
Pointcheval et al. 2017
)
by the PS signature scheme, and we sign
(\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}},\allowbreak\mathit{v})
. Thus, the wallet in our divisible
\mathrm{EC}
scheme has the same form as in our compact
\mathrm{EC}
scheme. Thanks to that, in the withdrawal phase we use the same algorithms
\mathsf{Request}
,
\mathsf{RequestVf}
,
\mathsf{Issue}
,
\mathsf{IssueVf}
and
\mathsf{AggrWallet}
(see §
5.1.3
) to provide a threshold issuance protocol. At setup, algorithms
\mathsf{KeyGenV}
and
\mathsf{KeyGenU}
also work as in §
5.1.3
.

In the spending phase in
(
Pointcheval et al. 2017
)
, a Groth-Sahai non-interactive ZK proof and a Groth-Sahai non-interactive witness-indistinguishable proof are computed. The latter involves proof of possession of the signature on
(U_{1},\allowbreak U_{2})
. In our scheme, we use a NIZK argument computed via the Fiat-Shamir heuristic, which involves all the statements proven in both the Groth-Sahai ZK proof and witness-indistinguishable proof. To prove possession of a PS signature on
(\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}},\allowbreak\mathit{v})
, we use the method depicted in algorithm
\mathsf{Spend}
in §
5.1.3
.

Because Groth-Sahai proofs are randomizable, in
(
Pointcheval et al. 2017
)
, the user needs to compute a one-time signature on the payment. A statement is added to the Groth-Sahai proof to certify the public key used for the one-time signature. In our scheme, this is not needed, because non-interactive ZK arguments computed via the Fiat-Shamir heuristic are signatures of knowledge.

The remaining values computed in the spending phase, and the statements proven about them, are the same in
(
Pointcheval et al. 2017
)
and in our scheme. We note that our non-interactive ZK argument involves proving knowledge of group elements in addition to discrete logarithm representations. We show how this is done in §
A.3
.

5.2.1.
High-level Overview

A wallet of
L
coins is a signature on
(\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}},\allowbreak\mathit{v})
, where
\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}}
is the secret key of user
\mathcal{U}_{j}
and
\mathit{v}
is a coin secret. The
L
serial numbers of the coins in a wallet are given by

\mathrm{SN}_{l}\allowbreak=\allowbreak\mathit{e}(\varsigma,\mathit{\tilde{g}})^{\mathit{v}y^{l}},\ l\in[1,L]

where values
y
and
\varsigma
are part of the parameters of the scheme.

Withdrawal Phase.:

The withdrawal phase is the same as in our compact
\mathrm{EC}
scheme in §
5.1.3
.

Spending Phase.:

In the spending phase, to spend
V
coins, the user needs to give information that allows the authorities to compute the
V
serial numbers of the spent coins, but no more than that. To this end, to spend
V
coins with indices
[l,l+V-1]
, the user computes an ElGamal encryption
\phi_{V,l}
of
\varsigma_{l}^{\mathit{v}}
under the public key
\eta_{V}
.
The values
\varsigma_{l}
(for
l\allowbreak\in\allowbreak[1,L]
) and
\eta_{V}
(for
V\allowbreak\in\allowbreak[1,L]
) are part of the public parameters of the scheme.

\phi_{V,l}
is used in the deposit phase by the authorities to generate the serial numbers
(\mathrm{SN}_{l},\ldots,\mathrm{SN}_{l+V-1})
. This ELGamal encryption with public key
\eta_{V}
restricts the authorities to generate only those
V
serial numbers.

To enable identification of double spenders, the user computes the double spending tag
\varphi_{V,l}
as an ElGamal encryption of
(\mathit{g}^{R})^{\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}}}\theta_{l}^{\mathit{v}}
under public key
\eta_{V}
, where
R
is a hash of the payment information
\mathit{payinfo}
given by the provider. The values
\theta_{l}
, for
l\allowbreak\in\allowbreak[1,L]
,
are part of the parameters of the scheme.

The user also needs to prove in zero-knowledge that
\phi_{V,l}
and
\varphi_{V,l}
are correctly computed. To this end, the user proves possession of a signature on
(\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}},\allowbreak\mathit{v})
and proves that those values were used to compute
\phi_{V,l}
and
\varphi_{V,l}
. Additionally, the user needs to prove that the correct values
\varsigma_{l}
and
\theta_{l}
in the public parameters have been used, and that
l\allowbreak\leq\allowbreak L-V+1
. To allow the user to prove those statements, the public parameters of the scheme contain signatures on the pairs
(\varsigma_{l},\allowbreak\theta_{l})
(for
l\allowbreak\in\allowbreak[1,L]
). The user proves possession of the signature on the pair
(\varsigma_{l+V-1},\allowbreak\theta_{l+V-1})
and proves that
(\varsigma_{l},\allowbreak\theta_{l})
are correctly chosen through the equations
\mathit{e}(\varsigma_{l},\tilde{\delta}_{V-1})=\mathit{e}(\varsigma_{l+V-1},\tilde{g})
and
\mathit{e}(\theta_{l},\tilde{\delta}_{V-1})=\mathit{e}(\theta_{l+V-1},\tilde{g})
. The values
\tilde{\delta}_{k}\leftarrow\mathit{\tilde{g}}^{y^{k}}
(for
k\in[0,L-1]
) are part of the parameters of the scheme. Although this proof does not prove that
l\geq 1
, in the security analysis it is shown that the user is unable to generate
(\varsigma_{l},\allowbreak\theta_{l})
such that
l\allowbreak\notin\allowbreak[1,L]
.

Deposit Phase.:

In the deposit phase, an authority checks whether a coin has been double spent. For this purpose, the authority computes the serial numbers of the spent coins by doing
\mathrm{SN}_{k}\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak\mathit{e}(\phi_{V,l}[2],\tilde{\delta}_{k})\allowbreak\mathit{e}(\phi_{V,l}[1],\tilde{\eta}_{V,k})
for
k\in[0,V-1]
.
Here, the values
\tilde{\eta}_{l,k}

for
l\allowbreak\in\allowbreak[1,L]
and
k\allowbreak\in\allowbreak[0,\allowbreak l-1]

are part of the parameters of the scheme. Because of those values, the size of the parameters is quadratic in the number
L
of coins in a wallet, but this is only the case for the parameters that the authorities need, i.e., the size of parameters for users is linear in
L
.

When a collision between serial numbers of two payments is detected, the authority uses the security tags of both payments for identification of the user who double spent. The mechanism used is similar to the one of our compact
\mathrm{EC}
scheme in §
5.1
. However, in the divisible
\mathrm{EC}
scheme, the authority, rather than computing the user key, checks whether an equality holds for each of the public keys of the users one by one, which is a disadvantage.

5.2.2.
Construction

Our divisible e-cash scheme works as follows:

\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\mathit{k}},L)
.:

Execute the following steps:

•:

Run
\mathit{grp}=(\mathit{p},\mathbb{G},\mathbb{\tilde{G}},\allowbreak{\mathbb{G}_{t}},\mathit{e},\mathit{g},\mathit{\tilde{g}})\leftarrow\mathcal{G}(1^{\mathit{k}})
.

•:

Pick random generators
\eta,\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak\mathbb{G}
.

•:

Generate random scalars
(z,y)\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak\mathbb{Z}_{p}
and compute
(\varsigma,\theta)\leftarrow(\mathit{g}^{z},\eta^{z})
. Generate for
l\allowbreak\in\allowbreak[1,L]
,
a_{l}\leftarrow\mathbb{Z}_{p}
.

•:

For
l\allowbreak\in\allowbreak[1,L]
, compute
(\varsigma_{l},\theta_{l})\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak(\varsigma^{y^{l}},\allowbreak\theta^{y^{l}})
.

•:

For
k\in[0,L-1]
, compute
\tilde{\delta}_{k}\leftarrow\mathit{\tilde{g}}^{y^{k}}
.

•:

For
l\allowbreak\in\allowbreak[1,L]
, compute
\eta_{l}\leftarrow\mathit{g}^{a_{l}}
.

•:

For
l\allowbreak\in\allowbreak[1,L]
, for
k\allowbreak\in\allowbreak[0,\allowbreak l-1]
, compute
\tilde{\eta}_{l,k}\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak\mathit{\tilde{g}}^{-a_{l}\cdot y^{k}}
.

•:

Run algorithm
(\mathit{pk}_{sps},\allowbreak\mathit{sk}_{sps})\leftarrow\mathsf{KeyGen}(\mathit{grp},2,0)
of the SPS scheme in §
A.5
.

•:

For
l\allowbreak\in\allowbreak[1,L]
, compute
\tau_{l}\leftarrow\mathsf{Sign}(\mathit{sk}_{sps},\langle\varsigma_{l},\theta_{l}\rangle)
.

•:

Set the parameters for users
\mathit{params}_{u}\leftarrow(\mathit{p},\allowbreak\mathbb{G},\allowbreak\mathbb{\tilde{G}},\allowbreak{\mathbb{G}_{t}},\allowbreak\mathit{e},\allowbreak\mathit{g},\allowbreak\mathit{\tilde{g}},\allowbreak\eta,\allowbreak\gamma_{1},\allowbreak\gamma_{2},\allowbreak\{\eta_{l},\allowbreak\varsigma_{l},\allowbreak\theta_{l},\allowbreak\tau_{l}\}_{l=1}^{L},\allowbreak\{\tilde{\delta}_{k}\}_{k=0}^{L-1},\allowbreak\mathit{pk}_{sps})
. Set the additional parameters for authorities
\mathit{params}_{a}\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak(\{\langle\tilde{\eta}_{l,k}\rangle_{k=0}^{l-1}\}_{l=1}^{L-1})
.

•:

Output
\mathit{params}\leftarrow(\mathit{params}_{u},\mathit{params}_{a})
.

\mathsf{Spend}(\mathit{pk},\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}},\mathit{W},\mathit{payinfo},V)
.:

Execute the following steps:

•:

Parse
\mathit{W}
as
(\sigma,\allowbreak\mathit{v},\allowbreak l)
and
\sigma
as
(\mathit{h},\allowbreak s)
. If
l+V-1>L
, output
0
.

•:

Parse
\mathit{pk}
as
(\mathit{params}_{u},\allowbreak\tilde{\alpha},\allowbreak\beta_{1},\allowbreak\tilde{\beta}_{1},\beta_{2},\tilde{\beta}_{2})
.

•:

Pick random scalars
r\leftarrow\mathbb{Z}_{p}
and
r^{\prime}\leftarrow\mathbb{Z}_{p}
and compute
\sigma^{\prime}=(\mathit{h}^{\prime},s^{\prime})\leftarrow(\mathit{h}^{r^{\prime}},s^{r^{\prime}}(\mathit{h}^{\prime})^{r})
.

•:

Compute
\kappa\leftarrow\tilde{\alpha}\tilde{\beta}_{1}^{\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}}}\tilde{\beta}_{2}^{\mathit{v}}\mathit{\tilde{g}}^{r}
.

•:

Pick random
r_{1},r_{2}\leftarrow\mathbb{Z}_{p}
and set
\phi_{V,l}=(\phi_{V,l}[1],\phi_{V,l}[2])\leftarrow(\mathit{g}^{r_{1}},\varsigma_{l}^{\mathit{v}}\eta_{V}^{r_{1}})
.

•:

Set
R\leftarrow H^{\prime}(\mathit{payinfo})
, where
H^{\prime}
is a collision-resistant hash function, and compute

\varphi_{V,l}=(\varphi_{V,l}[1],\varphi_{V,l}[2])\leftarrow(\mathit{g}^{r_{2}},(\mathit{g}^{R})^{\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}}}\theta_{l}^{\mathit{v}}\eta_{V}^{r_{2}})

•:

Take
\mathit{params}_{u}
from
\mathit{pk}
. Take the public key
\mathit{pk}_{sps}\allowbreak=\allowbreak(Y,\allowbreak W_{1},\allowbreak W_{2},\allowbreak Z)
and the signature
\tau_{l+V-1}\allowbreak=\allowbreak(R_{l+V-1},\allowbreak S_{l+V-1},\allowbreak T_{l+V-1})
.

•:

Compute a ZK argument of knowledge
\pi_{v}
via the Fiat-Shamir heuristic for the following relation:

\displaystyle\pi_{v}=

\displaystyle\mathrm{NIZK}\{(\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}},\mathit{v},r,r_{1},r_{2},\varsigma_{l},\theta_{l},\varsigma_{l+V-1},\theta_{l+V-1},R_{l+V-1},S_{l+V-1},T_{l+V-1}):

\displaystyle\kappa=\tilde{\alpha}\tilde{\beta}_{1}^{\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}}}\tilde{\beta}_{2}^{\mathit{v}}\mathit{\tilde{g}}^{r}\ \land\ \phi_{V,l}[1]=\mathit{g}^{r_{1}}\ \land\ \phi_{V,l}[2]=\varsigma_{l}^{\mathit{v}}\eta_{V}^{r_{1}}\ \land\

\displaystyle\varphi_{V,l}[1]=\mathit{g}^{r_{2}}\ \land\ \varphi_{V,l}[2]=(\mathit{g}^{R})^{\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}}}\theta_{l}^{\mathit{v}}\eta_{V}^{r_{2}}\ \land\

\displaystyle\mathit{e}(\varsigma_{l},\tilde{\delta}_{V-1})=\mathit{e}(\varsigma_{l+V-1},\tilde{g})\ \land\

\displaystyle\mathit{e}(\theta_{l},\tilde{\delta}_{V-1})=\mathit{e}(\theta_{l+V-1},\tilde{g})\ \land\ \mathit{e}(R_{l+V-1},T_{l+V-1})\mathit{e}(\mathit{g},\mathit{\tilde{g}})^{-1}=1

\displaystyle\mathit{e}(R_{l+V-1},Y)\mathit{e}(S_{l+V-1},\mathit{\tilde{g}})e(\varsigma_{l+V-1},W_{1})e(\theta_{l+V-1},W_{2})\cdot\ \mathit{e}(g,Z)^{-1}=1\

Non-interactive ZK arguments computed via the Fiat-Shamir heuristic are signatures of knowledge, i.e. they can be used to sign messages. This non-interactive argument signs the payment information
\mathit{payinfo}
. Since there are group elements in the witness, the transformation described in §
A.3
is needed. We depict the argument after applying the transformation in Appendix
G
.

•:

Output a payment
\mathit{pay}\leftarrow(\kappa,\sigma^{\prime},\phi_{V,l},\varphi_{V,l},R,\pi_{v},V)
and an updated wallet
\mathit{W}^{\prime}\leftarrow(\sigma,\allowbreak\mathit{v},\allowbreak l+V)
.

\mathsf{SpendVf}(\mathit{pk},\mathit{pay},\mathit{payinfo})
.:

Execute the following steps:

•:

Parse
\mathit{pay}
as
(\kappa,\sigma^{\prime},\phi_{V,l},\varphi_{V,l},R,\pi_{v},V)
and
\sigma^{\prime}
as
(\mathit{h}^{\prime},\allowbreak s^{\prime})
. Output
0
if
\mathit{h}^{\prime}=1
or if
\mathit{e}(\mathit{h}^{\prime},\kappa)\allowbreak=\allowbreak\mathit{e}(s^{\prime},\mathit{\tilde{g}})
does not hold.

•:

Output
0
if
R\neq H^{\prime}(\mathit{payinfo})
.

•:

Verify
\pi_{v}
by using
\mathit{payinfo}
,
\mathit{pk}
,
\phi_{V,l}
,
\varphi_{V,l}
,
V
,
R
and
\kappa
. Output
0
if the proof is not correct, else output
V
.

\mathsf{Identify}(\mathit{params},\mathit{PK},\mathit{pay}_{1},\mathit{pay}_{2},\mathit{payinfo}_{1},\mathit{payinfo}_{2})
.:

Compute:

•:

Parse
\mathit{pay}_{1}
as
(\kappa_{1},\allowbreak\sigma^{\prime}_{1},\allowbreak\phi_{V_{1},l_{1},1},\allowbreak\varphi_{V_{1},l_{1},1},\allowbreak R_{1},\allowbreak\pi_{v,1},\allowbreak V_{1})
and
\mathit{pay}_{2}
as
(\kappa_{2},\allowbreak\sigma^{\prime}_{2},\allowbreak\phi_{V_{2},l_{2},2},\allowbreak\varphi_{V_{2},l_{2},2},\allowbreak R_{2},\allowbreak\pi_{v,2},\allowbreak V_{2})
.

•:

For
k\in[0,V_{1}-1]
, compute the serial numbers

\displaystyle\mathrm{SN}_{k,1}\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak\mathit{e}(\phi_{V_{1},l_{1},1}[2],\tilde{\delta}_{k})\allowbreak\mathit{e}(\phi_{V_{1},l_{1},1}[1],\tilde{\eta}_{V_{1},k}).

For
k\in[0,V_{2}-1]
, compute the serial numbers

\displaystyle\mathrm{SN}_{k,2}\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak\mathit{e}(\phi_{V_{2},l_{2},2}[2],\tilde{\delta}_{k})\allowbreak\mathit{e}(\phi_{V_{2},l_{2},2}[1],\tilde{\eta}_{V_{2},k}).

•:

Output
1
if none of the serial numbers
\mathrm{SN}_{k_{1},1}
, for
k_{1}\in[0,V_{1}-1]
, is equal to
\mathrm{SN}_{k_{2},2}
, for
k_{2}\in[0,V_{2}-1]
.

•:

Else, output
\mathit{payinfo}_{1}
if
\mathit{payinfo}_{1}=\mathit{payinfo}_{2}
.

•:

Else, let
k_{1}\in[0,V_{1}-1]
and
k_{2}\in[0,V_{2}-1]
be two indices such that
\mathrm{SN}_{k_{1},1}=\mathrm{SN}_{k_{2},2}
. Compute

\displaystyle T_{1}\leftarrow\mathit{e}(\varphi_{V_{1},l_{1},1}[2],\tilde{\delta}_{k_{1}})\mathit{e}(\varphi_{V_{1},l_{1},1}[1],\tilde{\eta}_{V_{1},k_{1}})

and

\displaystyle T_{2}\leftarrow\mathit{e}(\varphi_{V_{2},l_{2},2}[2],\tilde{\delta}_{k_{2}})\mathit{e}(\varphi_{V_{2},l_{2},2}[1],\tilde{\eta}_{V_{2},k_{2}}).

For each
\mathit{pk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}}\in\mathrm{PK}
, check whether
T_{1}T_{2}^{-1}=\mathit{e}(\mathit{pk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}},\tilde{\delta}_{k_{1}}^{R_{1}}\tilde{\delta}_{k_{2}}^{-R_{2}})
and output
\mathit{pk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}}
if the equality holds. Output
\bot
if the equality does not hold for any
\mathit{pk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}}\in\mathrm{PK}
.

5.2.3.
Security Analysis of Divisible E-Cash

In §
E
, we prove formally that
\mathrm{\Pi}_{\mathrm{EC}}
, when instantiated with the algorithms of our divisible
\mathrm{EC}
scheme, realizes
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{EC}}
. In this section, we give intuition on why our scheme is secure. The security analysis of our divisible
\mathrm{EC}
scheme is based on the security analysis given for our compact
\mathrm{EC}
scheme regarding the withdrawal phase and the non-interactive ZK argument of possession of PS signatures used in the spending phase. As in our compact
\mathrm{EC}
scheme, the anonymity property also relies on the hiding property of Pedersen commitments and the ZK property of the proof system, as well as on the method to “randomize” signatures in the spend phase. The traceability property relies on the weak simulation extractability property of the non-interactive ZK arguments of knowledge, the binding property of the commitment scheme and the existential unforgeability property of PS signatures in the RO model.

The remaining part of our analysis follows the security proof given in
(
Pointcheval et al. 2017
)
for the divisible e-cash scheme. The anonymity property holds under the
N
-MXDH’ assumption (see §
A.2
). In
(
Pointcheval et al. 2017
)
, it is shown that this assumption holds in the generic bilinear group model.

The traceability property relies on the existential unforgeability of the SPS scheme in
(
Abe et al. 2011
)
, which guarantees that the values
(\varsigma_{l+V-1},\allowbreak\theta_{l+V-1})
used as a witness in the ZK argument
\pi_{v}
are correct. It also relies on the
BDHI
assumption, which guarantees that the adversary cannot generate values
(\varsigma_{l},\allowbreak\theta_{l})
such that
l<1
. Those two properties, along with the above-mentioned binding property of the commitment scheme and the existential unforgeability property of PS signatures in the RO model, guarantee that the ElGamal encryptions
\phi_{V,l}
and
\varphi_{V,l}
in payment are computed correctly. This ensures that, if there is double-spending, an authority can identify the user that double-spent a coin.

As noted in
(
Bourse et al. 2019
)
, in an RO model version of the scheme in
(
Pointcheval et al. 2017
)
, like our scheme, we can extract the user’s secret key from the ZK argument
\pi_{v}
. Thus, we can show that an honest user cannot be found guilty of double spending under the discrete logarithm assumption. In our scheme, unlike in
(
Pointcheval et al. 2017
)
, the authority does not contribute randomness to the generation of the coin secret
\mathit{v}
. This means that, as in our compact
\mathrm{EC}
scheme, the adversary is able to generate two payments where there is no double spending, and yet there is a match between serial numbers. In that case, it is guaranteed that an honest user will not be found guilty.

As for clearance, like in our compact
\mathrm{EC}
scheme, our construction in §
4
guarantees that only the provider that receives a payment can deposit it. This is done by using an authenticated bulletin board and checking that the provider’s identity is contained in the payment information
\mathit{payinfo}
.

6.
Efficiency Analysis and Comparison

For years research has focused almost exclusively on divisible e-cash because
of the constant cost of the spending phase. However, this is achieved at the expense of much more expensive deposit and identification phases. In this section, we compare the efficiency of our compact and divisible
\mathrm{EC}
schemes. To this end, in §
F
, we describe an instantiation of our compact
\mathrm{EC}
scheme with a concrete set membership proof, and in §
G
, we describe how the NIZK arguments of our divisible
\mathrm{EC}
scheme are instantiated.
Our comparison shows that our compact EC with multiple denominations, an idea mentioned but never explored, keeps efficient deposit and identification phases, while dramatically reducing the spending phase cost as opposed to using one denomination. In fact, when the price range is not large, the concrete (as opposed to asymptotic) cost of compact EC with multiple denominations is smaller than that of divisible EC also in the spending phase. Such a comparison was not done before and can guide the choice of an EC scheme for a practical payment system.

In §
6.1
, we analyze the average number of coins that need to be spent depending on the choice of denominations. In §
6.2
, we describe the implementation of our schemes and compare their performance.

Table 1.
Average number of spent coins given
D
and
P_{max}

P_{max}

D

Avg. Num. Coins

10

[1,2,5]

1.9

100

[1,2,5,10,20,50]

3.4

1000

[1,2,5,\ldots,100,200,500]

5.1

10000

[1,2,5,\ldots,1000,2000,5000]

6.8

100000

[1,2,5,\ldots,10000,20000,50000]

8.5

1000000

[1,2,5,\ldots,10000,20000,50000]

17.5

6.1.
Choice of Multiple Denominations

The spending phase is arguably the phase in which time constraints are more demanding. Spending one coin is more efficient in our compact e-cash scheme than in our divisible e-cash scheme. However, for any real-world payment, multiple coins need to be spent, and consequently, the compact e-cash scheme is not practical.
To counter this problem, a solution that has often been suggested in the e-cash literature, but not studied in depth, is to use multiple denominations.
A question that arises when using multiple denominations is how to choose those denominations optimally, i.e., to minimize the average number of coins that need to be spent. This
optimal denomination problem
has been studied in the context of Fiat currencies
(
Shallit 2003
)
. Assuming that all the prices within a range
[1,\allowbreak P_{max}]
are equally likely, and fixing the number
D
of denominations, the problem is to find the set of denominations that minimizes the average number of spent coins.
As calculated in
(
Shallit 2003
)
, for a price range of
[1,\allowbreak 100]
cents, and the denominations
[1,\allowbreak 5,\allowbreak 10,\allowbreak 25]
, the average number of coins spent is
4.7
. However, using the optimal sets, which are
[1,\allowbreak 5,\allowbreak 18,\allowbreak 25]
and
[1,\allowbreak 5,\allowbreak 18,\allowbreak 29]
, the average is
3.89
.

In general, the optimal denomination problem is NP-hard. Given a naive approach of choosing
1
as the smallest denomination (to be able to pay for the lowest price), we must calculate the number of all possible sets of
D-1
denominations in the range
[1,\allowbreak P_{max}]
, which is given by the number of combinations without repetition
C_{D-1}(P_{max})
, and
the average number of coins needed to pay for prices in
[1,\allowbreak P_{max}]

for each set of those
D
denominations. This computation is too expensive for practical values of
D
and
P_{max}
(e.g.
D=15
and
P_{max}=1000000
cents), although it can be optimized by restricting the set of denominations to those that fulfil certain properties.

A problem with using multiple denominations in e-cash is that a user may not be able to pay a price even given enough funds. For example, a user left with two coins of 10 cents is not able to pay for a price of 11 cents. The obvious solution to this problem would be to allow the user to exchange one coin of 10 cents for 10 coins of 1 cent, but this would require interaction with authorities, turning the scheme into an online one. For the scheme to remain offline, the only solution would be for the user to withdraw coins of 1 cent denomination.
Therefore, for practical use of the compact e-cash scheme, we need an easy-to-use set of denominations.
Taking the denominations of the euro (i.e.
[1,\allowbreak 2,\allowbreak 5,\allowbreak 10,\allowbreak 20,\allowbreak 50,\allowbreak 100,\allowbreak 200,\allowbreak 500,\allowbreak 1000,\allowbreak 2000,\allowbreak 5000,\allowbreak 10000,\allowbreak 20000,\allowbreak 50000]
cents) as an example, in Table
1
we calculate the average number of coins that need to be spent for different values of
P_{max}
and
D
. Although these denominations are not optimal,
the results in
(
Shallit 2003
)
suggest that the improvement derived from using an optimal denomination set for the same values of
D
is not dramatic.
Furthermore, these denominations allow us to compute the optimal representation for a given price (i.e. the optimal number of coins of each denomination that are needed to pay), by running the simple
greedy algorithm

(
Shallit 2003
)
, whereas the optimal denomination set may not allow that. Therefore, we use those denominations to compare the efficiency of our compact e-cash with that of the divisible e-cash scheme.

6.2.
Implementation and Comparison

Implementation

We implement the protocols presented in §
5.1
and §
5.2
in
\mathsf{Rust1.56.0}
. Our implementation is open source.
2
2

2

https://github.com/nymtech/nym/tree/research/ecash
For the implementation of the elliptic curve we use a fork of
bls12_381
library and further extend it to facilitate operations in
{\mathbb{G}_{t}}
.
3
3

3

https://github.com/jstuczyn/bls12_381
All benchmarks were run on a dedicated 16 GB Linode machine, with a
2.2
GHz AMD CPU. To minimize accuracy errors, we execute each measurement 300 times and compute its average.
With this specification, a single exponentiation in
\mathbb{G}
takes approximately
531.24
us, in
\mathbb{\tilde{G}}
approximately
2.13
ms, while in
{\mathbb{G}_{t}}
approximately
3.85
ms. A single pairing operation takes
2.59
ms.

Evaluation

For our benchmarks, we set the number of authorities to
n=100
with a threshold of
t=70
. The maximum number of coins in a wallet is set to
L=100
. We also set the number of users to
100
, which determines the size of the set of public keys used as input to algorithm
\mathsf{Identify}
. We always place the target public key as the last one
PK
list.

The withdrawal protocol is the same in both our compact and divisible
\mathrm{EC}
schemes. Algorithm
\mathsf{Request}
takes on average
9.16
ms. The algorithms run by the authorities, i.e.
\mathsf{RequestVf}
and
\mathsf{Issue}
, take in total
8.64
ms on average. Algorithm
\mathsf{IssueVf}
takes
8.47
ms. We remark that to withdraw a wallet, a user needs to run
\mathsf{Request}
only once because the same request is sent to at least
t
authorities. However, algorithm
\mathsf{IssueVf}
needs to be run at least
t
times to verify each of the responses. Finally, algorithm
\mathsf{AggrWallet}
, which is run just once after
t
valid responses have been received from different authorities, takes
65.28
ms. We consider that those timings are practical, and we stress that they do not depend on the number of coins in a full wallet.

\mathrm{EC}
. For Compact
\mathrm{EC}
we measure for
V=1
.

Table
2
presents a comparison between spending a single coin in our compact
\mathrm{EC}
scheme vs spending
V
coins in our divisible
\mathrm{EC}
scheme. Algorithms
\mathsf{Spend}
and
\mathsf{SpendVf}
are almost four times faster in the compact than in the divisible
\mathrm{EC}
scheme. However, as we noted earlier, the cost of the spending phase in the divisible
\mathrm{EC}
scheme is independent of the number of coins spent, while in the compact
\mathrm{EC}
scheme, it grows linearly. As an example, let
1267
be the price to be paid. The total execution time to settle a payment using the compact
\mathrm{EC}
scheme would take
44.03
seconds, thus significantly more than in the divisible
\mathrm{EC}
scheme.

However, the spending phase of our compact e-cash scheme can be optimized as follows. First, algorithm
\mathsf{Spend}
allows us to spend
V
coins with cost smaller than the cost of running
V
times
\mathsf{Spend}
to spend one coin. Our benchmarks show that spending
V=2
takes on average
53.43
ms, which is almost
25\%
faster than executing the spend protocol twice sequentially to spend one coin. Second, as discussed in §
6.1
, we can run several instances of the scheme in parallel and assign to each of them a different denomination. For example, let’s consider a set of denominations
[1000,500,100,50,20,10,5,2,1]
. Given our price of
1267
, the spender now executes the pay function five times with value
V=1
for coins
[1000,50,10,5,2]
and once with value
V=2
for coins with denomination
100
.
Thus, the total execution time is
261.93
ms, which is significantly faster. However, in case of large payments and a limited number of denominations, the compact e-cash is still significantly inefficient. For example, given denominations
[100,50,20,10,5,2,1]
we need
425.34
ms to complete a payment of
1267
. A similar dependency can be observed in the case of payment verification.

We use our analysis from §
6.1
to estimate the average time required to complete a payment using our compact
\mathrm{EC}
scheme given different price ranges
[1,\allowbreak P_{max}]
and sets of denominations. The results are summarised in Table
3
. We observe that our compact
\mathrm{EC}
scheme is more efficient than our divisible
\mathrm{EC}
scheme for small price ranges and small sets of denominations.

The major advantage of the compact
\mathrm{EC}
scheme over the divisible
\mathrm{EC}
scheme is a fast
identification
phase. In the compact
\mathrm{EC}
scheme, to detect double-spending, an authority simply needs to compare serial numbers. In a practical setting, it is likely that double spending happens infrequently, and so the computation cost for the authority is negligible. However, in the divisible
\mathrm{EC}
scheme, the authority needs to compute the serial numbers of a payment by running
\mathsf{Identify}
before the authority can compare them with the serial numbers of other payments. The computation of a serial number involves two pairings.

Once double spending is detected, the compact
\mathrm{EC}
scheme can identify the user guilty of double spending in
1.61
ms, independently of the number of users in the system. However, in the divisible
\mathrm{EC}
scheme, the computation is more expensive and it grows linearly with the number of users in the system. We remark that the timings given in Table
2
are calculated for 100 users, but in practice this number could be orders of magnitude bigger, which makes identification in compact e-cash much more efficient than in divisible e-cash.

7.
Integration

In our schemes, deposited payments are verified by the authorities. Double-spending is detected by storing previous payments on a bulletin board and checking that serial
numbers in a deposited payment are not equal to any serial number
of previously submitted payments.
A blockchain can be used to implement the bulletin board. When a provider wishes to deposit a payment, the payment is broadcast to authorities, which could also be
n
validators in a proof-of-stake blockchain. The consensus mechanism of the blockchain is then used to agree on whether the payment is valid and, in that case, recorded in the blockchain. This allows decentralizing also the deposit phase of our protocols.

Also, note that authorities in this setting may leave and enter the system dynamically. However, we must take into account that an authority that leaves the system still possesses a valid share of the secret key, and we assume that, after leaving the system, the authority becomes corrupt. Similarly to decentralized e-cash schemes, in our schemes we assume an honest majority, i.e. if
n=2a+1
, there are at most
a
corrupt authorities. For our schemes to be secure, if
a
is the number of corrupt authorities currently in the system, and
b
is the number of authorities that have left, we need that
a+b<t
, where
t
is the threshold. Therefore, algorithm
\mathsf{KeyGenV}
needs to be run periodically to create a new public verification key
\mathit{pk}
and new key pairs
(\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{V}_{i}},\allowbreak\mathit{pk}_{\mathcal{V}_{i}})_{i\in[1,n]}
so as to ensure that
a+b<t
. This implies that e-cash expires whenever a new key is created. A time interval in which users can convert old wallets to use the new secret key can be given
(
Chaum et al. 2021
)
. Once this interval ends, e-cash expiration makes it possible to delete the payments stored for double-spending detection and reset the bulletin board, which avoids an ever-growing blockchain
(
Chawla 2022
)
. The blockchain can thus be used as a settlement layer for offline e-cash transactions.

This scheme would get the best of both worlds, that of online blockchain and offline e-cash, but further research needs to be done to specify this model formally and parameterize a real-world implementation. Threshold-issuance offline e-cash may end up solving the pressing problems of privacy and scalability of payments in both blockchain and even CBDCs as e-cash moves from theory into practice.

8.
Conclusion

In this work, we proposed the first offline anonymous e-cash scheme with threshold issuance, motivated by the concrete scalability concerns of blockchains and concerns with centralization in CBDCs
(
Bank 2023
)
. We define the ideal functionality and propose two instantiations based on an improved compact and a divisible e-cash. We have shown that our schemes realize the ideal functionality and formally prove their security. We have also implemented both schemes and compared their efficiency, showing that compact e-cash is more efficient and feasible for smaller transactions, which would naturally compose the majority of offline e-cash transactions in application scenarios such as a user-facing blockchain or CBDC where practical deployment concerns would necessitate distributed authorities.

Appendix A
Building Blocks

A.1.
Bilinear Maps

Let
\mathbb{G}
,
\mathbb{\tilde{G}}
and
{\mathbb{G}_{t}}
be groups of prime order
\mathit{p}
. A map
\mathit{e}:\mathbb{G}\times\mathbb{\tilde{G}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{G}_{t}}
must satisfy bilinearity, i.e.,
\mathit{e}(\mathit{g}^{x},\mathit{\tilde{g}}^{y})=e(\mathit{g},\mathit{\tilde{g}})^{xy}
; non-degeneracy, i.e., for all generators
\mathit{g}\in\mathbb{G}
and
\mathit{\tilde{g}}\in\mathbb{\tilde{G}}
,
\mathit{e}(\mathit{g},\mathit{\tilde{g}})
generates
{\mathbb{G}_{t}}
; and efficiency, i.e., there exists an efficient algorithm
\mathcal{G}(1^{\mathit{k}})
that outputs the pairing group setup
(\mathit{p},\mathbb{G},\mathbb{\tilde{G}},\allowbreak{\mathbb{G}_{t}},\mathit{e},\mathit{g},\mathit{\tilde{g}})
and an efficient algorithm to compute
\mathit{e}(a,b)
for any
a\in\mathbb{G}
,
b\in\mathbb{\tilde{G}}
. In type 3 pairings,
\mathbb{G}\neq\mathbb{\tilde{G}}
and there exists no efficiently computable homomorphism
f:\mathbb{\tilde{G}}\rightarrow\mathbb{G}
.

A.2.
Assumptions

We recall the assumptions that are needed to prove the security of our schemes.

Definition A.1.

[XDH and SXDH Assumptions
(
Ballard et al. 2005
)
]
Given
(\mathit{p},\allowbreak\mathbb{G},\allowbreak\mathbb{\tilde{G}},\allowbreak{\mathbb{G}_{t}},\allowbreak\mathit{e},\allowbreak\mathit{g},\allowbreak\mathit{\tilde{g}})
, the external Diffie-Hellman assumption states that the decisional Diffie-Hellman problem is intractable in
\mathbb{G}
or in
\mathbb{\tilde{G}}
. The symmetric external Diffie-Hellman assumption states that it is intractable in both
\mathbb{G}
and
\mathbb{\tilde{G}}
.

Definition A.2.

[
q
-SDH Assumption
(
Boneh and Boyen 2008
)
]
Given
(\mathit{g},\allowbreak\mathit{g}^{x},\allowbreak\mathit{g}^{x^{2}},\allowbreak\ldots,\allowbreak\mathit{g}^{x^{q}})\allowbreak\in\allowbreak\mathbb{G}^{q+1}
, the strong Diffie-Hellman assumption states that it is hard to output a pair
(m,\allowbreak\mathit{g}^{1/(x+m)})\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}\times\mathbb{G}
.

Definition A.3.

[
y
-DDHI Assumption
(
Camenisch et al. 2005
)
]
Let
\mathbb{G}
be a group of prime order
q
and let
\mathit{g}
be a generator of
\mathbb{G}
. Given
(\mathit{g},\allowbreak\mathit{g}^{x},\ldots,\allowbreak\mathit{g}^{(x^{y})},\allowbreak R)
for a random
x\leftarrow\mathbb{Z}_{p}
, the decisional Diffie-Hellman inversion assumption states that it is hard to decide if
R=\mathit{g}^{1/x}
or not.

Definition A.4.

[
N
-BDHI Assumption
(
Boneh and Boyen 2004
)
]
Given
(\mathit{p},\allowbreak\mathbb{G},\allowbreak\mathbb{\tilde{G}},\allowbreak{\mathbb{G}_{t}},\allowbreak\mathit{e},\allowbreak\mathit{g},\allowbreak\mathit{\tilde{g}})
and the tuple
(\{\mathit{g}^{y^{i}}\}_{i=0}^{N},\{\mathit{\tilde{g}}^{y^{i}}\}_{i=0}^{N})\in\mathbb{G}^{N+1}\times\mathbb{\tilde{G}}^{N+1}
, the bilinear Diffie-Hellman inversion assumption states that it is hard to compute
\mathit{e}(\mathit{g},\mathit{\tilde{g}})^{1/y}\in{\mathbb{G}_{t}}
.

Definition A.5.

[
N
-MXDH’ Assumption
(
Pointcheval et al. 2017
)
]

\forall N\in\mathbb{N^{\ast}}
, we define
C=N^{3}-N^{2}
,
S=C+1
,
E=N^{2}-N
,
D=S+E
, and
P=D+C
. Given
(\mathit{p},\mathbb{G},\mathbb{\tilde{G}},\allowbreak{\mathbb{G}_{t}},\mathit{e},\mathit{g},\mathit{\tilde{g}})
and
\{(\mathit{g}^{\gamma^{k}},\allowbreak\mathit{h}^{\gamma^{k}})_{k=0}^{P},\allowbreak(\mathit{g}^{\alpha\delta\gamma^{-k}},\allowbreak\mathit{h}^{\alpha\delta\gamma^{-k}})_{k=0}^{E},\allowbreak(\mathit{g}^{\chi\gamma^{k}},\allowbreak\mathit{h}^{\chi\gamma^{k}})_{k=D+1}^{P},\allowbreak(\mathit{g}^{\alpha\gamma^{-k}},\allowbreak\mathit{g}^{\chi\gamma^{k}/\alpha},\allowbreak\mathit{h}^{\chi\gamma^{k}/\alpha})_{k=0}^{C}\}\allowbreak\in\allowbreak\mathbb{G}^{2P+5S+2E+2}
, as well as
(\mathit{\tilde{g}}^{\gamma^{k}},\mathit{\tilde{g}}^{\alpha\gamma^{-k}})_{k=0}^{C}\in\mathbb{\tilde{G}}^{2S}
and a pair
(\mathit{g}^{z_{1}},\mathit{h}^{z_{2}})\in\mathbb{G}^{2}
, it is hard to decide whether
z_{1}=z_{2}=\delta+\chi\gamma^{D}/\alpha
or
(z_{1},\allowbreak z_{2})
is random.

Definition A.6.

[
n
-DHE
(
Camenisch et al. 2009
)
]
Let
(\mathit{p},\allowbreak\mathbb{G},\allowbreak\mathbb{\tilde{G}},\allowbreak{\mathbb{G}_{t}},\allowbreak\mathit{e},\allowbreak\mathit{g},\allowbreak\mathit{\tilde{g}})\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak\mathcal{G}(1^{k})
and
\alpha\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak\mathbb{Z}_{p}
. Given
(\mathit{p},\allowbreak\mathbb{G},\allowbreak\mathbb{\tilde{G}},\allowbreak{\mathbb{G}_{t}},\allowbreak\mathit{e},\allowbreak\mathit{g},\allowbreak\mathit{\tilde{g}})
and a tuple
(\mathit{g}_{1},\allowbreak\mathit{\tilde{g}}_{1},\allowbreak\ldots,\allowbreak\mathit{g}_{n},\allowbreak\mathit{\tilde{g}}_{n},\allowbreak\mathit{g}_{n+2},\allowbreak\ldots,\allowbreak\mathit{g}_{2n})
such that
\mathit{g}_{i}\allowbreak=\allowbreak\mathit{g}^{(\alpha^{i})}
and
\mathit{\tilde{g}}_{i}\allowbreak=\allowbreak\mathit{\tilde{g}}^{(\alpha^{i})}
, for any p.p.t. adversary
\mathcal{A}
,
\mathrm{Pr}[\mathit{g}^{(\alpha^{n+1})}\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak\mathcal{A}(\mathit{p},\allowbreak\mathbb{G},\allowbreak\mathbb{\tilde{G}},\allowbreak{\mathbb{G}_{t}},\allowbreak\mathit{e},\allowbreak\mathit{g},\allowbreak\mathit{\tilde{g}},\allowbreak\mathit{g}_{1},\allowbreak\mathit{\tilde{g}}_{1},\allowbreak\ldots,\allowbreak\mathit{g}_{n},\allowbreak\mathit{\tilde{g}}_{n},\allowbreak\mathit{g}_{n+2},\allowbreak\ldots,\allowbreak\mathit{g}_{2n})]\allowbreak\leq\allowbreak\epsilon(k)
.

A.3.
Zero-Knowledge Arguments of Knowledge

Informally speaking, a zero-knowledge argument of knowledge is a two-party protocol between a prover and a verifier with two properties. First, it should be a proof of knowledge, i.e., there should exist a knowledge extractor that extracts the secret input from a successful prover with all but negligible probability. Second, it should be zero-knowledge, i.e., for all possible verifiers there exists a simulator that, without knowledge of the secret input, yields a distribution that cannot be distinguished from the interaction with a real prover.

To express a zero-knowledge argument of knowledge, we follow the notation introduced by Camenisch and Stadler
(
Camenisch and Stadler 1997
)
, i.e., we denote as
{\mathsf{ZK}}\{(w):y=f(w)\}
a “
zero-knowledge proof of knowledge of the secret input
w
such that
y=f(w)
”, where
w
is a secret input, while
y
and the function
f
are publicly known.

Let
\mathcal{L}
be a language in NP. We can associate to any NP-language
\mathcal{L}
a polynomial time recognizable relation
\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{L}}
defining
\mathcal{L}
as
\mathcal{L}=\{x:\exists w\ \mathrm{s.t.}\ (x,w)\in\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{L}}\}
, where
|w|\leq\mathrm{poly}(|x|)
. The string
w
is called a witness for membership of
x\in\mathcal{L}
.

A protocol
\Sigma=(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{V})
for an NP-language
\mathcal{L}
is an interactive proof system. The prover
\mathcal{P}
and the verifier
\mathcal{V}
know an instance
x
of the language
\mathcal{L}
. The prover
\mathcal{P}
also knows a witness
w
for membership of
x\in\mathcal{L}
.
\Sigma
-protocols have a 3-move shape where the first message
\alpha
, called
commitment
, is sent by the prover. The second message
\beta
, called
challenge
, is chosen randomly and sent by the verifier. The last message
\gamma
, called
response
, is sent by the prover. A
\Sigma
-protocol fulfills the properties of completeness, honest-verifier zero-knowledge, and special soundness defined in Faust et al.
(
Faust et al. 2012
)
.

In our e-cash schemes, zero-knowledge arguments of knowledge based on the Fiat-Shamir transform
(
Fiat and Shamir 1987
)
are used. The Fiat-Shamir transform removes the interaction between the prover
\mathcal{P}
and the verifier
\mathcal{V}
of a
\Sigma
protocol by replacing the challenge with a hash value
H(\alpha,x)
computed by the prover, where
H
is modeled as a random oracle. (It is possible to include an additional message
m
as input to
H
, i.e.
H(\alpha,x,m)
, turning the argument of knowledge into a
signature of knowledge
of the message
m
.) An argument
\pi
consists of
(\alpha,H(\alpha,x),\gamma)
. The Fiat-Shamir system is denoted by
(\mathcal{P}^{H},\mathcal{V}^{H})
and fulfills the properties of zero-knowledge and weak simulation extractability defined in Faust et al.
(
Faust et al. 2012
)
, which we recall below.

Definition A.7 (Zero-Knowledge).

Define the zero knowledge simulator
\mathcal{S}
as follows.
\mathcal{S}
is a stateful algorithm that can operate in two modes:
(h_{i},st)\leftarrow\mathcal{S}(1,st,q_{i})
answers random oracle queries
q_{i}
, while
(\pi,st)\leftarrow\mathcal{S}(2,st,x)
outputs a simulated proof
\pi
for an instance
x
.
\mathcal{S}(1,\cdots)
and
\mathcal{S}(2,\cdots)
share the state
st
that is updated after each operation.

Let
\mathcal{L}
be a language in NP. Denote with
(\mathcal{S}_{1},\mathcal{S}_{2})
the oracles such that
\mathcal{S}_{1}(q_{i})
returns the first output of
(h_{i},st)\leftarrow\mathcal{S}(1,st,q_{i})
and
\mathcal{S}_{2}(x,w)
returns the first output of
(\pi,st)\leftarrow\mathcal{S}(2,st,x)
if
(x,w)\in\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{L}}
. A protocol
(\mathcal{P}^{H},\mathcal{V}^{H})
is a non-interactive zero-knowledge proof for the language
\mathcal{L}
in the random oracle model if there exists a ppt simulator
\mathcal{S}
such that for all ppt distinguishers
\mathcal{D}
we have

\textrm{Pr}[\mathcal{D}^{H(\cdot),\mathcal{P}^{H}(\cdot,\cdot)}(1^{k})=1]\approx\textrm{Pr}[\mathcal{D}^{\mathcal{S}_{1}(\cdot),\mathcal{S}_{2}(\cdot,\cdot)}(1^{k})=1],

where both
\mathcal{P}
and
\mathcal{S}_{2}
oracles output
\bot
if
(x,w)\notin\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{L}}
.

Definition A.8 (Weak Simulation Extractability).

Let
\mathcal{L}
be a language in NP. Consider a non-interactive zero-knowledge proof system
(\mathcal{P}^{H},\mathcal{V}^{H})
for
\mathcal{L}
with zero-knowledge simulator
\mathcal{S}
. Let
(\mathcal{S}_{1},\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{2})
be oracles returning the first output of
(h_{i},st)\leftarrow\mathcal{S}(1,st,q_{i})
and
(\pi,st)\leftarrow\mathcal{S}(2,st,x)
respectively.
(\mathcal{P}^{H},\mathcal{V}^{H})
is weakly simulation extractable with extraction error
\nu
and with respect to
\mathcal{S}
in the random oracle model, if for all ppt adversaries
\mathcal{A}
there exists an efficient algorithm
\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{A}}
with access to the answers
(\mathcal{T}_{H},\mathcal{T})
of
(\mathcal{S}_{1},\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{2})
respectively such that the following holds. Let

\displaystyle\mathrm{acc}=\textrm{Pr}[(x^{\ast},\pi^{\ast})\leftarrow\mathcal{A}^{\mathcal{S}_{1}(\cdot),\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{2}(\cdot)}(1^{k};\rho):

\displaystyle(x^{\ast},\pi^{\ast})\notin\mathcal{T};\mathcal{V}^{\mathcal{S}_{1}}(x^{\ast},\pi^{\ast})=1]

\displaystyle\mathrm{ext}=\textrm{Pr}[(x^{\ast},\pi^{\ast})\leftarrow\mathcal{A}^{\mathcal{S}_{1}(\cdot),\mathcal{S}^{\prime}_{2}(\cdot)}(1^{k};\rho);

\displaystyle\indent w^{\ast}\leftarrow\mathcal{E}_{\mathcal{A}}(x^{\ast},\pi^{\ast};\rho,\mathcal{T}_{H},\mathcal{T}):(x^{\ast},\pi^{\ast})\notin\mathcal{T};(x^{\ast},w^{\ast})\in\mathcal{R}_{\mathcal{L}}],

where the probability space in both cases is over the random choices of
\mathcal{S}
and the adversary’s random tape
\rho
. Then, there exists a constant
d>0
and a polynomial
p
such that whenever
\mathrm{acc}\geq\nu
, we have
\mathrm{ext}\geq(1/p)(\mathrm{acc}-\nu)^{d}
.

Types of proofs.

We use known results for computing ZK proofs of discrete logarithms
(
Camenisch and Stadler 1997
)
. A protocol proving knowledge of exponents
(w_{1},\allowbreak\ldots,\allowbreak w_{n})
that satisfy the formula
\phi(w_{1},\allowbreak\ldots,\allowbreak w_{n})
is described as

(1)

{\mathsf{ZK}}\{(w_{1},\ldots,w_{n}):1=\phi(w_{1},\ldots,w_{n})\}

The formula
\phi(w_{1},\ldots,\allowbreak w_{n})
consists of conjunctions and disjunctions of “atoms”. An atom expresses
group relations
, such as

\prod_{j=1}^{k}g_{j}^{f_{j}}=1

where the
g_{j}
’s are elements of prime order groups and the
f_{j}
’s are polynomials in the variables
(w_{1},\allowbreak\ldots,\allowbreak w_{n})
.

A proof system for (
1
) can be transformed into a proof system for more expressive statements about secret exponents
\mathit{sexps}
and secret bases
\mathit{sbases}
:

(2)

{\mathsf{ZK}}\{(\mathit{sexps},\mathit{sbases}):1=\phi(\mathit{sexps},\mathit{bases}\cup\mathit{sbases})\}

The transformation adds an additional base
h
to the public bases. For each
g_{j}\in\mathit{sbases}
, the transformation picks a random exponent
\rho_{j}
and computes a blinded base
g_{j}^{\prime}=g_{j}\mathit{h}^{\rho_{j}}
. The transformation adds
g^{\prime}_{j}
to the public bases
\mathit{bases}
,
\rho_{j}
to the secret exponents
\mathit{sexps}
, and rewrites
g_{j}^{f_{j}}
into
{g_{j}^{\prime}}^{f_{j}}h^{-f_{j}\rho_{j}}
.

The proof system supports pairing product equations

(3)

\prod_{j=1}^{k}e(g_{j},\tilde{g}_{j})^{f_{j}}=1

in groups of prime order with a bilinear map
\mathit{e}
, by treating the target group
{\mathbb{G}_{t}}
as the group of the proof system. The embedding for secret bases is unchanged, except for the case in which both bases in a pairing are secret. In this case,
e(g_{j},\tilde{g}_{j})^{f_{j}}
must be transformed into
e(g^{\prime}_{j},\tilde{g}^{\prime}_{j})^{f_{j}}e(g_{j}^{\prime},\tilde{h})^{-f_{j}\tilde{\rho}_{j}}e(h,\tilde{g}^{\prime}_{j})^{-f_{j}\rho_{j}}\allowbreak e(h,\tilde{h})^{f_{j}\rho_{j}\tilde{\rho}_{j}}
.

A.4.
Commitment Schemes

A commitment scheme consists of algorithms
\mathsf{CSetup}
,
\mathsf{Com}
and
\mathsf{VfCom}
. The algorithm
\mathsf{CSetup}(1^{\mathit{k}})
generates the parameters of the commitment scheme
\mathit{par_{c}}
, which include a description of the message space
\mathcal{M}
.
\mathsf{Com}(\mathit{par_{c}},\mathit{x})
outputs a commitment
\mathit{com}
to
\mathit{x}
and auxiliary information
\mathit{open}
. A commitment is opened by revealing
(\mathit{x},\allowbreak\mathit{open})
and checking whether
\mathsf{VfCom}(\mathit{par_{c}},\mathit{com},\mathit{x},\mathit{open})
outputs
1
or
0
.

A commitment scheme should fulfill the
correctness
,
hiding
and
binding
properties. We recall the definitions of those properties below.

Definition A.9 (Correctness).

Correctness requires that
\mathsf{VfCom}
accepts all commitments created by algorithm
\mathsf{Com}
, i.e., for all
\mathit{x}\in\mathcal{M}

\textrm{Pr}\left[\begin{array}[]{c}\mathit{par_{c}}\leftarrow\mathsf{CSetup}(1^{\mathit{k}});\ (\mathit{com},\mathit{open})\leftarrow\mathsf{Com}(\mathit{par_{c}},\mathit{x}):\\
1=\mathsf{VfCom}(\mathit{par_{c}},\mathit{com},\mathit{x},\mathit{open})\end{array}\right]=1~.

Definition A.10 (Hiding Property).

The hiding property ensures that a commitment
\mathit{com}
to
\mathit{x}
does not reveal any information about
\mathit{x}
. For any ppt adversary
\mathcal{A}
, the hiding property is defined as follows:

\textrm{Pr}\left[\begin{array}[]{l}\mathit{par_{c}}\leftarrow\mathsf{CSetup}(1^{\mathit{k}});\\
(\mathit{x}_{0},\mathit{x}_{1},\mathit{st})\leftarrow\mathcal{A}(\mathit{par_{c}});\\
\mathit{b}\leftarrow\{0,1\};\ (\mathit{com},\mathit{open})\leftarrow\mathsf{Com}(\mathit{par_{c}},\mathit{x}_{\mathit{b}});\\
\mathit{b}^{\prime}\leftarrow\mathcal{A}(\mathit{st},\mathit{com}):\\
\mathit{x}_{0}\in\mathcal{M}\ \land\ \mathit{x}_{1}\in\mathcal{M}\ \land\ \mathit{b}=\mathit{b}^{\prime}\end{array}\right]\leq\frac{1}{2}+\mathit{\epsilon}(\mathit{k})~.

Definition A.11 (Binding Property).

The binding property ensures that
\mathit{com}
cannot be opened to another value
\mathit{x}^{\prime}
. For any ppt adversary
\mathcal{A}
, the binding property is defined as follows:

\textrm{Pr}\left[\begin{array}[]{l}\mathit{par_{c}}\leftarrow\mathsf{CSetup}(1^{\mathit{k}});\\
(\mathit{com},\mathit{x},\mathit{open},\mathit{x}^{\prime},\mathit{open}^{\prime})\leftarrow\mathcal{A}(\mathit{par_{c}}):\\
\mathit{x}\in\mathcal{M}\ \land\ \mathit{x}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{M}\ \land\mathit{x}\neq\mathit{x}^{\prime}\\
\land\ 1=\mathsf{VfCom}(\mathit{par_{c}},\mathit{com},\mathit{x},\mathit{open})\\
\land\ 1=\mathsf{VfCom}(\mathit{par_{c}},\mathit{com},\mathit{x}^{\prime},\mathit{open}^{\prime})\end{array}\right]\leq\mathit{\epsilon}(\mathit{k})~.

Our e-cash schemes use the commitment scheme by Pedersen
(
Pedersen 1991
)
to commit to elements
x\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}
, where
p
is a prime. This commitment scheme is perfectly hiding and computationally binding under the discrete logarithm assumption. The Pedersen commitment scheme consists of the following algorithms.

\bullet

\mathsf{CSetup}(1^{k})
.:

On input the security parameter
1^{k}
, pick random generators
\mathit{g},\mathit{h}
of a group
\mathbb{G}_{p}
of prime order
\mathit{p}
. Output
\mathit{par_{c}}=(g,h,\mathcal{M})
, where
\mathcal{M}=\mathbb{Z}_{p}
.

\bullet

\mathsf{Com}(\mathit{par_{c}},x)
.:

Check that
x\in\mathcal{M}
. Pick random value
\mathit{open}\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}
, compute
\mathit{com}=\mathit{g}^{\mathit{open}}\mathit{h}^{x}
, and output
\mathit{com}
.

\bullet

\mathsf{VfCom}(\mathit{par_{c}},\mathit{com},x^{\prime},\mathit{open}^{\prime})
.:

Recompute
\mathit{com}^{\prime}=\mathit{g}^{\mathit{open}^{\prime}}\mathit{h}^{x^{\prime}}
. If
\mathit{com}\allowbreak=\allowbreak\mathit{com}^{\prime}
then output
1
else
0
.

When committing to a tuple of messages, the Pedersen commitment scheme works as follows.

\bullet

\mathsf{CSetup}(1^{\mathit{k}},l)
.:

On input the security parameter
1^{\mathit{k}}
and an upper bound
l
on the number of elements to be committed, pick
l+1
random generators
\mathit{h}_{1},\ldots,\mathit{h}_{l},\mathit{g}
of a group
\mathbb{G}_{p}
of prime order
\mathit{p}
. Output
\mathit{par_{c}}=(h_{1},\ldots,h_{l},g,\mathcal{M})
, where
\mathcal{M}=\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{l}
.

\bullet

\mathsf{Com}(\mathit{par_{c}},\langle x_{1},\ldots,x_{l}\rangle)
.:

Pick random value
\mathit{open}\leftarrow\mathbb{Z}_{p}
, compute
\mathit{com}=\mathit{g}^{\mathit{open}}\prod_{i=1}^{l}\mathit{h}_{i}^{x_{i}}
and output
\mathit{com}
.

\bullet

\mathsf{VfCom}(\mathit{par_{c}},\mathit{com},\langle x^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,x^{\prime}_{l}\rangle,\mathit{open}^{\prime})
.:

Recompute commitment
\mathit{com}^{\prime}=\mathit{g}^{\mathit{open}^{\prime}}\prod_{i=1}^{l}\mathit{h}_{i}^{x^{\prime}_{i}}
. If it is the case that
\mathit{com}=\mathit{com}^{\prime}
then output
1
else
0
.

A.5.
Signature Schemes

A signature scheme consists of the algorithms
\mathsf{KeyGen}
,
\mathsf{Sign}
, and
\mathsf{VfSig}
.
\mathsf{KeyGen}(1^{\mathit{k}})
outputs a secret key
\mathit{sk}
and a public key
\mathit{pk}
, which include a description of the message space
\mathcal{M}
.
\mathsf{Sign}(\mathit{sk},\mathit{m})
outputs a signature
\mathit{\sigma}
on message
\mathit{m}\in\mathcal{M}
.
\mathsf{VfSig}(\mathit{pk},\mathit{\sigma},\mathit{m})
outputs
1
if
\mathit{\sigma}
is a valid signature on
\mathit{m}
and
0
otherwise. This definition can be extended to blocks of messages
(\mathit{m}_{1},\ldots,\mathit{m}_{q})
. In this case,
\mathsf{KeyGen}(1^{\mathit{k}},q)
receives the maximum number of messages as input. A signature scheme must fulfill the correctness and existential unforgeability properties
(
Goldwasser et al. 1988
)
, which we recall below.

Definition A.12 (Correctness).

Correctness ensures that the algorithm
\mathsf{VfSig}
accepts the signatures created by the algorithm
\mathsf{Sign}
on input a secret key computed by algorithm
\mathsf{KeyGen}
. More formally, correctness is defined as follows.

\textrm{Pr}\left[\begin{array}[]{l}(\mathit{sk},\mathit{pk})\leftarrow\mathsf{KeyGen}(1^{\mathit{k}});\ \mathit{m}\leftarrow\mathcal{M};\\
\mathit{\sigma}\leftarrow\mathsf{Sign}(\mathit{sk},\mathit{m}):\ 1=\mathsf{VfSig}(\mathit{pk},\mathit{\sigma},\mathit{m})\end{array}\right]=1

Definition A.13 (Existential Unforgeability).

The property of existential unforgeability ensures that it is not feasible to output a signature on a message without knowledge of the secret key or of another signature on that message. Let
\mathcal{O}_{s}
be an oracle that, on input
\mathit{sk}
and a message
\mathit{m}\in\mathcal{M}
, outputs
\mathsf{Sign}(\mathit{sk},\allowbreak\mathit{m})
, and let
\mathit{S}_{s}
be a set that contains the messages sent to
\mathcal{O}_{s}
. More formally, for any ppt adversary
\mathcal{A}
, existential unforgeability is defined as follows.

\textrm{Pr}\left[\begin{array}[]{l}(\mathit{sk},\mathit{pk})\leftarrow\mathsf{KeyGen}(1^{\mathit{k}});\ (\mathit{m},\mathit{\sigma})\leftarrow\mathcal{A}(\mathit{pk})^{\mathcal{O}_{s}(\mathit{sk},\cdot)}:\\
1=\mathsf{VfSig}(\mathit{pk},\mathit{\sigma},\mathit{m})\ \land\ \mathit{m}\in\mathcal{M}\ \land\ \mathit{m}\notin\mathit{S}_{s}\\
\end{array}\right]\leq\epsilon(\mathit{k})

Pointcheval-Sanders (PS) signatures.

The PS signature scheme is defined as follows
(
Pointcheval and Sanders 2016
)
.

\mathsf{KeyGen}(1^{\mathit{k}},q)
.:

Run
\mathcal{G}(1^{\mathit{k}})
to obtain a pairing group setup
\theta=(\mathit{p},\mathbb{G},\mathbb{\tilde{G}},\allowbreak{\mathbb{G}_{t}},\mathit{e},\allowbreak\mathit{g},\allowbreak\mathit{\tilde{g}})
. Pick random secret key
(x,\allowbreak y_{1},\ldots,\allowbreak y_{q})\leftarrow\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{q+1}
. Output the secret key
\mathit{sk}=(\theta,\allowbreak x,\allowbreak y_{1},\allowbreak\ldots\allowbreak y_{q})
and the public key
\mathit{pk}=(\theta,\tilde{\alpha},\beta_{1},\tilde{\beta}_{1},\ldots,\beta_{q},\tilde{\beta}_{q})\leftarrow(\theta,\allowbreak\mathit{\tilde{g}}^{x},\allowbreak\mathit{g}^{y_{1}},\allowbreak\mathit{\tilde{g}}^{y_{1}},\allowbreak\ldots,\allowbreak\mathit{g}^{y_{q}},\allowbreak\mathit{\tilde{g}}^{y_{q}})
.

\mathsf{Sign}(\mathit{sk},\mathit{m}_{1},\ldots,\mathit{m}_{q})
.:

Parse
\mathit{sk}
as
(\theta,x,y_{1},\ldots,y_{q})
. Pick up random
r\leftarrow\mathbb{Z}_{p}
and set
h\leftarrow\mathit{g}^{r}
. Output the signature
\mathit{\sigma}=(h,s)\leftarrow(h,h^{x+y_{1}\mathit{m}_{1}+\ldots+y_{q}\mathit{m}_{q}})
.

\mathsf{VfSig}(\mathit{pk},\mathit{\sigma},\mathit{m}_{1},\ldots,\mathit{m}_{q})
.:

Output
1
if
\mathit{e}(h,\tilde{\alpha}\prod_{j=1}^{q}\tilde{\beta}_{j}^{\mathit{m}_{j}})\allowbreak=\allowbreak\mathit{e}(s,\mathit{\tilde{g}})
and
h\neq 1
. Otherwise output
0
.

This signature scheme is randomizable. To randomize a signature
\mathit{\sigma}=(h,\allowbreak s)
, pick random
r^{\prime}\leftarrow\mathbb{Z}_{p}
and compute
\mathit{\sigma}^{\prime}=(h^{r^{\prime}},s^{r^{\prime}})
. The elements
(\beta_{1},\allowbreak\ldots,\allowbreak\beta_{q})
in the public key are needed for the blind signature issuance protocol in
(
Pointcheval and Sanders 2016
)
, as well as for the issuance protocols of Coconut and of our e-cash schemes.

Pointcheval-Sanders signatures in the random oracle model.
Coconut and our e-cash schemes use a variant of PS signatures in which, in algorithm
\mathsf{Sign}
, the random generator
h
is computed via a hash function, which is modeled as a random oracle (RO). This variant has been formalized in
(
Rial and Piotrowska 2022
)
as PS signatures in the RO model.

In
(
Rial and Piotrowska 2022
)
, the syntax of algorithm
\mathsf{Sign}
is as follows.
\mathsf{Sign}
uses a random oracle
H:\mathcal{M}_{ro}\allowbreak\rightarrow\allowbreak\mathcal{S}
.
\mathsf{Sign}(\mathit{sk},\allowbreak\mathit{m}_{1},\allowbreak\ldots\allowbreak\mathit{m}_{q},\allowbreak\mathit{r},\allowbreak\mathit{st})
receives as input a secret key
\mathit{sk}
, a tuple of messages
(\mathit{m}_{1},\allowbreak\ldots,\allowbreak\mathit{m}_{q})
, a value
\mathit{r}\allowbreak\in\allowbreak\mathcal{M}_{ro}
and state information
\mathit{st}
, which stores tuples of the form
(\mathit{m}_{1},\allowbreak\ldots,\allowbreak\mathit{m}_{q},\allowbreak\mathit{r})
.
\mathsf{Sign}
outputs a signature
\mathit{\sigma}
on
(\mathit{m}_{1},\allowbreak\ldots,\allowbreak\mathit{m}_{q})
if
\mathit{st}
does not contain a tuple
(\mathit{m}^{\prime}_{1},\allowbreak\ldots,\allowbreak\mathit{m}^{\prime}_{q},\allowbreak\mathit{r}^{\prime})
such that
(\mathit{m}_{1},\allowbreak\ldots,\allowbreak\mathit{m}_{q})\neq(\mathit{m}^{\prime}_{1},\allowbreak\ldots,\allowbreak\mathit{m}^{\prime}_{q})
and
\mathit{r}=\mathit{r}^{\prime}
.
\mathsf{Sign}
also outputs updated state information
\mathit{st}^{\prime}
.

The PS signature scheme in the RO model scheme works as follows. The algorithms
\mathsf{KeyGen}
and
\mathsf{VfSig}
remain unmodified.

\mathsf{Sign}(\mathit{sk},\mathit{m}_{1},\ldots,\mathit{m}_{q},\mathit{r},\mathit{st})
.:

Parse
\mathit{sk}
as
(\theta,x,y_{1},\ldots,y_{q})
. If
\mathit{st}
contains a tuple
(\mathit{m}^{\prime}_{1},\allowbreak\ldots,\allowbreak\mathit{m}^{\prime}_{q},\allowbreak\mathit{r}^{\prime})
such that
(\mathit{m}_{1},\allowbreak\ldots,\allowbreak\mathit{m}_{q})\neq(\mathit{m}^{\prime}_{1},\allowbreak\ldots,\allowbreak\mathit{m}^{\prime}_{q})
and
\mathit{r}=\mathit{r}^{\prime}
, output
\mathit{\sigma}=\bot
and
\mathit{st}^{\prime}=\mathit{st}
. Otherwise compute
\mathit{h}\leftarrow H(\mathit{r})
and output the signature
\mathit{\sigma}=(h,s)\leftarrow(h,h^{x+y_{1}\mathit{m}_{1}+\ldots+y_{q}\mathit{m}_{q}})
and the updated state information
\mathit{st}^{\prime}=\mathit{st}\cup\{(\mathit{m}_{1},\allowbreak\ldots,\allowbreak\mathit{m}_{q},\mathit{r})\}
.

We recall the definition of the existential unforgeability property in the RO model below.

Definition A.14 (Existential Unforgeability in the RO
(
Rial and Piotrowska 2022
)
).

For any ppt adversary
\mathcal{A}
, existential unforgeability in the RO model is defined as follows.

\textrm{Pr}\left[\begin{array}[]{l}(\mathit{sk},\mathit{pk})\leftarrow\mathsf{KeyGen}(1^{\mathit{k}});\\
(\mathit{m},\mathit{\sigma})\leftarrow\mathcal{A}(\mathit{pk})^{\mathcal{O}_{s}(\mathit{sk},\cdot,\cdot),H(\cdot)}:\\
1=\mathsf{VfSig}(\mathit{pk},\mathit{\sigma},\mathit{m})\ \land\ \mathit{m}\in\mathcal{M}\ \land\ \mathit{m}\notin\mathit{S}_{s}\\
\end{array}\right]\leq\epsilon(\mathit{k})

\mathcal{O}_{s}(\mathit{sk},\cdot,\cdot)
works as follows. On input
\mathit{sk}
, a message
\mathit{m}=(\mathit{m}_{1},\allowbreak\ldots,\allowbreak\mathit{m}_{q})
and the value
\mathit{r}
,
\mathcal{O}_{s}
runs
(\mathit{\sigma},\mathit{st}^{\prime})\leftarrow\mathsf{Sign}(\mathit{sk},\mathit{m}_{1},\ldots,\mathit{m}_{q},\mathit{r},\mathit{st})
.
\mathcal{O}_{s}
replaces
\mathit{st}
by
\mathit{st}^{\prime}
and returns
\mathit{\sigma}
to
\mathcal{A}
. (
\mathit{st}
is empty in the first invocation of
\mathcal{O}_{s}
.)
\mathit{S}_{s}
is a set that contains the messages sent to
\mathcal{O}_{s}
.

In comparison to the definition of existential unforgeability (see Definition
A.13
),
\mathcal{A}
has access to the random oracle
H
, and the signing oracle is modified to follow the new syntax. The PS scheme in the RO model is existentially unforgeable under the generalized PS assumption proposed in
(
Kim et al. 2021a
;
Kim et al. 2021b
)
.

Structure-Preserving Signature (SPS) scheme.
In a SPS scheme, the public key, the messages, and the signatures are group elements in
\mathbb{G}
and
\mathbb{\tilde{G}}
, and verification must consist purely in the checking of pairing product equations. Our divisible e-cash scheme uses the SPS scheme in
(
Abe et al. 2011
)
. In this SPS scheme,
a
elements in
\mathbb{G}
and
b
elements in
\mathbb{\tilde{G}}
are signed.

\mathsf{KeyGen}(\mathit{grp},a,b)
.:

Let
\mathit{grp}\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak(\mathit{p},\allowbreak\mathbb{G},\allowbreak\mathbb{\tilde{G}},\allowbreak{\mathbb{G}_{t}},\allowbreak\mathit{e},\allowbreak\mathit{g},\allowbreak\mathit{\tilde{g}})
be the bilinear map parameters. Pick at random
u_{1},\allowbreak\dots,\allowbreak u_{b},\allowbreak y,\allowbreak w_{1},\dots\allowbreak w_{a},\allowbreak z\leftarrow\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{*}
and compute
U_{i}\allowbreak=\allowbreak g^{u_{i}}
,
i\allowbreak\in\allowbreak[1..b]
,
Y\allowbreak=\allowbreak\mathit{\tilde{g}}^{y}
,
W_{i}\allowbreak=\allowbreak\mathit{\tilde{g}}^{w_{i}}
,
i\allowbreak\in\allowbreak[1..a]
and
Z\allowbreak=\allowbreak\mathit{\tilde{g}}^{z}
. Return the verification key
\mathit{pk}\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak(\mathit{grp},\allowbreak U_{1},\allowbreak\dots,\allowbreak U_{b},\allowbreak Y,\allowbreak W_{1},\allowbreak\dots,\allowbreak W_{a},\allowbreak Z)
and the signing key
\mathit{sk}\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak(\mathit{pk},\allowbreak u_{1},\allowbreak\dots,\allowbreak u_{b},\allowbreak y,\allowbreak w_{1},\allowbreak\dots,\allowbreak w_{a},\allowbreak z)
.

\mathsf{Sign}(\mathit{sk},\langle m_{1},\dots,m_{a+b}\rangle)
.:

Pick
r\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{*}
, and set

\displaystyle R\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak\mathit{g}^{r},\quad S\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak\mathit{g}^{z-ry}\prod_{i=1}^{a}m_{i}^{-w_{i}},\quad T\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak(\mathit{\tilde{g}}\prod_{i=1}^{b}\allowbreak m_{a+i}^{-u_{i}})^{1/r},

and output the signature
\mathit{\sigma}\leftarrow(R,\allowbreak S,\allowbreak T)
.

\mathsf{VfSig}(\mathit{pk},\mathit{\sigma},\langle m_{1},\dots,m_{a+b}\rangle)
.:

Output
1
if it is satisfied that

\mathit{e}(R,Y)\allowbreak\mathit{e}(S,\mathit{\tilde{g}})\prod_{i=1}^{a}\mathit{e}(m_{i},\allowbreak W_{i})\allowbreak=\allowbreak\mathit{e}(g,\allowbreak Z)

and

\mathit{e}(R,T)\prod_{i=1}^{b}\mathit{e}(U_{i},m_{a+i})\allowbreak=\allowbreak\mathit{e}(\mathit{g},\allowbreak\mathit{\tilde{g}})

A.6.
Pseudorandom Functions

Pseudorandom functions (PF)
(
Goldreich et al. 1986
;
Boyle et al. 2014
)
are a family of indexed
functions
F=\{F_{s}\}
such that: (1) given the index
s
,
F_{s}
can be efficiently evaluated on all inputs; (2) no
probabilistic polynomial-time algorithm without
s
can distinguish evaluations
F_{s}(x_{i})
for inputs
x_{i}
of its choice from random values.
We recall the definition of pseudorandom functions in
(
Boyle et al. 2014
)
.

Definition A.15 (Pseudorandom Function Family).

A family of functions
\mathcal{F}=\{F_{s}\}_{s\in S}
, indexed by a set
S
, and where
F_{s}:D\rightarrow R
for all
s
, is a pseudorandom function (PRF) family if for a randomly chosen
s
, and all PPT
\mathcal{A}
, the distinguishing advantage
\Pr_{s\leftarrow S}[\mathcal{A}^{f_{s}(\cdot)}\allowbreak=\allowbreak 1]\allowbreak-\Pr_{f\leftarrow(D\rightarrow R)}[\mathcal{A}^{f(\cdot)}=1]
is negligible, where
(D\rightarrow R)
denotes the set of all functions from
D
to
R
.

Our compact e-cash scheme uses the PF in
(
Camenisch et al. 2005
)
, which works as follows. For every
n
, a function
f
is defined by the tuple
(\mathbb{G},\mathit{p},\mathit{g},s)
, where
\mathbb{G}
is a group of order
\mathit{p}
,
\mathit{p}
is an
n
-bit prime,
\mathit{g}
is a generator of
\mathbb{G}
, and
s
is a seed in
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
. For any input
x\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}
(except for
x=-1\ \mathrm{mod}\ \mathit{p}
), the function
f_{\mathbb{G},\mathit{p},\mathit{g},s}(\cdot)
, which we denote as
f_{\mathit{g},s}
for fixed values of
(\mathbb{G},\mathit{p},\mathit{g})
, is defined as
f_{\mathit{g},s}(x)=\mathit{g}^{1/(s+x+1)}
. This PF is secure under the
y
-DDHI assumption in
\mathbb{G}
, which we recall in §
A.2
. This PF is based on the verifiable random function in
(
Dodis and Yampolskiy 2005
)
, which is secure under the
y
-DBDHI assumption.

4
, we summarize the notation used in our paper.
