Appendix B
Ideal-World/Real-World Paradigm

The security of a protocol
\varphi
is analyzed by comparing the view of an environment
\mathcal{Z}
in a real execution of
\varphi
against that of
\mathcal{Z}
in the ideal protocol defined in
\mathcal{F}_{\varphi}
.
\mathcal{Z}
chooses the inputs of the parties and collects their outputs. In the real world,
\mathcal{Z}
can communicate freely with an adversary
\mathcal{A}
who controls both the network and any corrupt parties.
In the ideal world,
\mathcal{Z}
interacts with dummy parties, who simply relay inputs and outputs between
\mathcal{Z}
and
\mathcal{F}_{\varphi}
, and a simulator
\mathcal{S}
.
We say that a protocol
\varphi
securely realizes
\mathcal{F}_{\varphi}
if
\mathcal{Z}
cannot distinguish the real world from the ideal world, i.e.,
\mathcal{Z}
cannot distinguish whether it is interacting with
\mathcal{A}
and parties running protocol
\varphi
or with
\mathcal{S}
and dummy parties relaying to
\mathcal{F}_{\varphi}

A protocol
\varphi^{\mathcal{G}}
securely realizes
\mathcal{F}
in the
\mathcal{G}
-hybrid model when
\varphi
is allowed to invoke the ideal functionality
\mathcal{G}
. Therefore, for any protocol
\psi
that securely realizes
\mathcal{G}
, the composed protocol
\varphi^{\psi}
, which is obtained by replacing each invocation of an instance of
\mathcal{G}
with an invocation of an instance of
\psi
, securely realizes
\mathcal{F}
.

In the ideal functionalities described in this paper, we consider static corruptions. When describing ideal functionalities, we use the conventions introduced in
(
Camenisch et al. 2016
)
, which are summarised
in
B
.

Interface Naming Convention.:

An ideal functionality can be invoked by using one or more interfaces. The name of a message in an interface consists of three fields separated by dots, e.g.,
\mathsf{ec.setup.ini}
in
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{EC}}
in §
3.3
.
The first field indicates the name of the functionality and is the same in all interfaces of the functionality.
This field is useful for distinguishing between invocations of different functionalities in a hybrid protocol that uses two or more different functionalities.
The second field indicates the kind of action performed by the functionality and is the same in all messages that the functionality exchanges within the same interface.
The third field distinguishes between the messages that belong to the same interface, and can take the following different values.
A message
\mathsf{ec.setup.ini}
is the incoming message received by the functionality, i.e., the message through which the interface is invoked.
A message
\mathsf{ec.setup.end}
is the outgoing message sent by the functionality, i.e., the message that ends the execution of the interface.
The message
\mathsf{ec.setup.sim}
is used by the functionality to send a message to
\mathcal{S}
, and the message
\mathsf{ec.setup.rep}
is used to receive a message from
\mathcal{S}
.

Network vs local communication.:

The identity of an interactive Turing machine instance (ITI) consists of a party identifier
\mathit{pid}
and a session identifier
\mathit{sid}
. A set of parties in an execution of a system of interactive Turing machines is a protocol instance if they have the same session identifier
\mathit{sid}
.
ITIs can pass direct inputs to and outputs from “local” ITIs that have the same
\mathit{pid}
.
An ideal functionality
\mathcal{F}
has
\mathit{pid}=\bot
and is considered local to all parties. An instance of
\mathcal{F}
with the session identifier
\mathit{sid}
only accepts inputs from and passes outputs to machines with the same session identifier
\mathit{sid}
. Some functionalities require the session identifier to have some structure. Those functionalities check whether the session identifier possesses the required structure in the first message that invokes the functionality. For the subsequent messages, the functionality implicitly checks that the session identifier equals the session identifier used in the first message.
Communication between ITIs with different party identifiers must take place over the network.
The network is controlled by
\mathcal{A}
, meaning that he can arbitrarily delay, modify, drop, or insert messages.

Query identifiers.:

Some interfaces in a functionality can be invoked more than once. When the functionality sends a message
\mathsf{ec.setup.sim}
to
\mathcal{S}
in such an interface, a query identifier
\mathit{qid}
is included in the message. The query identifier must also be included in the response
\mathsf{ec.setup.rep}
sent by
\mathcal{S}
. The query identifier is used to identify the message
\mathsf{ec.setup.sim}
to which
\mathcal{S}
replies with a message
\mathsf{ec.setup.rep}
. We note that, typically,
\mathcal{S}
in the security proof may not be able to provide an immediate answer to the functionality after receiving a message
\mathsf{ec.setup.sim}
. The reason is that
\mathcal{S}
typically needs to interact with the copy of
\mathcal{A}
it runs in order to produce the message
\mathsf{ec.setup.rep}
, but
\mathcal{A}
may not provide the desired answer or may provide a delayed answer. In such cases, when the functionality sends more than one message
\mathsf{ec.setup.sim}
to
\mathcal{S}
,
\mathcal{S}
may provide delayed replies, and the order of those replies may not follow the order of the messages received.

Aborts.:

When an ideal functionality
\mathcal{F}
aborts after being activated with a message sent by a party, we mean that
\mathcal{F}
halts the execution of its program and sends a special abortion message to the party that invoked the functionality. When an ideal functionality
\mathcal{F}
aborts after being activated with a message sent by
\mathcal{S}
, we mean that
\mathcal{F}
halts the execution of its program and sends a special abortion message to the party that receives the outgoing message from
\mathcal{F}
after
\mathcal{F}
is activated by
\mathcal{S}
.

Appendix C
Definitions of Ideal Functionalities

C.1.
Secure Message Transmission

Our e-cash schemes use the functionality
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{SMT}}
for secure message transmission described in
(
Canetti 2001
)
.
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{SMT}}
interacts with a sender
\mathcal{T}
and a receiver
\mathcal{R}
, and consists of one interface
\mathsf{smt.send}
.
\mathcal{T}
uses the
\mathsf{smt.send}
interface to send a message
\mathit{m}
to
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{SMT}}
.
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{SMT}}
leaks
\mathit{l}(\mathit{m})
, where
\mathit{l}:\mathcal{M}\rightarrow\mathbb{N}
is a function that leaks the message length, to the simulator
\mathcal{S}
. After receiving a response from
\mathcal{S}
,
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{SMT}}
sends
\mathit{m}
to
\mathcal{R}
.
\mathcal{S}
cannot modify
\mathit{m}
. The session identifier
\mathit{sid}
contains the identities of
\mathcal{T}
and
\mathcal{R}
.

Ideal Functionality
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{SMT}}
.

\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{SMT}}
is parameterized by a message space
\mathcal{M}
and by a leakage function
\mathit{l}:\mathcal{M}\rightarrow\mathbb{N}
, which leaks the message length.

(1)

On input
(\mathsf{smt.send.ini},\allowbreak\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{m})
from a party
\mathcal{T}
:

•

Abort if
\mathit{sid}\neq(\mathcal{T},\mathcal{R},\mathit{sid}^{\prime})
or if
\mathit{m}\notin\mathcal{M}
.

•

Create a fresh
\mathit{qid}
and store
(\mathit{qid},\mathcal{R},\mathit{m})
.

•

Send
(\mathsf{smt.send.sim},\allowbreak\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{qid},\allowbreak\mathit{l}(\mathit{m}))
to
\mathcal{S}
.

S.

On input
(\mathsf{smt.send.rep},\allowbreak\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{qid})
from
\mathcal{S}
:

•

Abort if
(\mathit{qid},\mathcal{R},\mathit{m})
is not stored.

•

Delete the record
(\mathit{qid},\mathcal{R},\mathit{m})
.

•

Send
(\mathsf{smt.send.end},\allowbreak\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{m})
to
\mathcal{R}
.

C.2.
Key Genearation

In our e-cash constructions, the setup phase generates the parameters
\mathit{params}
through algorithm
\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\mathit{k}},\allowbreak L)
. Moreover, algorithm
\mathsf{KeyGenV}(\mathit{params},\allowbreak t,\allowbreak n)
generates a key pair for the Pointcheval-Sanders signature scheme in such a way that the shares of the secret key are given to
n
authorities, so that
t\allowbreak\leq\allowbreak n
authorities are needed to produce a signature.

To simplify our security analysis, in a manner similar to
(
Rial and Piotrowska 2022
)
, we define an ideal functionality
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{KG}}
that runs both algorithms. In our construction in §
4
,
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{KG}}
gives
\mathit{params}
and the public keys
\mathit{pk}
and
(\mathit{pk}_{\mathcal{V}_{i}})_{i\in[1,n]}
to any party running the protocol, while each authority
\mathcal{V}_{i}
also receives his secret key
\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{V}_{i}}
.

\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{KG}}
could be replaced by an ideal functionality for distributed key generation (DKG)
(
Kate et al. 2012
;
Groth 2021
)
. DKG would avoid the need of a trusted party to generate the keys. With that replacement, our constructions would realize a modified version of our functionality in §
3.3
, where authorities cannot finalize the execution of the setup interface without involvement of other authorities. The security analysis of the remaining phases of our e-cash schemes is not affected by the fact that the authorities keys are generated by a trusted party or through a DKG protocol.

\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{KG}}
interacts with
n
authorities
(\mathcal{A}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{A}_{n})
.
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{KG}}
consists of two interfaces
\mathsf{kg.getkey}
and
\mathsf{kg.retrieve}
. The interface
\mathsf{kg.getkey}
is used by
\mathcal{A}_{i}
to obtain its public key
\mathit{pk}_{\mathcal{V}_{i}}
and secret key
\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{V}_{i}}
, as well as the public key
\mathit{pk}
and the parameters
\mathit{params}
. The interface
\mathsf{kg.retrieve}
is used by any party
\mathcal{P}
to obtain
\mathit{pk}
,
\mathit{params}
and the public keys
\langle\mathit{pk}_{\mathcal{V}_{i}}\rangle_{i=1}^{n}
of each of the authorities.

Ideal Functionality
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{KG}}
.

\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{KG}}
is parameterized by probabilistic algorithms
\mathsf{Setup}
and
\mathsf{KeyGenV}
, a security parameter
1^{\mathit{k}}
, a threshold
t
and a number
L
of coins in a wallet.

(1)

On input
(\mathsf{kg.getkey.ini},\mathit{sid})
from an authority
\mathcal{A}_{i}
:

•

Abort if
\mathit{sid}\neq(\mathcal{A}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{A}_{n},\mathit{sid}^{\prime})
, or if
n<t
.

•

If
(\mathit{sid},\mathit{params},\allowbreak\mathit{pk},\allowbreak\langle\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{V}_{i}},\allowbreak\mathit{pk}_{\mathcal{V}_{i}}\rangle_{i\in[1,n]})
is not stored, run
\mathit{params}\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\mathit{k}},\allowbreak\allowbreak L)
and
(\mathit{pk},\allowbreak\langle\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{V}_{i}},\allowbreak\mathit{pk}_{\mathcal{V}_{i}}\rangle_{i\in[1,n]})\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak\mathsf{KeyGenV}(\mathit{params},\allowbreak t,\allowbreak n)
and store
(\mathit{sid},\mathit{params},\allowbreak\mathit{pk},\allowbreak\langle\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{V}_{i}},\allowbreak\mathit{pk}_{\mathcal{V}_{i}}\rangle_{i\in[1,n]})
.

•

Create a fresh
\mathit{qid}
and store
(\mathit{qid},\allowbreak\mathcal{A}_{i})
.

•

Send
(\mathsf{kg.getkey.sim},\allowbreak\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{qid},\allowbreak\mathit{params},\allowbreak\mathit{pk},\allowbreak\langle\mathit{pk}_{\mathcal{V}_{i}}\rangle_{i\in[1,n]})
to
\mathcal{S}
.

S.

On input
(\mathsf{kg.getkey.rep},\allowbreak\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{qid})
from the simulator
\mathcal{S}
:

•

Abort if
(\mathit{qid},\allowbreak\mathcal{A}_{i})
such that
\mathit{qid}\allowbreak\neq\allowbreak\mathit{qid}^{\prime}
is not stored.

•

Delete
(\mathit{qid},\allowbreak\mathcal{A}_{i})
.

•

Send
(\mathsf{kg.getkey.end},\allowbreak\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{params},\allowbreak\mathit{pk},\allowbreak\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{V}_{i}},\allowbreak\mathit{pk}_{\mathcal{V}_{i}})
to
\mathcal{A}_{i}
.

(2)

On input
(\mathsf{kg.retrieve.ini},\allowbreak\mathit{sid})
from any party
\mathcal{P}
:

•

Abort if
\mathit{sid}\neq(\mathcal{A}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{A}_{n},\mathit{sid}^{\prime})
.

•

If
(\mathit{sid},\mathit{params},\allowbreak\mathit{pk},\allowbreak\langle\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{V}_{i}},\allowbreak\mathit{pk}_{\mathcal{V}_{i}}\rangle_{i\in[1,n]})
is stored, set
\mathit{v}\leftarrow(\mathit{params},\allowbreak\mathit{pk},\allowbreak\langle\mathit{pk}_{\mathcal{V}_{i}}\rangle_{i\in[1,n]})
, else set
\mathit{v}\leftarrow\bot
.

•

Create a fresh
\mathit{qid}
and store
(\mathit{qid},\mathcal{P},\mathit{v})
.

•

Send
(\mathsf{kg.retrieve.sim},\allowbreak\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{qid},\allowbreak\mathit{v})
to
\mathcal{S}
.

S.

On input
(\mathsf{kg.retrieve.rep},\allowbreak\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{qid})
from
\mathcal{S}
:

•

Abort if
(\mathit{qid}^{\prime},\mathcal{P},\mathit{v})
such that
\mathit{qid}^{\prime}\neq\mathit{qid}
is not stored.

•

Delete the record
(\mathit{qid},\mathcal{P},\mathit{v})
.

•

Send
(\mathsf{kg.retrieve.end},\allowbreak\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{v})
to
\mathcal{P}
.

C.3.
Registration

Our protocol uses the functionality
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{REG}}
for key registration by Canetti
(
Canetti 2001
)
.
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{REG}}
interacts with any party
\mathcal{T}
that registers a message
\mathit{v}
and with any party
\mathcal{P}
that retrieves the registered message.
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{REG}}
consists of two interfaces
\mathsf{reg.register}
and
\mathsf{reg.retrieve}
. The interface
\mathsf{reg.register}
is used by
\mathcal{T}
to register a message
\mathit{v}
with
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{REG}}
. A party
\mathcal{P}
uses
\mathsf{reg.retrieve}
to retrieve
\mathit{v}
from
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{REG}}
.

Ideal Functionality
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{REG}}
.

\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{REG}}
is parameterized by a message space
\mathcal{M}
.

(1)

On input
(\mathsf{reg.register.ini},\allowbreak\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{v})
from a party
\mathcal{T}
:

•

Abort if
\mathit{sid}\neq(\mathcal{T},\mathit{sid}^{\prime})
, or if
\mathit{v}\notin\mathcal{M}
or if there is a tuple
(\mathit{sid},\mathit{v}^{\prime},0)
stored.

•

Store
(\mathit{sid},\mathit{v},0)
.

•

Send
(\mathsf{reg.register.sim},\allowbreak\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{v})
to
\mathcal{S}
.

S.

On input
(\mathsf{reg.register.rep},\allowbreak\mathit{sid})
from the simulator
\mathcal{S}
:

•

Abort if
(\mathit{sid},\mathit{v},0)
is not stored or if
(\mathit{sid},\mathit{v},1)
is already stored.

•

Store
(\mathit{sid},\mathit{v},1)
and parse
\mathit{sid}
as
(\mathcal{T},\mathit{sid}^{\prime})
.

•

Send
(\mathsf{reg.register.end},\allowbreak\mathit{sid})
to
\mathcal{T}
.

(2)

On input
(\mathsf{reg.retrieve.ini},\allowbreak\mathit{sid})
from any party
\mathcal{P}
:

•

If
(\mathit{sid},\mathit{v},1)
is stored, set
\mathit{v}^{\prime}\leftarrow\mathit{v}
; else set
\mathit{v}^{\prime}\leftarrow\bot
.

•

Create a fresh
\mathit{qid}
and store
(\mathit{qid},\mathcal{P},\mathit{v}^{\prime})
.

•

Send
(\mathsf{reg.retrieve.sim},\mathit{sid},\mathit{qid},\mathit{v}^{\prime})
to
\mathcal{S}
.

S.

On input
(\mathsf{reg.retrieve.rep},\mathit{sid},\mathit{qid})
from
\mathcal{S}
:

•

Abort if
(\mathit{qid},\mathcal{P},\mathit{v}^{\prime})
is not stored.

•

Delete the record
(\mathit{qid},\mathcal{P},\mathit{v}^{\prime})
.

•

Send
(\mathsf{reg.retrieve.end},\mathit{sid},\mathit{v}^{\prime})
to
\mathcal{P}
.

C.4.
Pseudonymous Channel

Our e-cash schemes use the functionality
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{NYM}}
for a secure idealized pseudonymous channel. We use
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{NYM}}
to describe our e-cash schemes for simplicity, in order to hide the details of real-world pseudonymous channels.
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{NYM}}
is similar to the functionality for anonymous secure message transmission in
(
Camenisch et al. 2014
)
.
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{NYM}}
interacts with senders
\mathcal{T}
and receivers
\mathcal{R}
.
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{NYM}}
is parameterized by a message space
\mathcal{M}
, a security parameter
\mathit{k}
, a universe of pseudonyms
\mathbb{U}_{p}
, and a leakage function
\mathit{l}
, which leaks the message length.
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{NYM}}
consists of one interfaces
\mathsf{nym.send}
.
\mathcal{T}
uses the
\mathsf{nym.send}
interface to send a message
\mathit{m}\in\mathcal{M}
, a pseudonym
\mathit{P}\in\mathbb{U}_{p}
and a receiver identifier
\mathcal{R}
to
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{NYM}}
.
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{NYM}}
sends
\mathit{l}(\mathit{m})
to the simulator
\mathcal{S}
. After receiving a response from
\mathcal{S}
,
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{NYM}}
sends
\mathit{m}
and
\mathit{P}
to
\mathcal{R}
.

\mathcal{R}
does not learn the identifier
\mathcal{T}
. Instead
\mathcal{R}
learns a pseudonym
\mathit{P}
chosen by
\mathcal{T}
.
\mathcal{T}
can choose different pseudonyms to make the messages sent unlinkable towards
\mathcal{R}
.

Ideal Functionality
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{NYM}}
.

\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{NYM}}
is parameterized by a message space
\mathcal{M}
, a security parameter
\mathit{k}
, a universe of pseudonyms
\mathbb{U}_{p}
, and a leakage function
\mathit{l}
, which leaks the message length.

(1)

On input
(\mathsf{nym.send.ini},\allowbreak\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{m},\allowbreak\mathit{P},\allowbreak\mathcal{R})
from
\mathcal{T}
:

•

Abort if
\mathit{m}\allowbreak\notin\allowbreak\mathcal{M}
, or if
\mathit{P}\allowbreak\notin\allowbreak\mathbb{U}_{p}
.

•

Create a fresh
\mathit{qid}
and store
(\mathit{qid},\allowbreak\mathit{P},\allowbreak\mathcal{T},\allowbreak\mathit{m},\allowbreak\mathcal{R})
.

•

Send
(\mathsf{nym.send.sim},\allowbreak\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{qid},\allowbreak\mathit{l}(\mathit{m}))
to
\mathcal{S}
.

S.

On input
(\mathsf{nym.send.rep},\allowbreak\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{qid})
from
\mathcal{S}
:

•

Abort if
(\mathit{qid}^{\prime},\allowbreak\mathit{P},\allowbreak\mathcal{T},\allowbreak\mathit{m},\allowbreak\mathcal{R})
such that
\mathit{qid}=\mathit{qid}^{\prime}
is not stored.

•

Delete the record
(\mathit{qid},\allowbreak\mathit{P},\allowbreak\mathcal{T},\allowbreak\mathit{m},\allowbreak\mathcal{R})
.

•

Send
(\mathsf{nym.send.end},\allowbreak\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{m},\allowbreak\mathit{P})
to
\mathcal{R}
.

C.5.
Authenticated Bulletin Board

Our e-cash schemes use the functionality
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{BB}}
for an authenticated bulletin board
BB

(
Wikström 2004
)
. A
BB
is used to store the payments deposited by providers, and to verify that there are not double spendings or double deposits.
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{BB}}
interacts with writers
\mathcal{W}_{j}
and readers
\mathcal{R}_{k}
.
\mathcal{W}_{j}
uses the
\mathsf{bb.write}
interface to send a message
\mathit{m}
to
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{BB}}
.
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{BB}}
increments a counter
\mathit{ct}
of the number of messages stored in
BB
and appends
[\mathit{ct},\allowbreak\mathcal{W}_{j},\allowbreak\mathit{m}]
to
BB
.
\mathcal{R}_{k}
uses the
\mathsf{bb.read}
interface on input an index
\mathit{i}
. If
\mathit{i}\allowbreak\in\allowbreak[1,\allowbreak\mathit{ct}]
,
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{BB}}
takes the tuple
[\mathit{i},\allowbreak\mathcal{W}_{j},\allowbreak\mathit{m}]
in
BB
and sends
(\mathcal{W}_{j},\allowbreak\mathit{m})
to
\mathcal{R}_{k}
.

Ideal Functionality
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{BB}}
.

\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{BB}}
is parameterized by a universe of messages
\mathbb{U}_{m}
.
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{BB}}
interacts with writers
\mathcal{W}_{j}
and readers
\mathcal{R}_{k}
.

(1)

On input
(\mathsf{bb.write.ini},\allowbreak\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{m})
from
\mathcal{W}_{j}
:

•

Abort if
\mathit{m}\allowbreak\notin\allowbreak\mathbb{U}_{m}
.

•

Create a fresh
\mathit{qid}
and store
(\mathit{qid},\allowbreak\mathcal{W}_{j},\allowbreak\mathit{m})
.

•

Send
(\mathsf{bb.write.sim},\allowbreak\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{qid})
to
\mathcal{S}
.

S.

On input
(\mathsf{bb.write.rep},\allowbreak\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{qid})
from
\mathcal{S}
:

•

Abort if
(\mathit{qid}^{\prime},\allowbreak\mathcal{W}_{j},\allowbreak\mathit{m})
such that
\mathit{qid}^{\prime}\allowbreak=\allowbreak\mathit{qid}
is not stored.

•

If
(\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\textsf{BB},\allowbreak\mathit{ct})
is not stored, set
\textsf{BB}\leftarrow\bot
and
\mathit{ct}\leftarrow 0
.

•

Increment
\mathit{ct}
, append
[\mathit{ct},\allowbreak\mathcal{W}_{j},\allowbreak\mathit{m}]
to
BB
and update
\mathit{ct}
and
BB
in
(\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\textsf{BB},\allowbreak\mathit{ct})
.

•

Delete
(\mathit{qid},\allowbreak\mathcal{W}_{j},\allowbreak\mathit{m})
.

•

Send
(\mathsf{bb.write.end},\allowbreak\mathit{sid})
to
\mathcal{W}_{j}
.

(2)

On input
(\mathsf{bb.read.ini},\allowbreak\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{i})
from
\mathcal{R}_{k}
:

•

Create a fresh
\mathit{qid}
and store
(\mathit{qid},\allowbreak\mathcal{R}_{k},\allowbreak\mathit{i})
.

•

Send
(\mathsf{bb.read.sim},\allowbreak\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{qid})
to
\mathcal{S}
.

S.

On input
(\mathsf{bb.read.rep},\allowbreak\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{qid})
from
\mathcal{S}
:

•

Abort if
(\mathit{qid}^{\prime},\mathcal{R}_{k},\allowbreak\mathit{i})
such that
\mathit{qid}^{\prime}=\mathit{qid}
is not stored.

•

If
(\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\textsf{BB},\allowbreak\mathit{ct})
is stored and
\mathit{i}\in[1,\mathit{ct}]
, take
[\mathit{i},\allowbreak\mathcal{W}_{j},\allowbreak\mathit{m}]
from
BB
and set
\mathit{m}^{\prime}\leftarrow(\mathcal{W}_{j},\allowbreak\mathit{m})
, else set
\mathit{m}^{\prime}\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak\bot
.

•

Send
(\mathsf{bb.read.end},\allowbreak\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{m}^{\prime})
to
\mathcal{R}_{k}
.

Appendix D
Security Proof for Our Compact E-Cash Scheme

To prove that construction
\mathrm{\Pi}_{\mathrm{EC}}
, instantiated with the algorithms of the compact e-cash scheme in §
5.1
, securely realizes the ideal functionality
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{EC}}
, we have to show that for any environment
\mathcal{Z}
and any adversary
\mathcal{A}
there exists a simulator
\mathcal{S}
such that
\mathcal{Z}
cannot distinguish between whether it is interacting with
\mathcal{A}
and the protocol in the real world or with
\mathcal{S}
and
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{EC}}
. The simulator thereby plays the role of all honest parties in the real world and interacts with
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{EC}}
for all corrupt parties in the ideal world.

\mathcal{S}
runs a copy of any adversary
\mathcal{A}
, which is used to provide to
\mathcal{Z}
a view that is indistinguishable from the view given by
\mathcal{A}
in the real world. To achieve that,
\mathcal{S}
must simulate the real-world protocol towards the copy of
\mathcal{A}
, in such a way that
\mathcal{A}
cannot distinguish an interaction with
\mathcal{S}
from an interaction with the real-world protocol.
\mathcal{S}
uses the information provided by
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{EC}}
to provide a simulation of the real-world protocol.

Our simulator
\mathcal{S}
runs copies of the functionalities
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{SMT}}
,
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{NYM}}
,
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{KG}}
,
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{REG}}
and
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{BB}}
. When any of the copies of these functionalities aborts,
\mathcal{S}
implicitly forwards the abortion message to the adversary if the functionality sends the abortion message to a corrupt party.

\mathcal{S}
also runs copies of the extractors
\mathcal{E}_{s}
and
\mathcal{E}_{v}
and simulators
\mathcal{S}_{s}
and
\mathcal{S}_{v}
for the non-interactive zero-knowledge arguments of knowledge
\pi_{s}
and
\pi_{v}
, which are computed through the Fiat-Shamir transform. We remark that they involve calls to the random oracle and rewinding of the adversary. For simplicity, we omit those details.

Challenges in the proof.

Our compact e-cash scheme with threshold issuance is based on an existing compact e-cash scheme
(
Camenisch et al. 2005
)
. We instantiate that scheme with the Pointcheval-Sanders signature scheme, and then we use an existing threshold issuance protocol for Pointcheval-Sanders signatures
(
Rial and Piotrowska 2022
)
to provide threshold issuance. Nevertheless, the security proof does not follow straightforwardly from the proofs in
(
Camenisch et al. 2005
;
Rial and Piotrowska 2022
)
.

First, in the blind issuance protocol of the compact e-cash scheme in
(
Camenisch et al. 2005
)
one of the coin secrets is chosen jointly between the user and the bank, i.e. both the bank and the user add randomness to compute the secret. In a threshold issuance setting, it would be necessary that all the authorities add the same randomness without communicating between them. While adding a mechanism to do that is possible, in order to improve efficiency we decided to have the secret be chosen by the user only. This implies that, when a corrupt user picks up the secrets of two wallets, the user could pick up the secrets in such a way that two coins have the same serial number. Consequently, when those coins are spent, there is a situation in which double spending is detected (because serial numbers are the same), although no double spending has happened. In that case, we prove that the identification algorithm cannot output the public key of an honest user, and we are able to do that under the discrete logarithm assumption.

Second, to improve efficiency, we use only one coin secret instead of the two coins secrets used in [14]. When using two coin secrets, it is straightforward to show that serial numbers and double spending tags do not reveal any information about the secrets by using the pseudorandomness property of the pseudorandom function. However, when using the same secret for both, but a different generator, we need to add an additional reduction to the XDH assumption.

Simulator.

We describe the simulator
\mathcal{S}
for the case in which a subset of users
\mathcal{U}_{j}
, a subset of providers
\mathcal{P}_{k}
and up to
t-1
authorities are corrupt.
\mathcal{S}
simulates the honest parties in the protocol
\mathrm{\Pi_{\mathrm{EC}}}
and runs copies of the ideal functionalities involved.

Honest authority
\mathcal{V}_{i}
starts setup.:

When the functionality
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{EC}}
sends the message
(\mathsf{ec.setup.sim},\allowbreak\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathcal{V}_{i})
, the simulator
\mathcal{S}
runs a copy of the functionality
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{KG}}
on input
(\mathsf{kg.getkey.ini},\allowbreak\mathit{sid})

,
\mathcal{S}
forwards that message to
\mathcal{A}
.

Honest authority
\mathcal{V}_{i}
ends setup.:

When the adversary
\mathcal{A}
sends the message
(\mathsf{kg.getkey.rep},\allowbreak\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{qid})

\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{KG}}
on input that message. When

Honest user sends a request to honest authority.:

(\mathsf{ec.request.sim},\allowbreak\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{qid},\allowbreak\mathcal{U}_{j},\allowbreak\mathcal{V}_{i})

\mathit{sid}
as
(\mathcal{V}_{1},\allowbreak\ldots,\allowbreak\mathcal{V}_{n},\allowbreak\mathit{sid}^{\prime})
, sets

and sends
(\mathsf{smt.send.sim},\allowbreak\mathit{sid}_{\mathrm{SMT}},\mathit{qid},\allowbreak\mathit{l}(\mathit{m}))

\mathcal{A}
, where

Adversary forwards request.:

When the adversary
\mathcal{A}
sends the message
(\mathsf{smt.send.rep},\allowbreak\mathit{sid}_{\mathrm{SMT}},\allowbreak\mathit{qid})
to reply to
(\mathsf{ec.request.sim},\allowbreak\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{qid},\allowbreak\mathcal{U}_{j},\allowbreak\mathcal{V}_{i})

checks if a tuple
(\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathcal{U}_{j},\allowbreak\mathcal{V}_{i})
is stored. If not,
\mathcal{S}
runs the procedure in Figure
3
. After that,
\mathcal{S}
sends the message
(\mathsf{ec.request.rep},\allowbreak\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{qid})
to
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{EC}}

Honest user sends a request to corrupt authority.:

When
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{EC}}
sends
(\mathsf{ec.request.sim},\allowbreak\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{qid},\allowbreak\mathcal{U}_{j},\allowbreak\mathcal{V}_{i},\allowbreak\mathit{wid})
,

checks if a tuple
(\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathcal{U}^{\prime}_{j},\allowbreak\mathit{wid}^{\prime},\allowbreak\mathit{wn})
such that
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}_{j}\allowbreak=\allowbreak\mathcal{U}_{j}
and
\mathit{wid}^{\prime}\allowbreak=\allowbreak\mathit{wid}
is stored. If not,
\mathcal{S}
does the following:

•:

Pick up the stored tuple
(\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathcal{U}^{\prime}_{j},\allowbreak\mathit{wct})
such that
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}_{j}\allowbreak=\allowbreak\mathcal{U}_{j}
. (This tuple is initialized to
\mathit{wct}\allowbreak=\allowbreak 0
.)

•:

Set
\mathit{wct}\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak\mathit{wct}\allowbreak+\allowbreak 1
.

•:

Set
\mathit{wn}\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak\mathit{wct}
.

•:

Store
(\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathcal{U}_{j},\allowbreak\mathit{wid},\allowbreak\mathit{wn})
.

•:

Update
\mathit{wct}
in the tuple
(\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathcal{U}_{j},\allowbreak\mathit{wct})
.

\mathcal{S}
parses the session identifier
\mathit{sid}
as
(\mathcal{V}_{1},\allowbreak\ldots,\allowbreak\mathcal{V}_{n},\allowbreak\mathit{sid}^{\prime})
, sets
\mathit{sid}_{\mathrm{SMT}}\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak(\mathcal{U}_{j},\mathcal{V}_{i},\mathit{sid}^{\prime})
and sends
(\mathsf{smt.send.sim},\allowbreak\mathit{sid}_{\mathrm{SMT}},\mathit{qid},\allowbreak\mathit{l}(\mathit{m}))
to
\mathcal{A}
, where
\mathit{l}(\mathit{m})
is equal to the length of the message that the honest user sends to an authority in the
\mathsf{ec.request}
interface.

Corrupt authority receives request from honest user.:

When
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{EC}}
sends
(\mathsf{ec.request.end},\allowbreak\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathcal{U}_{j},\allowbreak\mathit{reqid})
to a corrupt authority
\tilde{\mathcal{V}}_{i}
,
\mathcal{S}
retrieves the stored tuple
(\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathcal{U}_{j},\allowbreak\mathit{wid},\allowbreak\mathit{wn})
such that
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}_{j}\allowbreak=\allowbreak\mathcal{U}_{j}
and
\mathit{wid}
was received before in the message
(\mathsf{ec.request.sim},\ldots)
associated with the message
(\mathsf{ec.request.end},\ldots)
received now.
\mathcal{S}
runs a copy of a user on input
(\mathsf{ec.request.ini},\allowbreak\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\tilde{\mathcal{V}}_{i},\allowbreak\mathit{reqid},\allowbreak\mathit{wn})
. (We remark that
\mathit{wn}
used a input to the copy of the user is not necessarily equal to the value of
\mathit{wn}
received by the honest user from the environment. However, it is enough to ensure that the same
\mathit{wn}
is used for each
\mathit{wid}
received from
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{EC}}
, so that the copy of the user produces the same request for each
\mathit{wid}
.) When the copy of the user runs algorithm
\mathsf{Request}
,
\mathcal{S}
uses the public key
\mathit{pk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}}
that was previously computed for user
\mathcal{U}_{j}
.
\mathcal{S}
also uses the simulator
\mathcal{S}_{s}
to compute a simulated proof
\pi_{s}
, and sets
\mathit{com}
,
\mathit{com}_{1}
and
\mathit{com}_{2}
to be commitments to random messages. When the copy of the user sends the message
(\mathsf{smt.send.ini},\allowbreak\mathit{sid}_{\mathrm{SMT}},\allowbreak\langle\mathit{reqid},\allowbreak\mathit{req}\rangle)
to
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{SMT}}
,
\mathcal{S}
runs
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{SMT}}
on input that message. When
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{SMT}}
sends the message
(\mathsf{smt.send.sim},\allowbreak\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{qid},\allowbreak\mathit{l}(\mathit{m}))
,
\mathcal{S}
runs
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{SMT}}
on input
(\mathsf{smt.send.rep},\allowbreak\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{qid})
. When
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{SMT}}
sends
(\mathsf{smt.send.end},\allowbreak\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\langle\mathit{reqid},\allowbreak\mathit{req}\rangle)
,
\mathcal{S}
forwards that message to
\tilde{\mathcal{V}}_{i}
.

Procedure: Retrieve user key

•:

The simulator
\mathcal{S}
sets
\mathit{sid}_{\mathrm{REG}}\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak(\mathcal{U}_{j},\allowbreak\mathit{sid}^{\prime})
and runs the functionality
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{REG}}
on input
(\mathsf{reg.retrieve.ini},\allowbreak\mathit{sid}_{\mathrm{REG}})
. When
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{REG}}
sends

(\mathsf{reg.retrieve.sim},\allowbreak\mathit{sid}_{\mathrm{REG}},\allowbreak\mathit{qid},\allowbreak\mathit{v}^{\prime})
,
\mathcal{S}
forwards that message to
\mathcal{A}
.

•:

When
\mathcal{A}
sends
(\mathsf{reg.retrieve.rep},\allowbreak\mathit{sid}_{\mathrm{REG}},\allowbreak\mathit{qid})
,
\mathcal{S}
runs
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{REG}}
on input that message. When
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{REG}}
sends
(\mathsf{reg.retrieve.end},\allowbreak\mathit{sid}_{\mathrm{REG}},\allowbreak\mathit{v}^{\prime})
, if
\mathit{v}^{\prime}=\bot
,
\mathcal{S}
aborts, else
\mathcal{S}
stores
(\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathcal{U}_{j},\allowbreak\mathcal{V}_{i})
.

Figure 3.
Procedure for simulating user key retrieval.

Honest authority receives request from corrupt user.:

When

the adversary
\mathcal{A}
sends
(\mathsf{smt.send.rep},\allowbreak\mathit{sid}_{\mathrm{SMT}},\allowbreak\mathit{qid})
,
\mathcal{S}
runs
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{SMT}}
on input that message. When
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{SMT}}
sends the message
(\mathsf{smt.send.end},\allowbreak\mathit{sid}_{\mathrm{SMT}},\allowbreak\mathit{m})
,
\mathcal{S}
parses the message
\mathit{m}
as
\langle\mathit{reqid},\allowbreak\mathit{req}\rangle
and
\mathit{sid}_{\mathrm{SMT}}
as
(\mathcal{U}_{j},\allowbreak\mathcal{V}_{i},\allowbreak\mathit{sid}^{\prime})
.
\mathcal{S}
does the following:

•:

Abort if the authority
\mathcal{V}_{i}
did not end the setup.

•:

Check if a tuple
(\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathcal{U}_{j},\allowbreak\mathcal{V}_{i})
is stored. If not,
\mathcal{S}
runs the procedure in Figure
3
.

•:

Abort if there is a stored tuple
(\mathcal{U}^{\prime}_{j},\allowbreak\mathit{wn},\allowbreak\mathbb{R},\allowbreak\mathit{req},\allowbreak\langle\mathit{m}_{1},\allowbreak\mathit{m}_{2},\allowbreak o,\allowbreak o_{1},\allowbreak o_{2}\rangle)
such that
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}_{j}\allowbreak=\allowbreak\mathcal{U}_{j}
and
\{\mathit{reqid},\mathcal{V}_{i}\}\allowbreak\in\allowbreak\mathbb{R}
. Here
\mathcal{S}
aborts because there is a request pending with the same request identifier
\mathit{reqid}
from
\mathcal{U}_{j}
for
\mathcal{V}_{i}
, like it is done in the real protocol.

•:

If there is a stored tuple
(\mathcal{U}^{\prime}_{j},\allowbreak\mathit{wn},\allowbreak\mathbb{R},\allowbreak\mathit{req}^{\prime},\allowbreak\langle\mathit{m}_{1},\allowbreak\mathit{m}_{2},\allowbreak o,\allowbreak o_{1},\allowbreak o_{2}\rangle)
such that
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}_{j}\allowbreak=\allowbreak\mathcal{U}_{j}
and
\mathit{req}^{\prime}\allowbreak=\allowbreak\mathit{req}
, then update the set
\mathbb{R}\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak\mathbb{R}\cup\{\mathit{reqid},\mathcal{V}_{i}\}
in that tuple and take
\mathit{wn}
from that tuple. Else do the following:

–:

Follow the steps of an honest authority in
\mathrm{\Pi}_{\mathrm{EC}}
to verify
\mathit{req}
. This involves verifying the request by running the algorithm
b\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak\mathsf{RequestVf}(\mathit{params},\allowbreak\mathit{req},\allowbreak\mathit{pk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}})
. (
\mathcal{S}
checked before that the corrupt user registered
\mathit{pk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}}
through the procedure in Figure
3
.) Abort if
b\allowbreak=\allowbreak 0
.

–:

Parse
\mathit{req}
as
(\mathit{h},\allowbreak\mathit{com},\allowbreak\mathit{com}_{1},\allowbreak\mathit{com}_{2},\allowbreak\pi_{s})
. Run the extractor
\mathcal{E}_{s}
to extract the witness
\langle\mathit{m}_{1},\allowbreak\mathit{m}_{2},\allowbreak o,\allowbreak o_{1},\allowbreak o_{2}\rangle
from
\pi_{s}
.

–:

If there is a tuple stored
(\mathcal{U}_{j},\allowbreak\mathit{wn},\allowbreak\mathbb{R},\allowbreak\langle\mathit{h},\allowbreak\mathit{com}^{\prime},\allowbreak\mathit{com}_{1},\allowbreak\mathit{com}_{2},\allowbreak\pi_{s}\rangle,\langle\mathit{m}^{\prime}_{1},\allowbreak\mathit{m}^{\prime}_{2},\allowbreak o,\allowbreak o_{1},\allowbreak o_{2}\rangle)
such that
\mathit{com}^{\prime}\allowbreak=\allowbreak\mathit{com}
but
(\mathit{m}^{\prime}_{1},\allowbreak\mathit{m}^{\prime}_{2})\allowbreak\neq\allowbreak(\mathit{m}_{1},\allowbreak\mathit{m}_{2})
,
\mathcal{S}
outputs failure.

–:

If there is a stored tuple
(\mathcal{U}_{j},\allowbreak\mathit{wn},\allowbreak\mathbb{R},\allowbreak\mathit{req},\allowbreak\langle\mathit{m}_{1},\allowbreak\mathit{m}_{2},\allowbreak o,\allowbreak o_{1},\allowbreak o_{2}\rangle)
such that
\mathit{req}\allowbreak=\allowbreak\allowbreak\langle\mathit{h},\allowbreak\mathit{com}^{\prime},\allowbreak\mathit{com}_{1},\allowbreak\mathit{com}_{2},\allowbreak\pi_{s}\rangle
and
\mathit{com}^{\prime}\allowbreak=\allowbreak\mathit{com}
, then take
\mathit{wn}
from that tuple and store a tuple
(\mathcal{U}_{j},\allowbreak\mathit{wn},\allowbreak\mathbb{R},\allowbreak\mathit{req},\allowbreak\langle\mathit{m}_{1},\allowbreak\mathit{m}_{2},\allowbreak o,\allowbreak o_{1},\allowbreak o_{2}\rangle)
, where
\mathbb{R}\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak\{\mathit{reqid},\allowbreak\mathcal{V}_{i}\}
and
\langle\mathit{m}_{1},\allowbreak\mathit{m}_{2},\allowbreak o,\allowbreak o_{1},\allowbreak o_{2}\rangle
is the witness that was output by the extractor
\mathcal{E}_{s}
. (We remark that
\mathit{wn}
is the same because, if
\mathit{com}
is the same in two requests, the partial signatures obtained from two different authorities can be aggregated, even if the values
(\mathit{com}_{1},\allowbreak\mathit{com}_{2},\allowbreak\allowbreak\pi_{s})
are different.) Else do the following:

*:

Pick up the stored tuple
(\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathcal{U}^{\prime}_{j},\allowbreak\mathit{wct})
such that
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}_{j}\allowbreak=\allowbreak\mathcal{U}_{j}
. (This tuple is initialized to
\mathit{wct}\allowbreak=\allowbreak 0
.)

*:

Set
\mathit{wct}\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak\mathit{wct}\allowbreak+\allowbreak 1
.

*:

Set
\mathit{wn}\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak\mathit{wct}
.

*:

Store
(\mathcal{U}_{j},\allowbreak\mathit{wn},\allowbreak\mathbb{R},\allowbreak\mathit{req},\allowbreak\langle\mathit{m}_{1},\allowbreak\mathit{m}_{2},\allowbreak o,\allowbreak o_{1},\allowbreak o_{2}\rangle)
, where
\mathbb{R}\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak\{\mathit{reqid},\mathcal{V}_{i}\}
.

*:

Update
\mathit{wct}
in the tuple
(\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathcal{U}_{j},\allowbreak\mathit{wct})
.

\mathcal{S}
sends
(\mathsf{ec.request.ini},\allowbreak\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathcal{V}_{i},\allowbreak\mathit{reqid},\allowbreak\mathit{wn})
to
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{EC}}
. When

sends
(\mathsf{ec.request.sim},\allowbreak\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{qid},\allowbreak\mathcal{U}_{j},\allowbreak\mathcal{V}_{i})
,
\mathcal{S}
sends the message
(\mathsf{ec.request.rep},\allowbreak\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{qid})
to
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{EC}}
.

Honest authority issues attribute.:

When the functionality
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{EC}}
sends the message
(\mathsf{ec.issue.sim},\allowbreak\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{qid},\allowbreak\mathcal{V}_{i},\allowbreak\mathcal{U}_{j})
,
\mathcal{S}
parses
\mathit{sid}
as
(\mathcal{V}_{1},\allowbreak\ldots,\allowbreak\mathcal{V}_{n},\allowbreak\mathit{sid}^{\prime})
, sets

and sends the message

Corrupt user receives issuance.:

When
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{EC}}
sends the message
(\mathsf{ec.issue.end},\allowbreak\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{reqid},\allowbreak\mathcal{V}_{i})
to a corrupt user
\mathcal{U}_{j}
, the simulator
\mathcal{S}
finds the stored tuple
(\mathcal{U}^{\prime}_{j},\allowbreak\mathit{wn},\allowbreak\mathbb{R},\allowbreak\mathit{req},\allowbreak\langle\mathit{m}_{1},\allowbreak\mathit{m}_{2},\allowbreak o,\allowbreak o_{1},\allowbreak o_{2}\rangle)
such that
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}_{j}\allowbreak=\allowbreak\mathcal{U}_{j}
and there is element
\{\mathit{reqid}^{\prime},\allowbreak\mathcal{V}^{\prime}_{i}\}\allowbreak\in\allowbreak\mathbb{R}
such that
\mathit{reqid}^{\prime}\allowbreak=\allowbreak\mathit{reqid}
and
\mathcal{V}^{\prime}_{i}\allowbreak=\allowbreak\mathcal{V}_{i}
.
\mathcal{S}
parses
\mathit{req}
as
(\mathit{h},\allowbreak\mathit{com},\allowbreak\mathit{com}_{1},\allowbreak\mathit{com}_{2},\allowbreak\pi_{s})
.
\mathcal{S}
parses the secret key
\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{V}_{i}}
as
(x_{i},\allowbreak y_{i,1},\allowbreak y_{i,2})
, computes
c=\mathit{h}^{x_{i}}\prod_{j=1}^{2}\mathit{com}_{j}^{y_{i,j}}
and sets the blinded signature share
\hat{\sigma}_{i}\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak(\mathit{h},\allowbreak c)
as in
\mathrm{\Pi}_{\mathrm{EC}}
. If a tuple
(\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathcal{U}^{\prime}_{j},\allowbreak\mathit{wn}^{\prime},\allowbreak\mathit{\mathbb{S}})
such that
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}_{j}\allowbreak=\allowbreak\mathcal{U}_{j}
and
\mathit{wn}^{\prime}\allowbreak=\allowbreak\mathit{wn}
is not stored,
\mathcal{S}
stores a tuple
(\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathcal{U}_{j},\allowbreak\mathit{wn},\allowbreak\{i\})
, else updates
\mathit{\mathbb{S}}\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak\mathit{\mathbb{S}}\cup\{i\}
in the tuple
(\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathcal{U}_{j},\allowbreak\mathit{wn},\allowbreak\mathit{\mathbb{S}})
. After that,
\mathcal{S}
removes
\{\mathit{reqid},\allowbreak\mathcal{V}_{i}\}
from the set
\mathbb{R}
in the tuple
(\mathcal{U}_{j},\allowbreak\mathit{wn},\allowbreak\mathbb{R},\allowbreak\mathit{req},\allowbreak\langle\mathit{m}_{1},\allowbreak\mathit{m}_{2},\allowbreak o,\allowbreak o_{1},\allowbreak o_{2}\rangle)
. Finally,
\mathcal{S}
sets
\mathit{sid}_{\mathrm{SMT}}\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak(\mathcal{V}_{i},\allowbreak\mathcal{U}_{j},\allowbreak\mathit{sid}^{\prime})
, sets
\mathit{res}\leftarrow\hat{\sigma}_{i}
and sends
(\mathsf{smt.send.end},\allowbreak\mathit{sid}_{\mathrm{SMT}},\allowbreak\langle\mathit{reqid},\allowbreak\mathit{res}\rangle)
to
\mathcal{A}
.

Corrupt authority issues attribute.:

When a corrupt authority

Honest user receives issuance from corrupt authority.:

After the adversary
\mathcal{A}
sends the message

\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{SMT}}

as
(\mathcal{V}_{i},\allowbreak\mathcal{U}_{j},\allowbreak\mathit{sid}^{\prime})

\mathcal{U}_{j}

\mathit{reqid}

(\mathsf{ec.issue.end},\allowbreak\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{reqid},\allowbreak\mathcal{V}_{i})

Honest user begins spending.:

When
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{EC}}
sends
(\mathsf{ec.spend.sim},\allowbreak\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{qid})

to
\mathcal{A}
, where
\mathit{l}(\mathit{m})
is the length of the message
\langle\mathit{pay},\allowbreak\mathit{payinfo}\rangle
sent by an honest user in the spend interface. Here we consider that the length does not change depending on the number of spent coins. This can be achieved by setting a maximum number of coins to be spent and sending always messages whose length is the length of the message when the maximum number of coins is spent.

Adversary forwards spending.:

When
\mathcal{A}
sends
(\mathsf{nym.send.rep},\allowbreak\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{qid})

by running the procedure in Figure

.

Honest provider receives spending from corrupt user.:

\mathcal{A}

. If

\mathcal{S}
runs the procedure in Figure
5
to check the payment and store a tuple
(\mathit{pay},\allowbreak\mathit{payinfo},\langle\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}},\allowbreak\mathit{v},\allowbreak r,\allowbreak o_{c},\allowbreak\langle l_{k},\allowbreak o_{a_{k}},\allowbreak\mu_{k},\allowbreak o_{\mu_{k}}\rangle_{k=0}^{V-1}\rangle)
. After that,
\mathcal{S}
checks that the algorithm for identification of double spenders identifies the double spender properly by running the procedure in Figure
6
.

\mathcal{S}
sets
\mathit{\mathbb{D}}\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak\{l_{k}|k\in[0,V-1]\}
and sends
(\mathsf{ec.spend.ini},\allowbreak\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{wn},\allowbreak V,\allowbreak\mathit{\mathbb{D}},\allowbreak\mathit{payinfo},\allowbreak\mathit{P},\allowbreak\mathcal{P}_{k})
to

. When the functionality
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{EC}}
sends the message

,
\mathcal{S}

(\mathsf{ec.spend.rep},\allowbreak\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{qid})
to

.

Honest provider begins deposit of honest user
\mathcal{U}_{j}
payment.:

If there are not corrupt authorities,
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{EC}}
sends
(\mathsf{ec.deposit.sim},\allowbreak\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{qid})
and
\mathcal{S}
picks up random
\mathit{payinfo}\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak\mathit{U_{info}}
and
V\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak[1,L]
. If there is at least one corrupt authority,
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{EC}}
sends the message
(\mathsf{ec.deposit.sim},\allowbreak\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{qid},\allowbreak V,\allowbreak\mathit{payinfo})
.

After receiving the message from
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{EC}}
,
\mathcal{S}
runs the procedure in Figure
4
to simulate an honest user payment
\mathit{pay}
.
\mathcal{S}
runs a copy of
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{BB}}
on input
(\mathsf{bb.write.ini},\allowbreak\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\langle\mathit{pay},\allowbreak\mathit{payinfo}\rangle)

sends
(\mathsf{bb.write.sim},\allowbreak\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{qid})
,
\mathcal{S}
forwards that message to
\mathcal{A}
.

Honest provider starts deposit of corrupt user
\mathcal{U}_{j}
payment.:

When
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{EC}}
sends the message
(\mathsf{ec.deposit.sim},\allowbreak\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{qid},\allowbreak\mathcal{U}_{j},\allowbreak\mathit{wn},\allowbreak V,\allowbreak\mathit{\mathbb{D}},\allowbreak\mathit{payinfo},\allowbreak\mathit{P},\allowbreak\mathcal{P}_{k})
,
\mathcal{S}
finds the tuple
(\mathit{pay},\allowbreak\mathit{payinfo}^{\prime},\langle\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}},\allowbreak\mathit{v},\allowbreak r,\allowbreak o_{c},\allowbreak\langle l_{k},\allowbreak o_{a_{k}},\allowbreak\mu_{k},\allowbreak o_{\mu_{k}}\rangle_{k=0}^{V-1}\rangle)
, where
\mathit{pay}
is
(\kappa,\allowbreak\sigma^{\prime},\allowbreak\langle S_{k},\allowbreak T_{k},\allowbreak A_{k}\rangle_{k\in[0,V^{\prime}-1]},\allowbreak V^{\prime},\allowbreak C,\allowbreak\pi_{v})
, such that
\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}}
is the secret key corresponding to the public key registered by the corrupt user
\mathcal{U}_{j}
,
\mathit{v}
is the serial number associated with the wallet
\mathit{wn}
,
V^{\prime}\allowbreak=\allowbreak V
,
\langle l_{k}\rangle_{k\in[0,V-1]}=\mathit{\mathbb{D}}
, and
\mathit{payinfo}^{\prime}\allowbreak=\allowbreak\mathit{payinfo}
.
\mathcal{S}
runs
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{BB}}
on input
(\mathsf{bb.write.ini},\allowbreak\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\langle\mathit{pay},\allowbreak\mathit{payinfo}\rangle)
. When
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{BB}}
sends
(\mathsf{bb.write.sim},\allowbreak\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{qid})
,
\mathcal{S}
forwards that message to
\mathcal{A}
. We remark that
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{EC}}
sends
(\mathsf{ec.deposit.sim},\allowbreak\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{qid},\allowbreak\mathcal{U}_{j},\allowbreak\mathit{wn},\allowbreak V,\allowbreak\mathit{\mathbb{D}},\allowbreak\mathit{payinfo},\allowbreak\mathit{P},\allowbreak\mathcal{P}_{k})
instead
(\mathsf{ec.deposit.sim},\allowbreak\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{qid})
when the deposit corresponds to a payment made by a corrupt user and when at least one authority is corrupt. When all the authorities are honest, the adversary in the real world does not have access to the information in
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{BB}}
, and thus the simulator does not need to learn from
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{EC}}
whether a payment made by a corrupt user has been deposited.

Honest provider ends deposit.:

When the adversary
\mathcal{A}
sends the message
(\mathsf{bb.write.rep},\allowbreak\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{qid})
,
\mathcal{S}
runs
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{BB}}
on input that message. When
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{BB}}
sends
(\mathsf{bb.write.end},\allowbreak\mathit{sid})
,
\mathcal{S}
sends
(\mathsf{ec.deposit.rep},\allowbreak\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{qid})
to
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{EC}}
.

Procedure: Simulate honest user payment

•:

Pick random
r\leftarrow\mathbb{Z}_{p}
and
r^{\prime}\leftarrow\mathbb{Z}_{p}
.

•:

Compute
\sigma^{\prime}=(\mathit{h}^{\prime},s^{\prime})\leftarrow(\mathit{g}^{r^{\prime}},\mathit{g}^{rr^{\prime}})
.

•:

Compute
\kappa\leftarrow\mathit{\tilde{g}}^{r}
.

•:

For
k\allowbreak\in\allowbreak[0,\allowbreak V-1]
, pick random
S_{k}\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak\mathbb{G}
,
T_{k}\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak\mathbb{G}
and
A_{k}\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak\mathbb{G}
.

•:

Pick random
C\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak\mathbb{G}
.

•:

Run the simulator
\mathcal{S}_{v}
to compute a simulated proof
\pi_{v}
.

Figure 4.
Procedure for simulating an honest user payment.

Procedure: Check Corrupt User Payment

•:

\mathcal{S}
parses
\mathit{pay}
as
(\kappa,\allowbreak\sigma^{\prime},\allowbreak\langle S_{k},\allowbreak T_{k},\allowbreak A_{k}\rangle_{k\in[0,V-1]},\allowbreak V,\allowbreak C,\allowbreak\pi_{v})
.

•:

\mathcal{S}
runs the extractor
\mathcal{E}_{v}
to extract the witness
(\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}},\allowbreak\mathit{v},\allowbreak r,\allowbreak o_{c},\allowbreak\langle l_{k},\allowbreak o_{a_{k}},\allowbreak\mu_{k},\allowbreak o_{\mu_{k}}\rangle_{k=0}^{V-1})
from the proof
\pi_{v}
.

•:

\mathcal{S}
parses
\sigma^{\prime}
as
(\mathit{h}^{\prime},\allowbreak s^{\prime})
and computes
\hat{\sigma}\allowbreak=\allowbreak(\hat{\mathit{h}},\allowbreak\hat{s})\allowbreak=\allowbreak(\mathit{h}^{\prime},\allowbreak s^{\prime}(\mathit{h}^{\prime})^{-r})
.

•:

\mathcal{S}
runs the verification equation
\mathit{e}(\hat{\mathit{h}},\allowbreak\tilde{\alpha}\allowbreak\tilde{\beta}_{1}^{\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}}}\tilde{\beta}_{2}^{\mathit{v}})\allowbreak=\allowbreak\mathit{e}(\hat{s},\allowbreak\mathit{\tilde{g}})
of the Pointcheval-Sanders signature scheme. If the verification equation does not hold,
\mathcal{S}
outputs failure.

•:

\mathcal{S}
finds a tuple
(\mathcal{U}_{j},\allowbreak\mathit{wn},\allowbreak\mathbb{R},\allowbreak\langle\mathit{h},\allowbreak\mathit{com},\allowbreak\mathit{com}_{1},\allowbreak\mathit{com}_{2},\allowbreak\pi_{s}\rangle,\allowbreak\langle\mathit{m}_{1},\allowbreak\mathit{m}_{2},\allowbreak o,\allowbreak o_{1},\allowbreak o_{2}\rangle)
such that
\mathit{m}_{1}\allowbreak=\allowbreak\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}}
and
\mathit{m}_{2}\allowbreak=\allowbreak\mathit{v}
. If such a tuple is not stored,
\mathcal{S}
outputs failure, else
\mathcal{S}
takes
\mathcal{U}_{j}
and
\mathit{wn}
from that tuple. We remark that, in this tuple,
\mathcal{U}_{j}
is always the identity of a corrupt user.

•:

\mathcal{S}
finds a tuple
(\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathcal{U}^{\prime}_{j},\allowbreak\mathit{wn}^{\prime},\allowbreak\mathit{\mathbb{S}})
such that
\mathcal{U}^{\prime}_{j}\allowbreak=\allowbreak\mathcal{U}_{j}
and
\mathit{wn}^{\prime}\allowbreak=\allowbreak\mathit{wn}
.
\mathcal{S}
outputs failure if such a tuple is not stored, or if
|\mathit{\mathbb{S}}|<t-\tilde{t}
, where
\tilde{t}
is the number of corrupt authorities.

•:

For
k\in[0,V-1]
,
\mathcal{S}
checks whether
S_{k}=\delta^{1/(\mathit{v}+l_{k}+1)}
and
T_{k}\allowbreak=\allowbreak\mathit{g}^{\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}}+R_{k}/(\mathit{v}+l_{k}+1)}
, where
R_{k}\leftarrow H^{\prime}(\mathit{payinfo},k)
. If any of those checks fails,
\mathcal{S}
outputs failure.

•:

\mathcal{S}
stores the tuple
(\mathit{pay},\allowbreak\mathit{payinfo},\langle\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}},\allowbreak\mathit{v},\allowbreak r,\allowbreak o_{c},\allowbreak\langle l_{k},\allowbreak o_{a_{k}},\allowbreak\mu_{k},\allowbreak o_{\mu_{k}}\rangle_{k=0}^{V-1}\rangle)
.

Figure 5.
Procedure for checking a corrupt user payment.

Procedure: Check Double Spending

•:

\mathcal{S}
takes the tuple
(\mathit{pay},\allowbreak\mathit{payinfo},\langle\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}},\allowbreak\mathit{v},\allowbreak r,\allowbreak o_{c},\allowbreak\langle l_{k},\allowbreak o_{a_{k}},\allowbreak\mu_{k},\allowbreak o_{\mu_{k}}\rangle_{k=0}^{V-1}\rangle)
for the payment made by a corrupt user.

•:

For all the stored tuples
(\mathit{pay}^{\prime},\allowbreak\mathit{payinfo}^{\prime},\langle\mathit{sk}^{\prime}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}},\allowbreak\mathit{v}^{\prime},\allowbreak r^{\prime},\allowbreak o^{\prime}_{c},\allowbreak\langle l^{\prime}_{k},\allowbreak o^{\prime}_{a_{k}},\allowbreak\mu^{\prime}_{k},\allowbreak o^{\prime}_{\mu_{k}}\rangle_{k=0}^{V^{\prime}-1}\rangle)
,
\mathcal{S}
checks whether
\mathit{sk}^{\prime}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}}\allowbreak=\allowbreak\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}}
,
\mathit{v}^{\prime}\allowbreak=\allowbreak\mathit{v}
and
\langle l^{\prime}_{k}\rangle_{k\in[0,V^{\prime}-1]}\cap\langle l_{k}\rangle_{k\in[0,V-1]}\allowbreak\neq\allowbreak\emptyset
, which means that there is a double spending.
\mathcal{S}
sets
\mathit{PK}
to contain all the registered public keys and runs the algorithm
c\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak\mathsf{Identify}(\mathit{params},\allowbreak\mathit{PK},\allowbreak\mathit{pay},\allowbreak\mathit{pay}^{\prime},\allowbreak\mathit{payinfo},\allowbreak\mathit{payinfo}^{\prime})
. Then
\mathcal{S}
proceeds as follows:

–:

If there is double spending,
\mathcal{S}
does the following:

*:

If
\mathit{payinfo}\allowbreak=\allowbreak\mathit{payinfo}^{\prime}
and
c\allowbreak\neq\allowbreak\mathit{payinfo}
,
\mathcal{S}
outputs failure. We note that authorities verify that
\mathit{payinfo}
contains an identifier of the provider that deposited the payment. This avoids that an honest provider can be found guilty when an adversarial provider e.g. double deposits a payment with payment information
\mathit{payinfo}
that contains the identifier of an honest provider.

*:

If
\mathit{payinfo}\allowbreak\neq\allowbreak\mathit{payinfo}^{\prime}
and
c\allowbreak\neq\allowbreak\mathit{pk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}}
, where
\mathit{pk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}}
is the public key associated with secret key
\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}}
,
\mathcal{S}
outputs failure.

–:

If there is not double spending, and if
c\allowbreak=\allowbreak\mathit{pk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}}
, where
\mathit{pk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}}
is a public key associated with an honest user,
\mathcal{S}
outputs failure. We remark that it is possible for the adversary to produce two payments where there is not double-spending, and yet the algorithm
\mathsf{Identify}
detects double spending. When that happens, we must ensure that algorithm
\mathsf{Identify}
does not output the public key of an honest user. We recall that authorities verify that
\mathit{payinfo}
contains an identifier of the provider that deposited the payment, which also avoids an honest provider from being framed in this case.

Figure 6.
Procedure for checking double spending.

Corrupt provider begins deposit.:

When
\mathcal{A}
sends
(\mathsf{bb.write.ini},\allowbreak\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\langle\mathit{pay},\allowbreak\mathit{payinfo}\rangle)
, the simulator
\mathcal{S}
checks if a tuple
(\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{payid},\allowbreak\mathit{pay}^{\prime},\allowbreak\mathit{payinfo}^{\prime})
such that
\mathit{pay}^{\prime}\allowbreak=\allowbreak\mathit{pay}
and
\mathit{payinfo}^{\prime}\allowbreak=\allowbreak\mathit{payinfo}
is stored. If it is stored, which means that this is a payment that was sent to
\mathcal{A}
by the simulator acting as an honest user,
\mathcal{S}
takes
\mathit{payid}
from that tuple and proceeds with step 2 below. Otherwise we are in the case of a payment that a corrupt user sent to a corrupt provider, and thus
\mathcal{S}
did not receive it during the spending phase.
\mathcal{S}
proceeds with step 1.

Step 1.

\mathcal{S}
proceeds as follows:

•:

\mathcal{S}
runs
b\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak\mathsf{SpendVf}(\mathit{pk},\allowbreak\mathit{pay},\allowbreak\mathit{payinfo})
. If
b\allowbreak=\allowbreak 0
, the simulator
\mathcal{S}
aborts.

•:

\mathcal{S}
runs the procedure in Figure
5
to store
(\mathit{pay},\allowbreak\mathit{payinfo},\langle\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}},\allowbreak\mathit{v},\allowbreak r,\allowbreak o_{c},\allowbreak\langle l_{k},\allowbreak o_{a_{k}},\allowbreak\mu_{k},\allowbreak o_{\mu_{k}}\rangle_{k=0}^{V-1}\rangle)
.

•:

The simulator
\mathcal{S}
checks that the algorithm for identification of double spenders identifies the double spender properly by running the procedure in Figure
6
.

\mathcal{S}
sets
\mathit{\mathbb{D}}\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak\{l_{k}|k\in[0,V-1]\}
, picks a random pseudonym
\mathit{P}\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak\mathbb{U}_{p}
, takes the identity
\mathcal{P}_{k}
of the corrupt provider that sent the message
(\mathsf{bb.write.ini},\ldots)
, and sends
(\mathsf{ec.spend.ini},\allowbreak\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{wn},\allowbreak V,\allowbreak\mathit{\mathbb{D}},\allowbreak\mathit{payinfo},\allowbreak\mathit{P},\allowbreak\mathcal{P}_{k})
to
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{EC}}
. When
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{EC}}
sends the message
(\mathsf{ec.spend.sim},\allowbreak\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{qid})
,
\mathcal{S}
sends
(\mathsf{ec.spend.rep},\allowbreak\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{qid})
to
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{EC}}
. When
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{EC}}
sends the message
(\mathsf{ec.spend.end},\allowbreak\mathit{payid},\allowbreak V,\allowbreak\mathit{payinfo},\allowbreak\mathit{P})
,
\mathcal{S}
proceeds with step 2.

Step 2.

\mathcal{S}
sends
(\mathsf{ec.deposit.ini},\allowbreak\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{payid})
to
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{EC}}
. When
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{EC}}
sends
(\mathsf{ec.deposit.sim},\allowbreak\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{qid})
,
\mathcal{S}
runs a copy of
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{BB}}
on input the message
(\mathsf{bb.write.ini},\allowbreak\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\langle\mathit{pay},\allowbreak\mathit{payinfo}\rangle)
. When
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{BB}}
sends the message
(\mathsf{bb.write.sim},\allowbreak\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{qid})
,
\mathcal{S}
forwards that message to
\mathcal{A}
.

Corrupt provider ends deposit.:

When the adversary
\mathcal{A}
sends the message
(\mathsf{bb.write.rep},\allowbreak\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{qid})
,
\mathcal{S}
runs the copy of
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{BB}}
on input that message. When
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{BB}}
sends
(\mathsf{bb.write.end},\allowbreak\mathit{sid})
,
\mathcal{S}
sends
(\mathsf{ec.deposit.rep},\allowbreak\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{qid})
to
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{EC}}
. When
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{EC}}
sends
(\mathsf{ec.deposit.end},\allowbreak\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{payid})
,
\mathcal{S}
sends
(\mathsf{bb.write.end},\allowbreak\mathit{sid})
to
\mathcal{A}
.

Honest authority runs deposit verification.:

When
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{EC}}
sends the message
(\mathsf{ec.depvf.sim},\allowbreak\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{qid},\allowbreak\mathit{\mathbb{U}}^{\prime},\allowbreak\mathit{d})
, for all
\mathcal{U}_{j}\allowbreak\in\allowbreak\mathit{\mathbb{U}}^{\prime}
,
\mathcal{S}
runs the procedure in Figure
3
. After that, for
i=1
to
\mathit{d}
,
\mathcal{S}
does the following:

•:

Send
(\mathsf{bb.read.sim},\allowbreak\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{qid})
to
\mathcal{A}
.

•:

Receive
(\mathsf{bb.read.rep},\allowbreak\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{qid})
from
\mathcal{A}
.

\mathcal{S}
sends
(\mathsf{ec.depvf.rep},\allowbreak\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{qid})
to
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{EC}}
.

Corrupt authority starts reading of bulletin board.:

When
\mathcal{A}
sends
(\mathsf{bb.read.ini},\allowbreak\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{i})
,
\mathcal{S}
runs
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{BB}}
on input that message. When
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{BB}}
sends
(\mathsf{bb.read.sim},\allowbreak\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{qid})
,
\mathcal{S}
forwards that message to
\mathcal{A}
.

Corrupt authority ends reading of bulletin board.:

When the adversary
\mathcal{A}
sends
(\mathsf{bb.read.rep},\allowbreak\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{qid})
,
\mathcal{S}
runs
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{BB}}
on input that message. When
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{BB}}
sends
(\mathsf{bb.read.end},\allowbreak\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{m}^{\prime})
,
\mathcal{S}
parses
\mathit{m}^{\prime}
as
(\mathcal{P}_{k},\allowbreak\mathit{pay},\allowbreak\mathit{payinfo})
. If
(\mathit{pay},\allowbreak\mathit{payinfo})
is a payment that was computed by
\mathcal{S}
,
\mathcal{S}
parses
\mathit{pay}
as
(\kappa,\allowbreak\sigma^{\prime},\allowbreak\langle S_{k},\allowbreak T_{k},\allowbreak A_{k}\rangle_{k\in[0,V-1]},\allowbreak V,\allowbreak C,\allowbreak\pi_{v})
and checks whether any serial number
S_{k}
is equal to any of the other serial numbers stored in the payments in
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{BB}}
. If that is the case,
\mathcal{S}
outputs failure. Otherwise
\mathcal{S}
sends
(\mathsf{bb.read.end},\allowbreak\mathit{sid},\allowbreak\mathit{m}^{\prime})
to
\mathcal{A}
. We remark that payments computed by the adversary were already checked before by
\mathcal{S}
through the procedure in Figure
6
.

Theorem D.1.

When a subset of users
\mathcal{U}_{j}
, a subset of providers
\mathcal{P}_{k}
and up to
t-1
authorities
\mathcal{V}_{i}
are corrupt,
\mathrm{\Pi}_{\mathrm{EC}}
securely realizes
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{EC}}
in the random oracle model and in the
(\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{SMT}},\allowbreak\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{NYM}},\allowbreak\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{KG}},\allowbreak\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{REG}},\allowbreak\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{BB}})
-hybrid model if the non-interactive proof of knowledge scheme is zero-knowledge and provides weak simulation extractability, the signature scheme by Pointcheval-Sanders in the RO model is unforgeable, the commmitment scheme is hiding and binding, the function in §
A.6
is pseudorandom, and the hash function
H^{\prime}
is collision-resistant.

Proof of Theorem
D.1
.

We show by means of a series of hybrid games that the environment
\mathcal{Z}
cannot distinguish between the ensemble
\mathrm{REAL}_{\mathrm{\Pi}_{\mathrm{EC}},\mathcal{A},\mathcal{Z}}
and the ensemble
\mathrm{IDEAL}_{\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{EC}},\mathcal{S},\mathcal{Z}}
with non-negligible probability. We denote by
\textrm{Pr}[\textbf{Game}\ i]
the probability that the environment distinguishes
\textbf{Game}\ i
from the real-world protocol.

\textbf{Game}\ 0
::

This game corresponds to the execution of the real-world protocol. Therefore,
\textrm{Pr}[\textbf{Game}\ 0]=0
.

\textbf{Game}\ 1
::

This game proceeds as
\textbf{Game}\ 0
, except that
\textbf{Game}\ 1
runs the extractor
\mathcal{E}_{s}
for the non-interactive ZK proofs of knowledge
\pi_{s}
sent by the adversary. Under the weak simulation extractability property of the proof system (Definition
A.8
), we have that
|\Pr[\textbf{Game}\ 1]-\Pr[\textbf{Game}\ 0]|\allowbreak\leq\allowbreak\textrm{Adv}_{\mathcal{A}}^{\mathsf{ext}}
.

\textbf{Game}\ 2
::

This game proceeds as
\textbf{Game}\ 1
, except that
\textbf{Game}\ 2
outputs failure if two request messages were received from the adversary with commitments
\mathit{com}^{\prime}
and
\mathit{com}
and proofs
\pi^{\prime}_{s}
and
\pi_{s}
such that
\mathit{com}^{\prime}\allowbreak=\allowbreak\mathit{com}
but, after extraction of the witnesses
(\mathit{m}^{\prime}_{1},\allowbreak\mathit{m}^{\prime}_{2},\allowbreak o^{\prime},\allowbreak o^{\prime}_{1},\allowbreak o^{\prime}_{2})
from
\pi^{\prime}_{s}
and
(\mathit{m}_{1},\allowbreak\mathit{m}_{2},\allowbreak o,\allowbreak o_{1},\allowbreak o_{2})
from
\pi_{s}
,
(\mathit{m}^{\prime}_{1},\allowbreak\mathit{m}^{\prime}_{2})\allowbreak\neq\allowbreak(\mathit{m}_{1},\allowbreak\mathit{m}_{2})
. Under the binding property of the commitment scheme, we have that
|\Pr[\textbf{Game}\ 2]-\Pr[\textbf{Game}\ 1]|\allowbreak\leq\allowbreak\textrm{Adv}_{\mathcal{A}}^{\mathsf{bin}}
.

Proof.

Given an adversary that makes
\textbf{Game}\ 2
output failure with non-negligible probability, we construct an algorithm
B
that breaks the binding property of the commitment scheme with non-negligible probability.
B
works as follows.
B
receives the parameters of the Pedersen commitment scheme
\mathit{par_{c}}\allowbreak=(\mathit{g},\allowbreak\gamma_{1},\allowbreak\gamma_{2})
from the challenger. When running
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{KG}}
,
B
uses those parameters to set the values
(\mathit{g},\allowbreak\gamma_{1},\allowbreak\gamma_{2})
in the parameters
\mathit{params}\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak(\mathit{p},\allowbreak\mathbb{G},\allowbreak\mathbb{\tilde{G}},\allowbreak{\mathbb{G}_{t}},\allowbreak\mathit{e},\allowbreak\mathit{g},\allowbreak\mathit{\tilde{g}},\allowbreak\gamma_{1},\allowbreak\gamma_{2},\allowbreak\delta,\allowbreak L)
. When the adversary sends two requests with commitments
\mathit{com}^{\prime}
and
\mathit{com}
and proofs
\pi^{\prime}_{s}
and
\pi_{s}
such that
\mathit{com}^{\prime}\allowbreak=\allowbreak\mathit{com}
but, after extraction of the witnesses
(\mathit{m}^{\prime}_{1},\allowbreak\mathit{m}^{\prime}_{2},\allowbreak o^{\prime},\allowbreak o^{\prime}_{1},\allowbreak o^{\prime}_{2})
from
\pi^{\prime}_{s}
and
(\mathit{m}_{1},\allowbreak\mathit{m}_{2},\allowbreak o,\allowbreak o_{1},\allowbreak o_{2})
from
\pi_{s}
, it holds that
(\mathit{m}^{\prime}_{1},\allowbreak\mathit{m}^{\prime}_{2})\allowbreak\neq\allowbreak(\mathit{m}_{1},\allowbreak\mathit{m}_{2})
,
B
sends
(\mathit{com},\mathit{m}_{1},\allowbreak\mathit{m}_{2},\allowbreak o,\mathit{m}^{\prime}_{1},\allowbreak\mathit{m}^{\prime}_{2},\allowbreak o^{\prime})
to the challenger.
∎

\textbf{Game}\ 3
::

This game proceeds as
\textbf{Game}\ 2
, except that
\textbf{Game}\ 3
runs the extractor
\mathcal{E}_{v}
for the non-interactive ZK proofs of knowledge
\pi_{v}
sent by the adversary. Under the weak simulation extractability property of the proof system (Definition
A.8
), we have that
|\Pr[\textbf{Game}\ 3]-\Pr[\textbf{Game}\ 2]|\allowbreak\leq\allowbreak\textrm{Adv}_{\mathcal{A}}^{\mathsf{ext}}
.

\textbf{Game}\ 4
::

This game proceeds as
\textbf{Game}\ 3
, except that, after extracting the witness
(\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}},\allowbreak\mathit{v},\allowbreak r,\allowbreak o_{c},\allowbreak\langle l_{k},\allowbreak o_{a_{k}},\allowbreak\mu_{k},\allowbreak o_{\mu_{k}}\rangle_{k=0}^{V-1})
from a proof
\pi_{v}
,
\textbf{Game}\ 4
takes the payment
\mathit{pay}\allowbreak=\allowbreak(\kappa,\allowbreak\sigma^{\prime},\allowbreak\langle S_{k},\allowbreak T_{k},\allowbreak A_{k}\rangle_{k\in[0,V-1]},\allowbreak V,\allowbreak C,\allowbreak\pi_{v})
that contains
\pi_{v}
and parses
\sigma^{\prime}
as
(\mathit{h}^{\prime},\allowbreak s^{\prime})
.
\textbf{Game}\ 4
computes
\hat{\sigma}\allowbreak=\allowbreak(\hat{\mathit{h}},\allowbreak\hat{s})\allowbreak=\allowbreak(\mathit{h}^{\prime},\allowbreak s^{\prime}(\mathit{h}^{\prime})^{-r})
. Then
\textbf{Game}\ 4
outputs failure if
\hat{\sigma}
is not a valid signature. As shown below, the computation
\hat{\sigma}\allowbreak=\allowbreak(\hat{\mathit{h}},\allowbreak\hat{s})\allowbreak=\allowbreak(\mathit{h}^{\prime},\allowbreak s^{\prime}(\mathit{h}^{\prime})^{-r})
always produces a valid signature, and thus
|\Pr[\textbf{Game}\ 4]-\Pr[\textbf{Game}\ 3]|\allowbreak=\allowbreak 0
.

Proof.

We follow the proof in
(
Rial and Piotrowska 2022
)
. We observe that, after extraction from
\pi_{v}
is successful,
\kappa
is of the form
\kappa\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak\tilde{\alpha}\tilde{\beta}_{1}^{\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}}}\tilde{\beta}_{2}^{\mathit{v}}\mathit{\tilde{g}}^{r}
. We also know that the following equality holds

\mathit{e}(\mathit{h}^{\prime},\kappa)=\mathit{e}(s^{\prime},\mathit{\tilde{g}})

If we replace
\kappa
by
\tilde{\alpha}\tilde{\beta}_{1}^{\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}}}\tilde{\beta}_{2}^{\mathit{v}}\mathit{\tilde{g}}^{r}
, we have that

\mathit{e}(\mathit{h}^{\prime},\tilde{\alpha}\tilde{\beta}_{1}^{\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}}}\tilde{\beta}_{2}^{\mathit{v}}\mathit{\tilde{g}}^{r})=\mathit{e}(s^{\prime},\mathit{\tilde{g}})

If we now multiply the two sides of the equality by
\mathit{e}(\mathit{h}^{\prime},\mathit{\tilde{g}}^{-r})
, we have that

\mathit{e}(\mathit{h}^{\prime},\tilde{\alpha}\tilde{\beta}_{1}^{\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}}}\tilde{\beta}_{2}^{\mathit{v}}\mathit{\tilde{g}}^{r})\mathit{e}(\mathit{h}^{\prime},\mathit{\tilde{g}}^{-r})=\mathit{e}(s^{\prime},\mathit{\tilde{g}})\mathit{e}(\mathit{h}^{\prime},\mathit{\tilde{g}}^{-r})

and this gives us

\mathit{e}(\mathit{h}^{\prime},\tilde{\alpha}\tilde{\beta}_{1}^{\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}}}\tilde{\beta}_{2}^{\mathit{v}})=\mathit{e}(s^{\prime}(\mathit{h}^{\prime})^{-r},\mathit{\tilde{g}})

which is the verification equation of the Pointcheval-Sanders signature scheme for the signature
(\mathit{h}^{\prime},s^{\prime}(\mathit{h}^{\prime})^{-r})
. Therefore, the computation
\hat{\sigma}\allowbreak=\allowbreak(\hat{\mathit{h}},\allowbreak\hat{s})\allowbreak=\allowbreak(\mathit{h}^{\prime},\allowbreak s^{\prime}(\mathit{h}^{\prime})^{-r})
always produces a valid signature.
∎

\textbf{Game}\ 5
::

This game proceeds as
\textbf{Game}\ 4
, except that
\textbf{Game}\ 5
outputs failure if, after computing the signature
\hat{\sigma}\allowbreak=\allowbreak(\hat{\mathit{h}},\allowbreak\hat{s})\allowbreak=\allowbreak(\mathit{h}^{\prime},\allowbreak s^{\prime}(\mathit{h}^{\prime})^{-r})
on
(\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}},\allowbreak\mathit{v})
, it is the case that the adversary was not issued at least
t-\tilde{t}
signatures from
t-\tilde{t}
different authorities on
(\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}},\allowbreak\mathit{v})
. Under the unforgeability property of Pointcheval-Sanders signatures in the random oracle model, we have that
|\Pr[\textbf{Game}\ 5]-\Pr[\textbf{Game}\ 4]|\leq\textrm{Adv}_{\mathcal{A}}^{\mathsf{unf}}\cdot((n-\tilde{t})!/((t-1-\tilde{t})!(n-t+1)!))
, where
n
is the number of authorities,
t
is the threshold and
\tilde{t}
is the number of corrupt authorities.

Proof.

We follow the proof in
(
Rial and Piotrowska 2022
)
. We construct an algorithm
B
that interacts with the challenger of the existential unforgeability game in the RO model (Definition
A.14
) and the adversary
\mathcal{A}
and that shows that, if
\mathcal{A}
makes
\textbf{Game}\ 5
output failure with non-negligible probability, then
\mathcal{A}
can be used by
B
to break the existential unforgeability property in the RO model of Pointcheval-Sanders signatures.

B
receives a public key
(\theta,\allowbreak\tilde{\alpha},\allowbreak\beta_{1},\tilde{\beta}_{1},\allowbreak\beta_{2},\allowbreak\tilde{\beta}_{2})
from the challenger. To set up the keys when running functionality
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{KG}}
,
B
proceeds as follows.

•:

B
uses the bilinear map setup
\theta
to set
\mathit{params}
.
B
uses the public key received from the challenger to set the verification key
\mathit{pk}=(\mathit{params},\allowbreak\tilde{\alpha},\allowbreak\beta_{1},\allowbreak\tilde{\beta}_{1},\allowbreak\beta_{2},\tilde{\beta}_{2})
.

•:

Let
\mathbb{T}
be the set of indices of corrupt authorities. Let
\mathbb{U}
be a set of indices of size
t-1-|\mathbb{T}|
picked at random from
[1,n]\allowbreak\setminus\allowbreak\mathbb{T}
. Let
\mathbb{S}^{\prime}\leftarrow\mathbb{T}\cup\mathbb{U}
. To compute the secret keys and public keys of the authorities
\mathcal{V}_{i}
such that
i\allowbreak\in\allowbreak\mathbb{S}^{\prime}
,
B
picks random
(x_{i},\allowbreak y_{i,1},\allowbreak y_{i,2})\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak\mathbb{Z}_{p}
and computes
\mathit{pk}_{\mathcal{V}_{i}}\allowbreak=\allowbreak(\tilde{\alpha}_{i},\allowbreak\beta_{i,1},\allowbreak\tilde{\beta}_{i,1},\allowbreak\beta_{i,2},\allowbreak\tilde{\beta}_{i,2})\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak(\mathit{\tilde{g}}^{x_{i}},\allowbreak\mathit{g}^{y_{i,1}},\allowbreak\mathit{\tilde{g}}^{y_{i,1}},\allowbreak\mathit{g}^{y_{i,2}},\allowbreak\mathit{\tilde{g}}^{y_{i,2}})
.

•:

Let
\mathbb{S}\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak\mathbb{S}^{\prime}\allowbreak\cup\allowbreak\{0\}
and let
\mathbb{D}\allowbreak=\allowbreak[1,n]\setminus\mathbb{S}^{\prime}
. To compute the public keys of the remaining authorities, i.e. the authorities in the set
\mathbb{D}
,
B
does the following. Let
(\tilde{\alpha}_{0},\allowbreak\beta_{0,1},\allowbreak\tilde{\beta}_{0,1},\allowbreak\beta_{0,2},\allowbreak\tilde{\beta}_{0,2})\leftarrow(\tilde{\alpha},\allowbreak\beta_{1},\allowbreak\tilde{\beta}_{1},\allowbreak\beta_{2},\tilde{\beta}_{2})
. For all
d\in\mathbb{D}
:

–:

For all
i\in\mathbb{S}
, evaluate at
d
the Lagrange basis polynomials

l_{i}=[\prod_{j\in\mathbb{S},j\neq i}(d-j)][\prod_{j\in\mathbb{S},j\neq i}(i-j)]^{-1}\ \mathrm{mod}\ \mathit{p}

–:

For all
i\in\mathbb{S}
, take
(\tilde{\alpha}_{i},\allowbreak\beta_{i,1},\allowbreak\tilde{\beta}_{i,1},\allowbreak\beta_{i,2},\allowbreak\tilde{\beta}_{i,2})
and then do
\mathit{pk}_{\mathcal{V}_{d}}=(\tilde{\alpha}_{d},\allowbreak\beta_{d,1},\allowbreak\tilde{\beta}_{d,1},\allowbreak\beta_{d,2},\allowbreak\tilde{\beta}_{d,2})=(\prod_{i\in\mathbb{S}}\tilde{\alpha}_{i}^{l_{i}},\allowbreak\prod_{i\in\mathbb{S}}\beta_{i,1}^{l_{i}},\allowbreak\prod_{i\in\mathbb{S}}\tilde{\beta}_{i,1}^{l_{i}},\allowbreak\prod_{i\in\mathbb{S}}\beta_{i,2}^{l_{i}},\allowbreak\prod_{i\in\mathbb{S}}\tilde{\beta}_{i,2}^{l_{i}})
.

To reply the random oracle queries
H(\mathit{com})
of the adversary
\mathcal{A}
,
B
forwards the query
\mathit{com}
to the random oracle provided by the challenger and sends
\mathcal{A}
the response
\mathit{h}
given by the challenger.

When
\mathcal{A}
sends a valid request
\mathit{req}\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak(\mathit{h},\allowbreak\mathit{com},\allowbreak\mathit{com}_{1},\allowbreak\mathit{com}_{2},\allowbreak\pi_{s})
,
B
runs the extractor
\mathcal{E}_{s}
to extract the witness
(\mathit{m}_{1},\allowbreak\mathit{m}_{2},\allowbreak o,\allowbreak o_{1},\allowbreak o_{2})
from
\pi_{s}
.
B
outputs failure if two request messages were received with commitments
\mathit{com}^{\prime}
and
\mathit{com}
and proofs
\pi^{\prime}_{s}
and
\pi_{s}
such that
\mathit{com}^{\prime}\allowbreak=\allowbreak\mathit{com}
but, after extraction of the witnesses from
\pi^{\prime}_{s}
and
\pi_{s}
,
(m^{\prime}_{1},\allowbreak m^{\prime}_{2})\allowbreak\neq\allowbreak(m_{1},\allowbreak m_{2})
. As shown in
\textbf{Game}\ 2
, the probability that
B
fails is negligible if the commitment scheme is binding. This guarantees that
\mathit{com}
is different for each tuple of messages
(m^{\prime}_{1},\allowbreak m^{\prime}_{2})
, which is necessary when querying the signing oracle.

If the request is sent to an authority
\mathcal{V}_{i}
such that
i\allowbreak\in\allowbreak\mathbb{U}
,
B
computes an issuance message by following
\mathrm{\Pi}_{\mathrm{EC}}
and stores
(m_{1},\allowbreak m_{2},\allowbreak\mathcal{V}_{i})
. (We note that in this case
B
knows the secret key of the authority.) If the request is sent to
\mathcal{V}_{d}
such that
d\allowbreak\in\allowbreak\mathbb{D}
,
B
proceeds as follows:

•:

B
submits the message tuple
(m_{1},\allowbreak m_{2})
that was extracted by
\mathcal{E}_{s}
and the commitment
\mathit{com}
to the signing oracle provided by the challenger. The challenger sends a signature
\sigma_{0}\allowbreak=\allowbreak(\mathit{h},\allowbreak s_{0})
and state information
\mathit{st}^{\prime}
.

•:

For all
i\allowbreak\in\allowbreak\mathbb{S}^{\prime}
,
B
computes a signature
\sigma_{i}=(\mathit{h},s_{i})
by using the secret keys of authorities in
\mathbb{S}^{\prime}
.

•:

For all
i\allowbreak\in\allowbreak\mathbb{S}
,
B
evaluates at
d
the Lagrange basis polynomials

l_{i}=[\prod_{j\in\mathbb{S},j\neq i}(d-j)][\prod_{j\in\mathbb{S},j\neq i}(i-j)]^{-1}\ \mathrm{mod}\ \mathit{p}

•:

B
computes the signature
\sigma_{d}=(\mathit{h},s_{d})\leftarrow(\mathit{h},\prod_{i\in\mathbb{S}}s_{i}^{l_{i}})
. We note that in this computation the signature sent by the challenger is used.

•:

B
computes
\hat{\sigma}_{d}=(\mathit{h},s_{d}\beta_{d,1}^{o_{1}}\beta_{d,2}^{o_{2}})
and includes it in the issuance message sent to
\mathcal{A}
.
B
stores
(m_{1},\allowbreak m_{2},\mathcal{V}_{d})
.

After the adversary
\mathcal{A}
sends a valid payment
\mathit{pay}\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak(\kappa,\allowbreak\sigma^{\prime},\allowbreak\langle S_{k},\allowbreak T_{k},\allowbreak A_{k}\rangle_{k\in[0,V-1]},\allowbreak V,\allowbreak C,\allowbreak\pi_{v})
,
B
proceeds as follows.

•:

B
runs the extractor
\mathcal{E}_{v}
to extract the witness
(\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}},\allowbreak\mathit{v},\allowbreak r,\allowbreak o_{c},\allowbreak\langle l_{k},\allowbreak o_{a_{k}},\allowbreak\mu_{k},\allowbreak o_{\mu_{k}}\rangle_{k=0}^{V-1})
from the proof
\pi_{v}
.

•:

B
parses
\sigma^{\prime}
as
(\mathit{h}^{\prime},\allowbreak s^{\prime})
and computes
\hat{\sigma}\allowbreak=\allowbreak(\hat{\mathit{h}},\allowbreak\hat{s})\allowbreak=\allowbreak(\mathit{h}^{\prime},\allowbreak s^{\prime}(\mathit{h}^{\prime})^{-r})
.
B
runs the verification equation
\mathit{e}(\hat{\mathit{h}},\allowbreak\tilde{\alpha}\allowbreak\tilde{\beta}_{1}^{\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}}}\tilde{\beta}_{2}^{\mathit{v}})\allowbreak=\allowbreak\mathit{e}(\hat{s},\allowbreak\mathit{\tilde{g}})
of the Pointcheval-Sanders signature scheme. If for any signature
\hat{\sigma}_{l}
the verification equation does not hold,
B
outputs failure. As shown in
\textbf{Game}\ 4
, the probability that
B
outputs failure is
0
.

•:

B
checks that there are at least
t-|\mathbb{T}|
tuples
(m_{1},\allowbreak m_{2},\allowbreak\mathcal{V}_{i})
stored for
t-|\mathbb{T}|
honest authorities. If that is the case,
B
does nothing because
\mathcal{A}
was issued enough signatures to compute the payment. Else, if the adversary
\mathcal{A}
received less than
t-|\mathbb{T}|
signatures from honest authorities, but
\mathcal{A}
did receive a signature from an authority
\mathcal{V}_{d}
such that
d\allowbreak\in\allowbreak\mathbb{D}
,
B
fails because
B
had to the query signing oracle to issue that signature to the adversary and therefore he cannot use
\hat{\sigma}
as a forgery. However, if
\mathcal{A}
received less than
t-|\mathbb{T}|
signatures from honest authorities, and all those authorities
\mathcal{V}_{i}
are such that
i\allowbreak\in\allowbreak\mathbb{U}
,
B
sends
\hat{\sigma}
to the challenger to win the existential unforgeability game.

Finally, the probability that
B
fails can be bound as follows.
B
needs to query the signing oracle of the challenger whenever
\mathcal{A}
requests a signature from an authority
\mathcal{V}_{d}
such that
d\in\mathbb{D}
. Therefore, when
\mathcal{A}
is able to show a signature without receiving
t-|\mathbb{T}|
signatures shares from
t-|\mathbb{T}|
different honest authorities,
B
fails whenever
\mathcal{A}
did request a signature from an authority
\mathcal{V}_{d}
such that
d\in\mathbb{D}
. In the worst case,
\mathcal{A}
received
t-1-|\mathbb{T}|
signatures from
t-1-|\mathbb{T}|
honest authorities. In that worst case,
B
only succeeds when those
t-1-|\mathbb{T}|
authorities are those authorities
\mathcal{V}_{i}
such that
i\in\mathbb{U}
. The probability that
B
succeeds, i.e. the probability that
\mathcal{A}
picks those
t-1-|\mathbb{T}|
authorities from the set of
n-|\mathbb{T}|
authorities is given by the inverse of the number of
(t-1-|\mathbb{T}|)
-element combinations of
n-|\mathbb{T}|
objects taken without repetition

\frac{(t-1-|\mathbb{T}|)!(n-t+1!)}{(n-|\mathbb{T}|)!}

We remark that, in the frequent case in which
t=n
, then
B
succeeds with probability
1/(t-|\mathbb{T}|)
.
∎

\textbf{Game}\ 6
::

This game proceeds as
\textbf{Game}\ 5
, except that, after extracting the witness
(\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}},\allowbreak\mathit{v},\allowbreak r,\allowbreak o_{c},\allowbreak\langle l_{k},\allowbreak o_{a_{k}},\allowbreak\mu_{k},\allowbreak o_{\mu_{k}}\rangle_{k=0}^{V-1})
from a proof
\pi_{v}
,
\textbf{Game}\ 6
takes the payment
\mathit{pay}\allowbreak=\allowbreak(\kappa,\allowbreak\sigma^{\prime},\allowbreak\langle S_{k},\allowbreak T_{k},\allowbreak A_{k}\rangle_{k\in[0,V-1]},\allowbreak V,\allowbreak C,\allowbreak\pi_{v})
that contains
\pi_{v}
and the associated payment information
\mathit{payinfo}
and computes the serial numbers
S^{\prime}_{k}\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak\delta^{1/(\mathit{v}+l_{k}+1)}
and the double spending tags
T^{\prime}_{k}\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak\mathit{g}^{\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}}+R_{k}/(\mathit{v}+l_{k}+1)}
, where
R_{k}\allowbreak=\allowbreak H^{\prime}(\mathit{payinfo},\allowbreak k)
. Then
\textbf{Game}\ 6
outputs failure if, for any
