k\allowbreak\in\allowbreak[0,V-1]
,
S^{\prime}_{k}\allowbreak\neq\allowbreak S_{k}
or
T^{\prime}_{k}\allowbreak\neq\allowbreak T_{k}
. Under the hardness of the discrete logarithm problem,
|\Pr[\textbf{Game}\ 6]-\Pr[\textbf{Game}\ 5]|\allowbreak\leq\allowbreak\textrm{Adv}_{\mathcal{A}}^{\mathsf{dlog}}
.

Proof.

We have an adversary
\mathcal{A}
that, with non-negligible probability, sends payment information
\mathit{payinfo}
and a payment
\mathit{pay}\allowbreak=\allowbreak(\kappa,\allowbreak\sigma^{\prime},\allowbreak\langle S_{k},\allowbreak T_{k},\allowbreak A_{k}\rangle_{k\in[0,V-1]},\allowbreak V,\allowbreak C,\allowbreak\pi_{v})
such that, after extracting from
\pi_{v}
the witness
(\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}},\allowbreak\mathit{v},\allowbreak r,\allowbreak o_{c},\allowbreak\langle l_{k},\allowbreak o_{a_{k}},\allowbreak\mu_{k},\allowbreak o_{\mu_{k}}\rangle_{k=0}^{V-1})
and computing
S^{\prime}_{k}\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak\delta^{1/(\mathit{v}+l_{k}+1)}
and
T^{\prime}_{k}\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak\mathit{g}^{\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}}+R_{k}/(\mathit{v}+l_{k}+1)}
for all
k\allowbreak\in\allowbreak[0,V-1]
, we have that
S^{\prime}_{k}\allowbreak\neq\allowbreak S_{k}
or
T^{\prime}_{k}\allowbreak\neq\allowbreak T_{k}
for some
k\allowbreak\in\allowbreak[0,V-1]
. We construct an algorithm
B
that uses that adversary to solve the discrete logarithm problem.

B
works as follows. Given an instance
(h,\allowbreak h^{x})
of the discrete logarithm problem, when running the functionality
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{KG}}
to set up the parameters
\mathit{params}\leftarrow(\mathit{p},\mathbb{G},\mathbb{\tilde{G}},\allowbreak{\mathbb{G}_{t}},\mathit{e},\mathit{g},\mathit{\tilde{g}},\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\delta,L)
,
B
sets
\mathit{g}\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak h
and
\gamma_{1}\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak h^{x}
. When the adversary outputs a payment
\mathit{pay}
that fulfills the condition described above for a certain
k\allowbreak\in\allowbreak[0,V-1]
,
B
outputs

x\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak\frac{(o_{a_{k}}+o_{c})\mu_{k}+o_{\mu_{k}}}{1-((\mathit{v}+l_{k}+1)\mu_{k})}

We show that the discrete logarithm
x
is computed correctly as follows. The non-interactive ZK proof of knowledge
\pi_{v}
is described by

\displaystyle\pi_{v}=

\displaystyle\mathrm{NIZK}\{(\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}},\mathit{v},r,o_{c},\langle l_{k},o_{a_{k}},\mu_{k},o_{\mu_{k}}\rangle_{k=0}^{V-1}):

\displaystyle\kappa=\tilde{\alpha}\tilde{\beta}_{1}^{\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}}}\tilde{\beta}_{2}^{\mathit{v}}\mathit{\tilde{g}}^{r}\ \land\ C=\mathit{g}^{o_{c}}\gamma_{1}^{\mathit{v}}\ \land\

\displaystyle\langle A_{k}=\mathit{g}^{o_{a_{k}}}\gamma_{1}^{l_{k}}\ \land\ l_{k}\in[0,L-1]\ \land\

\displaystyle S_{k}=\delta^{\mu_{k}}\ \land\ \gamma_{1}=(A_{k}C\gamma_{1})^{\mu_{k}}\mathit{g}^{o_{\mu_{k}}}\ \land\

\displaystyle T_{k}=\mathit{g}^{\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}}}(\mathit{g}^{R_{k}})^{\mu_{k}}\ \rangle_{k\in[0,V-1]}\}

After successful extraction of the witness
(\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}},\allowbreak\mathit{v},\allowbreak r,\allowbreak o_{c},\allowbreak\langle l_{k},\allowbreak o_{a_{k}},\allowbreak\mu_{k},\allowbreak o_{\mu_{k}}\rangle_{k=0}^{V-1})
, we know that the statements proven by
\pi_{v}
hold. In the case that
S^{\prime}_{k}\allowbreak\neq\allowbreak S_{k}
for some
k\allowbreak\in\allowbreak[0,V-1]
, we have that

\delta^{1/(\mathit{v}+l_{k}+1)}\neq\delta^{\mu_{k}}

and thus
1/(\mathit{v}+l_{k}+1)\neq{\mu_{k}}
.
In the case that
T^{\prime}_{k}\allowbreak\neq\allowbreak T_{k}
for some
k\allowbreak\in\allowbreak[0,V-1]
, we have that

\mathit{g}^{\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}}+R_{k}/(\mathit{v}+l_{k}+1)}\neq\mathit{g}^{\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}}}(\mathit{g}^{R_{k}})^{\mu_{k}}

and thus we can also deduce that
1/(\mathit{v}+l_{k}+1)\neq{\mu_{k}}
.

We also have the following:

\displaystyle\gamma_{1}

\displaystyle=(A_{k}C\gamma_{1})^{\mu_{k}}\mathit{g}^{o_{\mu_{k}}}

\displaystyle=(\mathit{g}^{o_{a_{k}}}\gamma_{1}^{l_{k}}\mathit{g}^{o_{c}}\gamma_{1}^{\mathit{v}}\gamma_{1})^{\mu_{k}}\mathit{g}^{o_{\mu_{k}}}

\displaystyle=\gamma_{1}^{(\mathit{v}+l_{k}+1)\mu_{k}}\mathit{g}^{(o_{a_{k}}+o_{c})\mu_{k}+o_{\mu_{k}}}

and therefore

\gamma_{1}=\mathit{g}^{\frac{(o_{a_{k}}+o_{c})\mu_{k}+o_{\mu_{k}}}{1-((\mathit{v}+l_{k}+1)\mu_{k})}}

This equation shows that, when
1/(\mathit{v}+l_{k}+1)\allowbreak\neq\allowbreak{\mu_{k}}
, we can compute the discrete logarithm
x
as described above.
∎

\textbf{Game}\ 7
::

This game proceeds as
\textbf{Game}\ 6
, except that in
\textbf{Game}\ 7
the non-interactive ZK proofs of knowledge
\pi_{v}
that are sent to the adversary are replaced by simulated proofs computed by the simulator
\mathcal{S}_{v}
. Under the zero-knowledge property of the proof system (see Definition
A.7
), we have that
|\Pr[\textbf{Game}\ 7]-\Pr[\textbf{Game}\ 6]|\allowbreak\leq\allowbreak\textrm{Adv}_{\mathcal{A}}^{\mathsf{zk}}
.

\textbf{Game}\ 8
::

This game proceeds as
\textbf{Game}\ 7
, except that in
\textbf{Game}\ 8
, for the payments
\mathit{pay}\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak(\kappa,\allowbreak\sigma^{\prime},\allowbreak\langle S_{k},\allowbreak T_{k},\allowbreak A_{k}\rangle_{k\in[0,V-1]},\allowbreak V,\allowbreak C,\allowbreak\pi_{v})
that are sent to the adversary, the values
\kappa
and
\sigma^{\prime}
are computed as follows:

•:

Pick random
t\leftarrow\mathbb{Z}_{p}
and
t^{\prime}\leftarrow\mathbb{Z}_{p}
.

•:

Compute
\sigma^{\prime}=(\mathit{h}^{\prime},s^{\prime})\leftarrow(\mathit{g}^{t^{\prime}},\mathit{g}^{tt^{\prime}})
.

•:

Compute
\kappa\leftarrow\mathit{\tilde{g}}^{t}
.

As shown below,
|\Pr[\textbf{Game}\ 8]-\Pr[\textbf{Game}\ 7]|=0
.

Proof.

This proof follows the proof in
(
Rial and Piotrowska 2022
)
. We show that values
\kappa
and
\sigma^{\prime}
follow the same distribution as the ones computed by the honest user in the real-world protocol. Observe that the honest user computes the following:

•:

Pick random
r\leftarrow\mathbb{Z}_{p}
and
r^{\prime}\leftarrow\mathbb{Z}_{p}
.

•:

Set
\sigma^{\prime}=(\mathit{h}^{\prime},s^{\prime})\leftarrow(\mathit{h}^{r^{\prime}},s^{r^{\prime}}(\mathit{h}^{\prime})^{r})
, where we have that

\displaystyle(\mathit{h}^{r^{\prime}},s^{r^{\prime}}(\mathit{h}^{\prime})^{r})

\displaystyle=(\mathit{h}^{r^{\prime}},\allowbreak\mathit{h}^{(x+\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}}y_{1}+\mathit{v}y_{2}+r)r^{\prime}})

\displaystyle=(\mathit{g}^{ur^{\prime}},\mathit{g}^{(x+\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}}y_{1}+\mathit{v}y_{2}+r)ur^{\prime}})

•:

Set
\kappa\leftarrow\alpha\beta_{1}^{\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}}}\beta_{2}^{\mathit{v}}\mathit{\tilde{g}}^{r}=\mathit{\tilde{g}}^{x+\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}}y_{1}+\mathit{v}y_{2}+r}
.

Therefore,
t
corresponds to
(x+\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}}y_{1}+\mathit{v}y_{2}+r_{l})
and
t^{\prime}
corresponds to
ur^{\prime}
, where
u
is a random value such that
\mathit{h}=\mathit{g}^{u}
. Both
(x+\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}}y_{1}+\mathit{v}y_{2}+r)
and
ur^{\prime}
are random. Observe as well that the verification equation
\mathit{e}(\mathit{h}^{\prime},\kappa)=\mathit{e}(s^{\prime},\mathit{\tilde{g}})
still holds because
\mathit{e}(\mathit{g}^{t^{\prime}},\mathit{\tilde{g}}^{t})=\mathit{e}(\mathit{g}^{tt^{\prime}},\mathit{\tilde{g}})
.
∎

\textbf{Game}\ 9
::

This game proceeds as
\textbf{Game}\ 8
, except that in
\textbf{Game}\ 9
the non-interactive ZK proofs of knowledge
\pi_{s}
that are sent to the adversary are replaced by simulated proofs computed by the simulator
\mathcal{S}_{s}
. Under the zero-knowledge property of the proof system (see Definition
A.7
), we have that
|\Pr[\textbf{Game}\ 9]-\Pr[\textbf{Game}\ 8]|\allowbreak\leq\allowbreak\textrm{Adv}_{\mathcal{A}}^{\mathsf{zk}}
.

\textbf{Game}\ 10
::

This game proceeds as
\textbf{Game}\ 9
, except that in
\textbf{Game}\ 10
, in each request that is sent to the adversary, the values
(\mathit{com}_{1},\allowbreak\mathit{com}_{2})
are replaced by
\mathit{com}_{1}\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak\mathit{g}^{o_{1}}\mathit{h}^{\mathit{m}_{1}}
and
\mathit{com}_{2}\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak\mathit{g}^{o_{2}}\mathit{h}^{\mathit{m}_{2}}
, where
(o_{1},\allowbreak\mathit{m}_{1},\allowbreak o_{2},\allowbreak\mathit{m}_{2})
are random values in
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
. At this point, the non-interactive ZK proofs of knowledge
\pi_{s}
are simulated proofs of false statements. We also remark that, after
\textbf{Game}\ 8
, the computation of payment messages does not use the signatures obtained in the issuance phase. Since the values
(\mathit{com}_{1},\allowbreak\mathit{com}_{2})
are uniformly distributed at random, this change does not alter the view of the environment and we have that
|\Pr[\textbf{Game}\ 10]-\Pr[\textbf{Game}\ 9]|=0
.

\textbf{Game}\ 11
::

This game proceeds as
\textbf{Game}\ 10
, except that in
\textbf{Game}\ 11
, in each request that is sent to the adversary, the value
\mathit{com}
is replaced by picking random
\mathit{com}\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak\mathbb{G}
. Under the hiding property of the commitment scheme,
|\Pr[\textbf{Game}\ 11]-\Pr[\textbf{Game}\ 10]|\allowbreak\leq\allowbreak N\cdot\textrm{Adv}_{\mathcal{A}}^{\mathsf{hid}}
, where
N
is the number of commitments
\mathit{com}
sent to the adversary. Since the Pedersen commitment scheme is perfectly hiding,
|\Pr[\textbf{Game}\ 11]-\Pr[\textbf{Game}\ 10]|\allowbreak=\allowbreak 0
.

Proof.

The proof uses a sequence of games
\textbf{Game}\ 10.i
, for
i=0
to
N
.
\textbf{Game}\ 10.0
is equal to
\textbf{Game}\ 10
, whereas
\textbf{Game}\ 10.N
is equal to
\textbf{Game}\ 11
. In
\textbf{Game}\ 10.i
, the first
i
commitments sent to the adversary are set to random values, whereas the remaining ones are set as in
\textbf{Game}\ 10
.

Given an adversary that distinguishes between
\textbf{Game}\ 10.i
and
\textbf{Game}\ 10.(i+1)
with non-negligible probability, we construct an algorithm
B
that breaks the hiding property of the commitment scheme.
B
works as follows.
B
receives the parameters of the Pedersen commitment scheme
\mathit{par_{c}}\allowbreak=(\mathit{g},\allowbreak\gamma_{1},\allowbreak\gamma_{2})
from the challenger. When running
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{KG}}
,
B
uses those parameters to set the values
(\mathit{g},\allowbreak\gamma_{1},\allowbreak\gamma_{2})
in the parameters
\mathit{params}\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak(\mathit{p},\allowbreak\mathbb{G},\allowbreak\mathbb{\tilde{G}},\allowbreak{\mathbb{G}_{t}},\allowbreak\mathit{e},\allowbreak\mathit{g},\allowbreak\mathit{\tilde{g}},\allowbreak\gamma_{1},\allowbreak\gamma_{2},\allowbreak\delta,\allowbreak L)
. To compute the first
i
commitments,
B
sets
\mathit{com}
to random. To compute the commitment
i+1
,
B
sends the messages
(\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}},\allowbreak\mathit{v})
to the challenger.
B
sets
\mathit{com}
to the challenge commitment received from the challenger. As can be seen, if the challenge commitment commits to
(\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}},\allowbreak\mathit{v})
, then we are in
\textbf{Game}\ 10.i
, whereas if the challenge commitment commits to a random message, then we are in
\textbf{Game}\ 10.(i+1)
. The remaining commitments are computed as in
\textbf{Game}\ 10
.
B
sends the adversarial guess to distinguish between
\textbf{Game}\ 10.i
and
\textbf{Game}\ 10.(i+1)
to the challenger of the hiding game.
∎

\textbf{Game}\ 12
::

This game proceeds as
\textbf{Game}\ 11
, except that in
\textbf{Game}\ 12
, for the payments
\mathit{pay}\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak(\kappa,\allowbreak\sigma^{\prime},\allowbreak\langle S_{k},\allowbreak T_{k},\allowbreak A_{k}\rangle_{k\in[0,V-1]},\allowbreak V,\allowbreak C,\allowbreak\pi_{v})
that are sent to the adversary, for all
k\allowbreak\in\allowbreak[0,V-1]
, the values
A_{k}\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak\mathbb{G}
are set to random elements in
\mathbb{G}
. The value
C
is also set to a random element in
\mathbb{G}
. Under the hiding property of the commitment scheme, we have that
|\Pr[\textbf{Game}\ 12]-\Pr[\textbf{Game}\ 11]|\allowbreak\leq\allowbreak N\cdot\textrm{Adv}_{\mathcal{A}}^{\mathsf{hid}}
, where
N
is the number of commitments
C
and
A_{k}
sent to the adversary. Since the Pedersen commitment scheme is perfectly hiding,
|\Pr[\textbf{Game}\ 12]-\Pr[\textbf{Game}\ 11]|\allowbreak=\allowbreak 0
. We omit the proof, which is similar to the proof of indistinguishability between
\textbf{Game}\ 10
and
\textbf{Game}\ 11
.

\textbf{Game}\ 13
::

This game proceeds as
\textbf{Game}\ 12
, except that in
\textbf{Game}\ 13
, for the payments
\mathit{pay}\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak(\kappa,\allowbreak\sigma^{\prime},\allowbreak\langle S_{k},\allowbreak T_{k},\allowbreak A_{k}\rangle_{k\in[0,V-1]},\allowbreak V,\allowbreak C,\allowbreak\pi_{v})
that are sent to the adversary, for all
k\allowbreak\in\allowbreak[0,V-1]
, the values
S_{k}
and
T_{k}
are computed by doing
S_{k}\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak\delta^{r_{k}}
and
T_{k}\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak\mathit{g}^{\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}}}(\mathit{g}^{r_{k}})^{R_{k}}
, where
r_{k}\leftarrow\mathbb{Z}_{p}
is picked up randomly. Under the pseudorandomness property of the pseudorandom function, we have that
|\Pr[\textbf{Game}\ 13]-\Pr[\textbf{Game}\ 12]|\allowbreak\leq\allowbreak\textrm{Adv}_{\mathcal{A}}^{\mathsf{pseu}}
.

Proof.

Given an adversary that is able to distinguish
\textbf{Game}\ 12
from
\textbf{Game}\ 13
with non-negligible probability, we construct an algorithm
B
that breaks the pseudorandomness property of the pseudorandom function
f_{\mathbb{G},\mathit{p},\mathit{g},s}(\cdot)
described in §
A.6
.
B
receives from the challenger the parameters
(\mathbb{G},\allowbreak\mathit{p},\allowbreak\mathit{g})
. When running
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{KG}}
,
B
picks up random
a\allowbreak\in\allowbreak\mathbb{Z}_{p}
, computes
\delta\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak\mathit{g}^{1/a}
and sets the parameters
\mathit{params}\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak(\mathit{p},\allowbreak\mathbb{G},\allowbreak\mathbb{\tilde{G}},\allowbreak{\mathbb{G}_{t}},\allowbreak\mathit{e},\allowbreak\mathit{g},\allowbreak\mathit{\tilde{g}},\allowbreak\gamma_{1},\allowbreak\gamma_{2},\allowbreak\delta,\allowbreak L)
. We remark that, after
\textbf{Game}\ 11
, request messages are computed without requiring knowledge of the coin secret
\mathit{v}
, and so
B
does not need to know the secret
s
of the pseudorandom function to compute them. Similarly, after
\textbf{Game}\ 12
, payment messages are computed without requiring knowledge of the coin secret
\mathit{v}
. To compute a payment
\mathit{pay}\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak(\kappa,\allowbreak\sigma^{\prime},\allowbreak\langle S_{k},\allowbreak T_{k},\allowbreak A_{k}\rangle_{k\in[0,V-1]},\allowbreak V,\allowbreak C,\allowbreak\pi_{v})
to be sent to the adversary,
B
follows the changes described up to
\textbf{Game}\ 12
and, additionally, to compute
\langle S_{k},\allowbreak T_{k}\rangle_{k\in[0,V-1]}
,
B
does the following. For all
k\in[0,V-1]
,
B
sends the coin index
l_{k}
to the oracle of the challenger, which provides a response
Z_{k}
.
B
sets
T_{k}\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak\mathit{g}^{\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}}}(Z_{k})^{R_{k}}
and
S_{k}\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak Z_{k}^{1/a}
. As can be seen, if
Z_{k}=f_{\mathbb{G},\mathit{p},\mathit{g},s}(l_{k})
,
T_{k}
and
S_{k}
are computed as in
\textbf{Game}\ 12
, whereas if
Z_{k}
is random,
Z_{k}
are computed as in
\textbf{Game}\ 13
. Therefore,
B
uses the guess of the adversary to distinguish between
\textbf{Game}\ 12
and
\textbf{Game}\ 13
in order to break the pseudorandomness property of the pseudorandom function.
∎

\textbf{Game}\ 14
::

This game proceeds as
\textbf{Game}\ 13
, except that in
\textbf{Game}\ 14
, for the payments
\mathit{pay}\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak(\kappa,\allowbreak\sigma^{\prime},\allowbreak\langle S_{k},\allowbreak T_{k},\allowbreak A_{k}\rangle_{k\in[0,V-1]},\allowbreak V,\allowbreak C,\allowbreak\pi_{v})
that are sent to the adversary, for all
k\allowbreak\in\allowbreak[0,V-1]
, the values
S_{k}
and
T_{k}
are computed by picking random
S_{k}\allowbreak\leftarrow\mathbb{G}
and
T_{k}\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak\mathbb{G}
. Under the external Diffie-Hellman (XDH) assumption in
\mathbb{G}
, we have that
|\Pr[\textbf{Game}\ 14]-\Pr[\textbf{Game}\ 13]|\allowbreak\leq\allowbreak N_{s}\cdot\textrm{Adv}_{\mathcal{A}}^{\mathsf{xdh}}
, where
N_{s}
is the number of serial numbers and double spending tags sent to the adversary.

Proof.

The proof uses a sequence of games
\textbf{Game}\ 13.i
, for
i=0
to
N_{s}
.
\textbf{Game}\ 13.0
is equal to
\textbf{Game}\ 13
, whereas
\textbf{Game}\ 13.(N_{s})
is equal to
\textbf{Game}\ 14
. In
\textbf{Game}\ 13.i
, the values
S_{k}
and
T_{k}
of the first
i
payments sent to the adversary are set to random values, whereas in the remaining payments they are set as in
\textbf{Game}\ 13
.

Given an adversary that distinguishes between
\textbf{Game}\ 13.i
and
\textbf{Game}\ 13.(i+1)
with non-negligible probability, we construct an algorithm
B
that uses that adversary to solve the XDH problem with non-negligible probability.
B
works as follows. Given an instance
(h,\allowbreak h^{a},\allowbreak h^{b},\allowbreak Z)
of the XDH problem in
\mathbb{G}
, when running
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{KG}}
to set up the parameters
\mathit{params}\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak(\mathit{p},\allowbreak\mathbb{G},\allowbreak\mathbb{\tilde{G}},\allowbreak{\mathbb{G}_{t}},\allowbreak\mathit{e},\allowbreak\mathit{g},\allowbreak\mathit{\tilde{g}},\allowbreak\gamma_{1},\allowbreak\gamma_{2},\allowbreak\delta,\allowbreak L)
,
B
sets
\mathit{g}\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak\mathit{h}^{a}
and
\delta\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak h
. When setting the
i+1
serial number and double spending tag sent to the adversary,
B
sets
S_{k}\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak\mathit{h}^{b}
and
T_{k}\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak\mathit{g}^{\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}}}(Z)^{R_{k}}
. As can be seen, when
Z
is random,
S_{k}
and
T_{k}
are random values and we are thus in
\textbf{Game}\ 13.(i+1)
. In contrast, when
Z\allowbreak=\allowbreak h^{ab}
, we have that
S_{k}\allowbreak=\allowbreak h^{b}\allowbreak=\allowbreak\delta^{b}
and
T_{k}\allowbreak=\allowbreak(h^{a})^{\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}}}(h^{ab})^{R_{k}}=\allowbreak(\mathit{g})^{\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}}}(\mathit{g}^{b})^{R_{k}}
, and thus the distribution is equal to that of
\textbf{Game}\ 13.i
. Therefore,
B
can use the guess of the adversary to distinguish between
\textbf{Game}\ 13.i
and
\textbf{Game}\ 13.(i+1)
in order to solve the XDH problem in
\mathbb{G}
with non-negligible probability.
∎

\textbf{Game}\ 15
::

This game proceeds as
\textbf{Game}\ 14
, except that
\textbf{Game}\ 15
checks that algorithm
\mathsf{Identify}
identifies the double spender when there is double spending. To do that, when the adversary sends two valid payments
(\mathit{pay},\allowbreak\mathit{payinfo})
and
(\mathit{pay}^{\prime},\allowbreak\mathit{payinfo}^{\prime})
, after extracting the witness
\langle\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}},\allowbreak\mathit{v},\allowbreak r,\allowbreak o_{c},\allowbreak\langle l_{k},\allowbreak o_{a_{k}},\allowbreak\mu_{k},\allowbreak o_{\mu_{k}}\rangle_{k=0}^{V-1}\rangle)
from the proofs
\pi_{v}\allowbreak\in\allowbreak\mathit{pay}
and
\langle\mathit{sk}^{\prime}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}},\allowbreak\mathit{v}^{\prime},\allowbreak r^{\prime},\allowbreak o^{\prime}_{c},\allowbreak\langle l^{\prime}_{k},\allowbreak o^{\prime}_{a_{k}},\allowbreak\mu^{\prime}_{k},\allowbreak o^{\prime}_{\mu_{k}}\rangle_{k=0}^{V^{\prime}-1}\rangle)
from the proof
\pi^{\prime}_{v}\allowbreak\in\allowbreak\mathit{pay}^{\prime}
,
\textbf{Game}\ 15
checks whether
\mathit{sk}^{\prime}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}}\allowbreak=\allowbreak\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}}
,
\mathit{v}^{\prime}\allowbreak=\allowbreak\mathit{v}
and
\langle l^{\prime}_{k}\rangle_{k\in[0,V^{\prime}-1]}\cap\langle l_{k}\rangle_{k\in[0,V-1]}\allowbreak\neq\allowbreak\emptyset
, which means that there is a double spending. In that case
\textbf{Game}\ 15
sets
\mathit{PK}
to contain all the registered public keys and runs the algorithm
c\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak\mathsf{Identify}(\mathit{params},\allowbreak\mathit{PK},\allowbreak\mathit{pay},\allowbreak\mathit{pay}^{\prime},\allowbreak\mathit{payinfo},\allowbreak\mathit{payinfo}^{\prime})
. Then
\textbf{Game}\ 15
does the following:

•:

If
\mathit{payinfo}\allowbreak=\allowbreak\mathit{payinfo}^{\prime}
and
c\allowbreak\neq\allowbreak\mathit{payinfo}
,
\textbf{Game}\ 15
outputs failure.

•:

If
\mathit{payinfo}\allowbreak\neq\allowbreak\mathit{payinfo}^{\prime}
and
c\allowbreak\neq\allowbreak\mathit{pk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}}
, where
\mathit{pk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}}
is the public key associated with secret key
\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}}
,
\textbf{Game}\ 15
outputs failure.

The probability that
\textbf{Game}\ 15
fails is negligible under the collision-resistance property of the hash function
H^{\prime}
, i.e. we have that
|\Pr[\textbf{Game}\ 15]-\Pr[\textbf{Game}\ 14]|\allowbreak\leq\allowbreak\textrm{Adv}_{\mathcal{A}}^{\mathsf{col-res}}
.

Proof.

In
\textbf{Game}\ 6
, we have shown that, if a payment is valid, under the hardness of the discrete logarithm assumption, the serial numbers
S_{k}
and the double-spending tags
T_{k}
are correctly computed. Hence, if there are two payments with witnesses
\langle\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}},\allowbreak\mathit{v},\allowbreak r,\allowbreak o_{c},\allowbreak\langle l_{k},\allowbreak o_{a_{k}},\allowbreak\mu_{k},\allowbreak o_{\mu_{k}}\rangle_{k=0}^{V-1}\rangle)
and
\langle\mathit{sk}^{\prime}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}},\allowbreak\mathit{v}^{\prime},\allowbreak r^{\prime},\allowbreak o^{\prime}_{c},\allowbreak\langle l^{\prime}_{k},\allowbreak o^{\prime}_{a_{k}},\allowbreak\mu^{\prime}_{k},\allowbreak o^{\prime}_{\mu_{k}}\rangle_{k=0}^{V^{\prime}-1}\rangle)
such that
\mathit{sk}^{\prime}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}}\allowbreak=\allowbreak\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}}
,
\mathit{v}^{\prime}\allowbreak=\allowbreak\mathit{v}
and
\langle l^{\prime}_{k}\rangle_{k\in[0,V^{\prime}-1]}\cap\langle l_{k}\rangle_{k\in[0,V-1]}\allowbreak\neq\allowbreak\emptyset
, the proof in
\textbf{Game}\ 6
guarantees that, for those coin indices such that
l^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}}\allowbreak=\allowbreak l_{k}
(where
k^{\prime}\allowbreak\in\allowbreak[0,V^{\prime}-1]
and
k\allowbreak\in\allowbreak[0,V-1]
), it is the case that
S^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}}\allowbreak=\allowbreak S_{k}=\allowbreak\delta^{1/(\mathit{v}+l_{k}+1)}
. Therefore, the algorithm
\mathsf{Identify}
always detects double spending.

After detecting double spending, the algorithm
\mathsf{Identify}
checks if
\mathit{payinfo}\allowbreak=\allowbreak\mathit{payinfo}^{\prime}
and in that case sets
c\allowbreak=\allowbreak\mathit{payinfo}
. Therefore, the first condition under which
\textbf{Game}\ 15
fails never happens.

If
\mathit{payinfo}\allowbreak\neq\allowbreak\mathit{payinfo}^{\prime}
, algorithm
\mathsf{Identify}
computes

\mathit{pk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}}\leftarrow((T^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}})^{R_{k}}/T_{k}^{R^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}}})^{(R_{k}-R^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}})^{-1}}

We have that
R_{k}\allowbreak=\allowbreak H^{\prime}(\mathit{payinfo},k)
and
R^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}}\allowbreak=\allowbreak H^{\prime}(\mathit{payinfo}^{\prime},k^{\prime})
. The proof in
\textbf{Game}\ 6
also guarantees that the double spending tags are correctly computed. Hence, we know that
T_{k}=\mathit{g}^{\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}}}(\mathit{g}^{1/(\mathit{v}+l_{k}+1)})^{R_{k}}
and
T^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}}=\mathit{g}^{\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}}}(\mathit{g}^{1/(\mathit{v}+l_{k}+1)})^{R^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}}}
. Let
Z\allowbreak=\allowbreak\mathit{g}^{1/(\mathit{v}+l_{k}+1)}
. We have that

\displaystyle\mathit{pk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}}

\displaystyle=((T^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}})^{R_{k}}/T_{k}^{R^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}}})^{(R_{k}-R^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}})^{-1}}

\displaystyle=((\mathit{g}^{\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}}}(Z)^{R^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}}})^{R_{k}}/(\mathit{g}^{\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}}}(Z)^{R_{k}})^{R^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}}})^{(R_{k}-R^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}})^{-1}}

\displaystyle=((\mathit{g}^{\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}}R_{k}}(Z)^{R^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}}R_{k}})/(\mathit{g}^{\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}}R^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}}}(Z)^{R_{k}R^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}}}))^{(R_{k}-R^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}})^{-1}}

\displaystyle=(\mathit{g}^{\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}}R_{k}}/\mathit{g}^{\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}}R^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}}})^{(R_{k}-R^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}})^{-1}}

\displaystyle=(\mathit{g}^{\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}}(R_{k}-R^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}})})^{(R_{k}-R^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}})^{-1}}

\displaystyle=\mathit{g}^{\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}}}

Therefore, algorithm
\mathsf{Identify}
outputs the public key of the double spender, except when
R_{k}\allowbreak=\allowbreak R^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}}
. Given that
R_{k}\allowbreak=\allowbreak H^{\prime}(\mathit{payinfo},k)
and
R^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}}\allowbreak=\allowbreak H^{\prime}(\mathit{payinfo}^{\prime},k^{\prime})
, if
R_{k}\allowbreak=\allowbreak R^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}}
, a collision for the hash function
H^{\prime}
has been found.
∎

\textbf{Game}\ 16
::

This game proceeds as
\textbf{Game}\ 15
, except that, even if there is not double spending,
\textbf{Game}\ 16
sets
\mathit{PK}
to contain all the registered public keys and runs the algorithm
c\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak\mathsf{Identify}(\mathit{params},\allowbreak\mathit{PK},\allowbreak\mathit{pay},\allowbreak\mathit{pay}^{\prime},\allowbreak\mathit{payinfo},\allowbreak\mathit{payinfo}^{\prime})
. If it is the case that
c\allowbreak=\allowbreak\mathit{pk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}}
, where
\mathit{pk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}}
is a public key associated with an honest user,
\textbf{Game}\ 16
outputs failure. We show that
\textbf{Game}\ 16
outputs failure with negligible probability under the hardness of the discrete logarithm problem, i.e.
|\Pr[\textbf{Game}\ 16]-\Pr[\textbf{Game}\ 15]|\allowbreak\leq\allowbreak N_{u}\allowbreak\cdot\allowbreak\textrm{Adv}_{\mathcal{A}}^{\mathsf{dlog}}
, where
N_{u}
is the number of public keys of honest users.

Proof.

Given an adversary that makes
\textbf{Game}\ 16
fail with non-negligible probability, we construct an algorithm
B
that solves the discrete logarithm problem with non-negligible probability.
B
works as follows.
B
receives an instance
(h,\allowbreak h^{x})
of the discrete logarithm problem from the challenger. When running
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{KG}}
,
B
sets
\mathit{g}\allowbreak\leftarrow h
and sets the parameters
\mathit{params}\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak(\mathit{p},\allowbreak\mathbb{G},\allowbreak\mathbb{\tilde{G}},\allowbreak{\mathbb{G}_{t}},\allowbreak\mathit{e},\allowbreak\mathit{g},\allowbreak\mathit{\tilde{g}},\allowbreak\gamma_{1},\allowbreak\gamma_{2},\allowbreak\delta,\allowbreak L)
.
B
picks randomly an honest user
\mathcal{U}_{j}
and, when that user registers her public key,
B
sets
\mathit{pk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}}\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak\mathit{h}^{x}
. We remark that, since
\textbf{Game}\ 9
, knowledge of
\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}}
is not needed to compute request messages. We also remark that, since
\textbf{Game}\ 8
, knowledge of
\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}}
is not needed to compute payment messages. Therefore,
B
can simulate those messages without knowledge of
\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}}
.

At some point
B
receives from the adversary two payments
(\mathit{pay},\allowbreak\mathit{payinfo})
and
(\mathit{pay}^{\prime},\allowbreak\mathit{payinfo}^{\prime})
that make
\textbf{Game}\ 16
fail. If the public key
\mathit{pk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}}
obtained after running
\mathsf{Identify}
is different from the value
h^{x}
received from the challenger,
B
fails. Otherwise
B
computes
\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}}
as follows. First,
B
extracts the witness
\langle\mathit{sk},\allowbreak\mathit{v},\allowbreak r,\allowbreak o_{c},\allowbreak\langle l_{k},\allowbreak o_{a_{k}},\allowbreak\mu_{k},\allowbreak o_{\mu_{k}}\rangle_{k=0}^{V-1}\rangle)
from the proofs
\pi_{v}\allowbreak\in\allowbreak\mathit{pay}
and
\langle\mathit{sk}^{\prime},\allowbreak\mathit{v}^{\prime},\allowbreak r^{\prime},\allowbreak o^{\prime}_{c},\allowbreak\langle l^{\prime}_{k},\allowbreak o^{\prime}_{a_{k}},\allowbreak\mu^{\prime}_{k},\allowbreak o^{\prime}_{\mu_{k}}\rangle_{k=0}^{V^{\prime}-1}\rangle)
from the proof
\pi^{\prime}_{v}\allowbreak\in\allowbreak\mathit{pay}^{\prime}
. Given that double spending has been detected, and that in
\textbf{Game}\ 6
we proved that serial numbers and double spending tags are correctly computed, we know that there is
S_{k}\in\mathit{pay}
and
S^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}}\in\mathit{pay}^{\prime}
such that
S_{k}=S^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}}=\delta^{b}
, where
b=(1/(\mathit{v}+l_{k}+1))=(1/(\mathit{v}^{\prime}+l^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}}+1))
. Therefore, we also know that the double spending tags are of the form
T_{k}\allowbreak=\allowbreak\mathit{g}^{\mathit{sk}+bR_{k}}
and
T^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}}\allowbreak=\allowbreak\mathit{g}^{\mathit{sk}^{\prime}+bR^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}}}
. From the computation of algorithm
\mathsf{Identify}
, we have that

\displaystyle\mathit{pk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}}

\displaystyle=((T^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}})^{R_{k}}/T_{k}^{R^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}}})^{(R_{k}-R^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}})^{-1}}

\displaystyle=((\mathit{g}^{\mathit{sk}^{\prime}+bR^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}}})^{R_{k}}/(\mathit{g}^{\mathit{sk}+bR_{k}})^{R^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}}})^{(R_{k}-R^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}})^{-1}}

\displaystyle=((\mathit{g}^{R_{k}\mathit{sk}^{\prime}+bR_{k}R^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}}})/(\mathit{g}^{R^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}}\mathit{sk}+bR_{k}R^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}}}))^{(R_{k}-R^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}})^{-1}}

\displaystyle=(\mathit{g}^{R_{k}\mathit{sk}^{\prime}-R^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}}\mathit{sk}})^{(R_{k}-R^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}})^{-1}}

\displaystyle=(\mathit{g}^{(R_{k}\mathit{sk}^{\prime}-R^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}}\mathit{sk})/(R_{k}-R^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}})})

Therefore,
B
computes
x\leftarrow(R_{k}\mathit{sk}^{\prime}-R^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}}\mathit{sk})/(R_{k}-R^{\prime}_{k^{\prime}})
to solve the discrete logarithm problem. If the adversary succeeds with probability
\alpha
,
B
succeeds with probability
\alpha/N_{u}
, where
N_{u}
is the number of public keys of honest users.
∎

\textbf{Game}\ 17
::

This game proceeds as
\textbf{Game}\ 16
, except that
\textbf{Game}\ 17
outputs failure when the serial number of a payment computed by an honest user is equal to a serial number of another payment. The probability that two serial numbers have the same value is bounded by
N_{s}/|\mathbb{G}|
, where
N_{s}
is the number of serial numbers and
|\mathbb{G}|
is the size of
\mathbb{G}
. Additionally, we show below that the probability that an adversarial user computes a payment with a serial number that is equal to a serial number in a payment computed by an honest user is negligible thanks to the hardness of the discrete logarithm problem. Therefore, we have that
|\Pr[\textbf{Game}\ 17]-\Pr[\textbf{Game}\ 16]|\allowbreak\leq\allowbreak N_{s}/|\mathbb{G}|+N_{s}\allowbreak\cdot\allowbreak\textrm{Adv}_{\mathcal{A}}^{\mathsf{dlog}}
.

Proof.

Given an adversary that makes
\textbf{Game}\ 17
fail with non-negligible probability, we construct an algorithm
B
that solves the discrete logarithm problem with non-negligible probability.
B
works as follows.
B
receives an instance
(h,\allowbreak h^{x})
of the discrete logarithm problem from the challenger. When running
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{KG}}
,
B
sets
\delta\allowbreak\leftarrow h
and sets the parameters
\mathit{params}\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak(\mathit{p},\allowbreak\mathbb{G},\allowbreak\mathbb{\tilde{G}},\allowbreak{\mathbb{G}_{t}},\allowbreak\mathit{e},\allowbreak\mathit{g},\allowbreak\mathit{\tilde{g}},\allowbreak\gamma_{1},\allowbreak\gamma_{2},\allowbreak\delta,\allowbreak L)
.
B
picks randomly a serial number in a payment computed by an honest user and sets
S_{k}\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak h^{x}
. We recall that, since
\textbf{Game}\ 14
, serial numbers in payments computed by honest users are random values in
\mathbb{G}
.

At some point,
B
finds that a payment received from the adversary has a serial number that is equal to a serial number in a payment computed by an honest user. If that serial number is not equal to
h^{x}
,
B
fails. Otherwise
B
extracts the witness
\langle\mathit{sk},\allowbreak\mathit{v},\allowbreak r,\allowbreak o_{c},\allowbreak\langle l_{k},\allowbreak o_{a_{k}},\allowbreak\mu_{k},\allowbreak o_{\mu_{k}}\rangle_{k=0}^{V-1}\rangle)
from the proof
\pi_{v}
in the payment
\mathit{pay}
sent by the adversary. Thanks to the proof in
\textbf{Game}\ 6
, we know that the serial number
S_{k}\allowbreak=\allowbreak h^{x}
in
\mathit{pay}
is of the form
S_{k}\allowbreak=\allowbreak\delta^{1/(\mathit{v}+l_{k}+1)}
. Therefore,
B
outputs
x\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak 1/(\mathit{v}+l_{k}+1)
. If the adversary succeeds with probability
\alpha
,
B
succeeds with probability
\alpha/N_{s}
, where
N_{s}
is the number of serial numbers.
∎

The distribution of
\textbf{Game}\ 17
is identical to that of our simulation. In
\textbf{Game}\ 17
, the request message is computed without knowledge of the values
\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}}
and
\mathit{v}
. The payment is computed without knowledge of the signatures and without knowledge of the signed messages
\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}}
and
\mathit{v}
. Additionally, it is guaranteed that the adversary cannot compute a payment on a wallet defined by
\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}}
and
\mathit{v}
unless the adversary obtained enough signatures from honest authorities. It is also guaranteed that, if the adversary double spends coins, then an adversarial user or provider will be identified. Moreover, it is guaranteed that an honest user or provider will not be found guilty of double spending. The overall advantage of the environment to distinguish between the real and the ideal protocol is
|\Pr[\textbf{Game}\ 17]-\Pr[\textbf{Game}\ 0]|\leq\textrm{Adv}_{\mathcal{A}}^{\mathsf{ext}}+\textrm{Adv}_{\mathcal{A}}^{\mathsf{bin}}+\textrm{Adv}_{\mathcal{A}}^{\mathsf{unf}}\cdot((n-\tilde{t})!/((t-1-\tilde{t})!(n-t+1)!))+\textrm{Adv}_{\mathcal{A}}^{\mathsf{zk}}+(N_{u}+N_{s}+1)\cdot\textrm{Adv}_{\mathcal{A}}^{\mathsf{dlog}}+N_{s}/|\mathbb{G}|+\textrm{Adv}_{\mathcal{A}}^{\mathsf{pseu}}+N_{s}\cdot\textrm{Adv}_{\mathcal{A}}^{\mathsf{xdh}}+\textrm{Adv}_{\mathcal{A}}^{\mathsf{col-res}}
, where
N_{u}
is the number of users and
N_{s}
is the number of serial numbers. The discrete logarithm assumption are implied by the DDHI assumption, which is used to prove that the function in §
A.6
is pseudorandom. This concludes the proof of Theorem
D.1
.

Appendix E
Security Proof for Our Divisible E-Cash Scheme

In §
D
, we provide a detailed security proof for our compact
\mathrm{EC}
scheme. In this section, we analyze the security of our divisible
\mathrm{EC}
scheme, but we omit a full proof. Instead, we discuss the points where the security analysis of our divisible
\mathrm{EC}
scheme differs from the analysis of our compact
\mathrm{EC}
scheme.

For the interfaces
\mathsf{ec.setup}
,
\mathsf{ec.register}
,
\mathsf{ec.request}
, and
\mathsf{ec.issue}
, the simulator for the divisible
\mathrm{EC}
scheme is equal to that of our compact
\mathrm{EC}
scheme. We recall that both schemes use the same algorithms for the
\mathsf{ec.register}
,
\mathsf{ec.request}
and
\mathsf{ec.issue}
interfaces. For the
\mathsf{ec.setup}
interface, although algorithm
\mathsf{Setup}
is different in our schemes, the algorithm is run in both cases by
\mathcal{F}_{\mathrm{KG}}
, as specified in our simulation.

In the spending phase, the simulator for the divisible
\mathrm{EC}
scheme, like in the compact
\mathrm{EC}
scheme, outputs failure when the adversary submits a payment such that the simulator fails to extract a valid PS signature, or when the adversary did not receive signatures from
t-\tilde{t}
authorities on the signed messages. Additionally, the simulator for the divisible
\mathrm{EC}
scheme outputs failure if it is unable to extract a SPS signature on
(\varsigma_{l+V-1},\allowbreak\theta_{l+V-1})
, where
(\varsigma_{l+V-1},\allowbreak\theta_{l+V-1})
are part of the public parameters. It also outputs failure if the extracted
(\varsigma_{l},\allowbreak\theta_{l})
are not part of the public parameters. In the security proof, the probability that extraction fails is negligible thanks to the weak simulation extractability property of the ZK argument
\pi_{v}
, the existential unforgeability of the SPS signature scheme, and the BDHI assumption. All these arguments ensure that
\phi_{V,l}
and
\varphi_{V,l}
are computed correctly.

In the spending phase, to simulate a payment
(\kappa,\allowbreak\sigma^{\prime},\allowbreak\phi_{V,l},\allowbreak\varphi_{V,l},\allowbreak R,\allowbreak\pi_{v},\allowbreak V)
, the values
\kappa
and
\sigma^{\prime}
for the proof of possession of a PS signature are simulated like in the simulator for our compact
\mathrm{EC}
scheme (see Figure
4
). The proof
\pi_{v}
is also simulated by using the simulator
S_{v}
. The values
\phi_{V,l}
and
\varphi_{V,l}
are set to random. In the security proof, it can be shown that a simulated payment is indistinguishable from an honestly computed payment under the
N
-MXDH’ assumption. The proof is similar to one given in
(
Pointcheval et al. 2017
)
. We remark that, in the proof in
(
Pointcheval et al. 2017
)
, an element of the
N
-MXDH’ instance is needed to simulate the request message because the element
U_{2}=u_{2}^{\mathit{v}}
is revealed to the bank, whereas in our case that is not needed thanks to the hiding property of the commitment scheme.

In the deposit phase, the behavior of both simulators is similar, taking into account that the serial numbers and double spending tags are computed differently. The simulator also outputs failure if double spending happened but the identification algorithm does not identify the double spender. In the security proof, after it is ensured, as described above, that the ElGamal encryptions
\phi_{V,l}
and
\varphi_{V,l}
sent by the adversary are computed correctly, we know that serial numbers and double spending tags can be retrieved. Then we can prove, as in the case of the compact
\mathrm{EC}
scheme, that the user guilty of double spending can be identified under the collision resistance property of the hash function
H^{\prime}
.

The simulator also outputs failure when there is not double spending, but an honest user is found guilty. In the security proof, like in our
\mathrm{EC}
scheme, we can show that the simulator fails with negligible probability under the hardness of the discrete logarithm problem.

Appendix F
Compact E-Cash with Range Proof Instantiation

The zero-knowledge argument of knowledge in algorithm
\mathsf{Spend}
involves a statement
l\allowbreak\in\allowbreak[0,L-1]
to prove the validity of the index of the spent coin. To implement it, one option is to use the set membership proof described in
(
Camenisch et al. 2008
)
. This proof consists in proving possession of a signature that signs the index
l
. We use the Pointcheval-Sanders signature scheme to instantiate it. Algorithm
\mathsf{Setup}
is extended to compute a signing key pair and signatures on all the values in the range
[0,\allowbreak L-1]
. Algorithm
\mathsf{Spend}
is extended to include a zero-knowledge argument of knowledge of the signature that signs
l
, and algorithm
\mathsf{SpendVf}
is extended to verify it. The modified algorithms work as follows:

\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\mathit{k}},L)
.:

Execute the following steps:

•:

Run
(\mathit{p},\mathbb{G},\mathbb{\tilde{G}},\allowbreak{\mathbb{G}_{t}},\mathit{e},\mathit{g},\mathit{\tilde{g}})\leftarrow\mathcal{G}(1^{\mathit{k}})
.

•:

Pick
3
random generators
(\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\delta)\leftarrow\mathbb{G}
.

•:

Run
(\mathit{sk},\mathit{pk})\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak\mathsf{KeyGen}(1^{\mathit{k}},\allowbreak 1)
, i.e., pick random secret key
(x,\allowbreak y)\leftarrow\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{2}
and output the secret key
\mathit{sk}=(x,\allowbreak y)
and the public key
\mathit{pk}=(\tilde{\alpha}_{sm},\tilde{\beta}_{sm})\leftarrow(\mathit{\tilde{g}}^{x},\allowbreak\mathit{\tilde{g}}^{y})
.

•:

For all
l\allowbreak\in\allowbreak[0,L-1]
, compute
\mathit{\sigma}_{l}\leftarrow\mathsf{Sign}(\mathit{sk},\allowbreak l)
, i.e., pick random
r_{l}\leftarrow\mathbb{Z}_{p}
, set
h_{l}\leftarrow\mathit{g}^{r_{l}}
and output the signature
\mathit{\sigma}_{l}=(h_{l},s_{l})\leftarrow(h_{l},h_{l}^{x+yl})
.

•:

Set the parameters
\mathit{params}\leftarrow(\mathit{p},\mathbb{G},\mathbb{\tilde{G}},\allowbreak{\mathbb{G}_{t}},\mathit{e},\mathit{g},\mathit{\tilde{g}},\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2},\allowbreak\delta,\allowbreak\tilde{\alpha}_{sm},\allowbreak\tilde{\beta}_{sm},\allowbreak\mathit{\sigma}_{0},\allowbreak\ldots,\allowbreak\mathit{\sigma}_{L-1},\allowbreak L)
.

•:

Output
\mathit{params}
.

\mathsf{Spend}(\mathit{pk},\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}},\mathit{W},\mathit{payinfo},V)
.:

Execute the following steps:

•:

Parse
\mathit{W}
as
(\sigma,\allowbreak\mathit{v},\allowbreak l)
. If
l+V-1\geq L
, output
0
.

•:

Parse
\sigma
as
(\mathit{h},\allowbreak s)
.

•:

Parse
\mathit{pk}
as
(\mathit{params},\allowbreak\tilde{\alpha},\allowbreak\beta_{1},\allowbreak\tilde{\beta}_{1},\beta_{2},\tilde{\beta}_{2})
.

•:

Pick random
r\leftarrow\mathbb{Z}_{p}
and
r^{\prime}\leftarrow\mathbb{Z}_{p}
.

•:

Compute
\sigma^{\prime}=(\mathit{h}^{\prime},s^{\prime})\leftarrow(\mathit{h}^{r^{\prime}},s^{r^{\prime}}(\mathit{h}^{\prime})^{r})
.

•:

Compute
\kappa\leftarrow\tilde{\alpha}\tilde{\beta}_{1}^{\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}}}\tilde{\beta}_{2}^{\mathit{v}}\mathit{\tilde{g}}^{r}
.

•:

Pick random
o_{c}\leftarrow\mathbb{Z}_{p}
and compute the commitment
C\leftarrow\mathit{g}^{o_{c}}\gamma_{1}^{\mathit{v}}
.

•:

For
k\in[0,V-1]
, compute
R_{k}\leftarrow H^{\prime}(\mathit{payinfo},k)
, where
\mathit{payinfo}
must contain the identifier of the merchant, and
H^{\prime}
is a collision-resistant hash function.

•:

For
k\in[0,V-1]
, set
l_{k}\leftarrow l+k
, pick random
o_{a_{k}}
and compute
A_{k}=\mathit{g}^{o_{a_{k}}}\gamma_{1}^{l_{k}}
.

•:

For
k\in[0,V-1]
, evaluate the pseudorandom functions
S_{k}\leftarrow f_{\delta,\mathit{v}}(l_{k})=\delta^{1/(\mathit{v}+l_{k}+1)}
and
T_{k}\leftarrow\mathit{g}^{\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}}}(f_{\mathit{g},\mathit{v}}(l_{k}))^{R_{k}}=\mathit{g}^{\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}}+R_{k}/(\mathit{v}+l_{k}+1)}
.

•:

For
k\in[0,V-1]
, compute the values
\mu_{k}\leftarrow 1/(\mathit{v}+l_{k}+1)
and
o_{\mu_{k}}\leftarrow-(o_{a_{k}}+o_{c})\mu_{k}
.

•:

Parse
\mathit{params}
as the tuple
(\mathit{p},\mathbb{G},\mathbb{\tilde{G}},\allowbreak{\mathbb{G}_{t}},\mathit{e},\mathit{g},\mathit{\tilde{g}},\gamma_{1},\allowbreak\gamma_{2},\allowbreak\delta,\allowbreak\tilde{\alpha}_{sm},\allowbreak\tilde{\beta}_{sm},\allowbreak\mathit{\sigma}_{0},\allowbreak\ldots,\allowbreak\mathit{\sigma}_{L-1},\allowbreak L)
.

•:

For
k\in[0,V-1]
, pick random
r_{k}\leftarrow\mathbb{Z}_{p}
and
r^{\prime}_{k}\leftarrow\mathbb{Z}_{p}
.

•:

For
k\in[0,V-1]
, parse
\mathit{\sigma}_{l_{k}}
as
(\mathit{h}_{l_{k}},\allowbreak s_{l_{k}})
. Compute
\sigma^{\prime}_{l_{k}}=(\mathit{h}^{\prime}_{l_{k}},s^{\prime}_{l_{k}})\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak(\mathit{h}_{l_{k}}^{r^{\prime}_{k}},s_{l_{k}}^{r^{\prime}_{k}}(\mathit{h}^{\prime}_{l_{k}})^{r_{k}})
.

•:

For
k\in[0,V-1]
, compute
\kappa_{k}\leftarrow\tilde{\alpha}_{sm}\tilde{\beta}_{sm}^{l_{k}}\mathit{\tilde{g}}^{r_{k}}
.

•:

Compute a ZK argument of knowledge
\pi_{v}
via the Fiat-Shamir heuristic for the following relation:

\displaystyle\pi_{v}=

\displaystyle\mathrm{NIZK}\{(\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}},\mathit{v},r,o_{c},\langle l_{k},r_{k},o_{a_{k}},\mu_{k},o_{\mu_{k}}\rangle_{k=0}^{V-1}):

\displaystyle\kappa=\tilde{\alpha}\tilde{\beta}_{1}^{\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}}}\tilde{\beta}_{2}^{\mathit{v}}\mathit{\tilde{g}}^{r}\ \land\ C=\mathit{g}^{o_{c}}\gamma_{1}^{\mathit{v}}\ \land\

\displaystyle\langle A_{k}=\mathit{g}^{o_{a_{k}}}\gamma_{1}^{l_{k}}\ \land\ \kappa_{k}=\tilde{\alpha}_{sm}\tilde{\beta}_{sm}^{l_{k}}\mathit{\tilde{g}}^{r_{k}}\land\

\displaystyle S_{k}=\delta^{\mu_{k}}\ \land\ \gamma_{1}=(A_{k}C\gamma_{1})^{\mu_{k}}\mathit{g}^{o_{\mu_{k}}}\ \land\

\displaystyle T_{k}=\mathit{g}^{\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}}}(\mathit{g}^{R_{k}})^{\mu_{k}}\ \rangle_{k\in[0,V-1]}\}

The equation
\kappa=\tilde{\alpha}\tilde{\beta}_{1}^{\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}}}\tilde{\beta}_{2}^{\mathit{v}}\mathit{\tilde{g}}^{r}
proves that
\kappa
commits to the signed messages. (
\kappa
is used as part of the proof of signature possession.) The equation
C=\mathit{g}^{o_{c}}\gamma_{1}^{\mathit{v}}
proves that
C
commits to the same value
\mathit{v}
committed in
\kappa
. The equations
A_{k}=\mathit{g}^{o_{a_{k}}}\gamma_{1}^{l_{k}}
and
\kappa_{k}=\tilde{\alpha}_{sm}\tilde{\beta}_{sm}^{l_{k}}\mathit{\tilde{g}}^{r_{k}}
prove that the value
l_{k}
committed in
A_{k}
is in the valid range
[0,L-1]
for a wallet with
L
coins. The equations
S_{k}=\delta^{\mu_{k}}
,
\gamma_{1}=(A_{k}C\gamma_{1})^{\mu_{k}}\mathit{g}^{o_{\mu_{k}}}
and
T_{k}=\mathit{g}^{\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}}}(\mathit{g}^{R_{k}})^{\mu_{k}}
prove that the serial numbers
S_{k}
and the security tags
T_{k}
are correctly computed. This non-interactive argument signs the payment information
\mathit{payinfo}
.

•:

Output
\mathit{pay}\leftarrow(\kappa,\sigma^{\prime},\langle S_{k},T_{k},A_{k},\kappa_{k},\sigma^{\prime}_{l+k}\rangle_{k\in[0,V-1]},V,C,\allowbreak\pi_{v})
and an updated wallet
\mathit{W}^{\prime}\leftarrow(\sigma,\allowbreak\mathit{v},\allowbreak l+V)
.

\mathsf{SpendVf}(\mathit{pk},\mathit{pay},\mathit{payinfo})
.:

Execute the following steps:

•:

Parse
\mathit{pk}
as
(\mathit{params},\allowbreak\tilde{\alpha},\allowbreak\beta_{1},\allowbreak\tilde{\beta}_{1},\beta_{2},\tilde{\beta}_{2})
.

•:

Parse
\mathit{pay}
as
(\kappa,\sigma^{\prime},\langle S_{k},T_{k},A_{k},\kappa_{k},\sigma^{\prime}_{l_{k}}\rangle_{k\in[0,V-1]},V,C,\pi_{v})
.

•:

Parse
\sigma^{\prime}
as
(\mathit{h}^{\prime},\allowbreak s^{\prime})
and output
0
if
\mathit{h}^{\prime}=1
or if
\mathit{e}(\mathit{h}^{\prime},\kappa)\allowbreak=\allowbreak\mathit{e}(s^{\prime},\mathit{\tilde{g}})
does not hold.

•:

For
k\in[0,V-1]
, parse
\sigma^{\prime}_{l_{k}}
as
(\mathit{h}^{\prime}_{l_{k}},\allowbreak s^{\prime}_{l_{k}})
and output
0
if
\mathit{h}^{\prime}_{l_{k}}=1
or if
\mathit{e}(\mathit{h}^{\prime}_{l_{k}},\kappa_{k})\allowbreak=\allowbreak\mathit{e}(s^{\prime}_{l_{k}},\mathit{\tilde{g}})
does not hold.

•:

Output
0
if not all the serial numbers
\langle S_{k}\rangle_{k\in[0,V-1]}
are different from each other.

•:

For
k\in[0,V-1]
, compute
R_{k}\leftarrow H^{\prime}(\mathit{payinfo},k)
.

•:

Verify
\pi_{v}
by using
\mathit{payinfo}
,
\mathit{pk}
,
\langle S_{k},T_{k},A_{k},R_{k},\kappa_{k}\rangle_{k\in[0,V-1]}
,
C
and
\kappa
. Output
0
if the proof is not correct, else output
V
.

Cost reduction by using one secret in the wallet.

We quantify the reduction of costs attained by having a wallet that signs one secret instead of two (as done in the compact e-cash scheme in
(
Camenisch et al. 2005
)
), in addition to the user secret key. First, we analyze the communication and storage costs. Let
|\mathbb{G}|
,
|\mathbb{\tilde{G}}|
and
|\mathbb{Z}_{p}|
denote the bit size of elements in
\mathbb{G}
,
\mathbb{\tilde{G}}
and
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
respectively. The size of the public parameters does not change. The size of public keys of authorities is
2|\mathbb{G}|+3|\mathbb{\tilde{G}}|
with one secret and
3|\mathbb{G}|+4|\mathbb{\tilde{G}}|
with two secrets. The size of the secret key of authorities is
3|\mathbb{Z}_{p}|
with one secret and
4|\mathbb{Z}_{p}|
with two secrets. In the withdrawal phase, the size of a response does not change, but the size of a request is
8|\mathbb{G}|+6|\mathbb{Z}_{p}|
with one secret and
10|\mathbb{G}|+8|\mathbb{Z}_{p}|
with two secrets. The wallet size is
2|\mathbb{G}|+1|\mathbb{Z}_{p}|
with one secret and
2|\mathbb{G}|+2|\mathbb{Z}_{p}|
with two secrets. The size of a payment of
V
coins is
(2+5V+1)|\mathbb{G}|+(1+V)|\mathbb{\tilde{G}}|+(5+5V)|\mathbb{Z}_{p}|
with one secret and
(2+5V+2)|\mathbb{G}|+(1+V)|\mathbb{\tilde{G}}|+(7+7V)|\mathbb{Z}_{p}|
with two secrets.

Second, we analyze the computation cost. We remark that the number of bilinear map computations does not change. Let
|M|
and
|E|
denote the cost of a multi-exponentiation and of a exponentiation respectively. In a withdrawal phase in which the user contacts
t
authorities, the total cost is
9|M|+(4+7t)|E|
with one secret and
12|M|+(5+10t)|E|
with two secrets. In a spending phase in which
V
coins are spent, the total cost is
(6+11V)|M|+(4+5V)|E|
with one secret and
(9+13V)|M|+(5+5V)|E|
with two secrets. The cost of the deposit phase does not change.

Appendix G
Complete Description of the Divisible E-Cash Scheme

The zero-knowledge argument of knowledge in algorithm
\mathsf{Spend}
in §
5.2
involves proving knowledge of secret bases. For this purpose, the transformation described in §
A.3
needs to be applied. In algorithm
\mathsf{Setup}
, the generators
\psi\allowbreak\in\allowbreak\mathbb{G}
and
\tilde{\psi}\allowbreak\in\allowbreak\mathbb{\tilde{G}}
are added. In algorithm
\mathsf{Spend}
, the secret bases are blinded, and the zero-knowledge argument is modified accordingly. We describe below the modified algorithms
\mathsf{Setup}
,
\mathsf{Spend}
and
\mathsf{SpendVf}
. Moreover, we also describe the algorithms
\mathsf{KeyGenV}
,
\mathsf{KeyGenU}
,
\mathsf{Request}
,
\mathsf{RequestVf}
,
\mathsf{Issue}
,
\mathsf{IssueVf}
and
\mathsf{AggrWallet}
, which were not depicted in §
5.2
.

\mathsf{Setup}(1^{\mathit{k}},L)
.:

Execute the following steps:

•:

Run
\mathit{grp}=(\mathit{p},\mathbb{G},\mathbb{\tilde{G}},\allowbreak{\mathbb{G}_{t}},\mathit{e},\mathit{g},\mathit{\tilde{g}})\leftarrow\mathcal{G}(1^{\mathit{k}})
.

•:

Pick random generators
\eta,\allowbreak\gamma_{1},\allowbreak\gamma_{2},\psi\in\mathbb{G}
and
\tilde{\psi}\in\mathbb{\tilde{G}}
.

•:

Generate random scalars
(z,y)\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak\mathbb{Z}_{p}
and, for
l\allowbreak\in\allowbreak[1,L]
,
a_{l}\leftarrow\mathbb{Z}_{p}
.

•:

Compute
(\varsigma,\theta)\leftarrow(\mathit{g}^{z},\eta^{z})
.

•:

For
l\allowbreak\in\allowbreak[1,L]
, compute
(\varsigma_{l},\theta_{l})\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak(\varsigma^{y^{l}},\allowbreak\theta^{y^{l}})
.

•:

For
k\in[0,L-1]
, compute
\tilde{\delta}_{k}\leftarrow\mathit{\tilde{g}}^{y^{k}}
.

•:

For
l\allowbreak\in\allowbreak[1,L]
, compute
\eta_{l}\leftarrow\mathit{g}^{a_{l}}
.

•:

For
l\allowbreak\in\allowbreak[1,L]
, for
k\allowbreak\in\allowbreak[0,\allowbreak l-1]
, compute
\tilde{\eta}_{l,k}\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak\mathit{\tilde{g}}^{-a_{l}\cdot y^{k}}
.

•:

Run the algorithm
(\mathit{pk}_{sps},\allowbreak\mathit{sk}_{sps})\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak\mathsf{KeyGen}(\mathit{grp},\allowbreak 2,\allowbreak 0)
of the structure-preserving signature scheme in §
A.5
.

•:

For
l\allowbreak\in\allowbreak[1,L]
, compute
\tau_{l}\leftarrow\mathsf{Sign}(\mathit{sk}_{sps},\langle\varsigma_{l},\theta_{l}\rangle)
.

•:

Set the parameters for users
\mathit{params}_{u}\leftarrow(\mathit{p},\mathbb{G},\mathbb{\tilde{G}},\allowbreak{\mathbb{G}_{t}},\mathit{e},\mathit{g},\mathit{\tilde{g}},\allowbreak\eta,\allowbreak\gamma_{1},\allowbreak\gamma_{2},\allowbreak\{\eta_{l},\allowbreak\varsigma_{l},\allowbreak\theta_{l},\allowbreak\tau_{l}\}_{l=1}^{L},\allowbreak\mathit{pk}_{sps},\allowbreak\psi,\allowbreak\tilde{\psi})
. Set the additional parameters for authorities
\mathit{params}_{a}\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak(\{\tilde{\delta}_{k}\}_{k=0}^{L-1},\allowbreak\{\langle\tilde{\eta}_{l,k}\rangle_{k=0}^{l-1}\}_{l=1}^{L-1})
.

•:

Set the parameters
\mathit{params}\leftarrow(\mathit{params}_{u},\mathit{params}_{a})
.

•:

Output
\mathit{params}
.

\mathsf{KeyGenV}(\mathit{params}_{u},t,n)
.:

Execute the following steps:

•:

Choose
(1+2)
polynomials
(v,w_{1},w_{2})
of degree
(t-1)
with random coefficients in
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
.

•:

Set
(x,y_{1},y_{2})\leftarrow(v(0),w_{1}(0),w_{2}(0))
.

•:

For
i=1
to
n
, set the secret key
\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{V}_{i}}
of each authority
\mathcal{V}_{i}
as
\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{V}_{i}}\allowbreak=\allowbreak(x_{i},\allowbreak y_{i,1},\allowbreak y_{i,2})\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak(v(i),\allowbreak w_{1}(i),\allowbreak w_{2}(i))
.

•:

For
i=1
to
n
, set the verification key
\mathit{pk}_{\mathcal{V}_{i}}
of each authority
\mathcal{V}_{i}
as
\mathit{pk}_{\mathcal{V}_{i}}\allowbreak=\allowbreak(\tilde{\alpha}_{i},\allowbreak\beta_{i,1},\allowbreak\tilde{\beta}_{i,1},\allowbreak\beta_{i,2},\allowbreak\tilde{\beta}_{i,2})\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak(\mathit{\tilde{g}}^{x_{i}},\allowbreak\mathit{g}^{y_{i,1}},\allowbreak\mathit{\tilde{g}}^{y_{i,1}},\allowbreak\mathit{g}^{y_{i,2}},\allowbreak\mathit{\tilde{g}}^{y_{i,2}})
.

•:

Compute the verification key
\mathit{pk}=(\mathit{params}_{u},\allowbreak\tilde{\alpha},\allowbreak\beta_{1},\allowbreak\tilde{\beta}_{1},\allowbreak\beta_{2},\tilde{\beta}_{2})\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak(\mathit{params}_{u},\allowbreak\mathit{\tilde{g}}^{x},\allowbreak\mathit{g}^{y_{1}},\allowbreak\mathit{\tilde{g}}^{y_{1}},\allowbreak\mathit{g}^{y_{2}},\allowbreak\mathit{\tilde{g}}^{y_{2}})
.

•:

Output
(\mathit{pk},\langle\mathit{pk}_{\mathcal{V}_{i}},\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{V}_{i}}\rangle_{i=1}^{n})
.

\mathsf{KeyGenU}(\mathit{params}_{u})
.:

Execute the following steps:

•:

Pick random
\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}}\leftarrow\mathbb{Z}_{p}
and compute
\mathit{pk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}}\leftarrow\mathit{g}^{\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}}}
.

•:

Output
(\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}},\allowbreak\mathit{pk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}})
.

\mathsf{Request}(\mathit{params}_{u},\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}})
.:

Execute the following steps:

•:

Pick random
\mathit{v}\leftarrow\mathbb{Z}_{p}
and set
(\mathit{m}_{1},\allowbreak\mathit{m}_{2})\allowbreak=(\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}},\allowbreak\mathit{v})
.

•:

Pick random
o\leftarrow\mathbb{Z}_{p}
and compute
\mathit{com}=\mathit{g}^{o}\prod_{j=1}^{2}\gamma_{j}^{\mathit{m}_{j}}
.

•:

Compute
\mathit{h}\leftarrow H(\mathit{com})
, where
H
is modeled as a random oracle.

•:

Compute commitments to each of the messages. For
j=1
to
2
, pick random
o_{j}\leftarrow\mathbb{Z}_{p}
and set
\mathit{com}_{j}=\mathit{g}^{o_{j}}\mathit{h}^{\mathit{m}_{j}}
.

•:

Compute a ZK argument of knowledge
\pi_{s}
via the Fiat-Shamir heuristic for the following relation:

\displaystyle\pi_{s}=

\displaystyle\mathrm{NIZK}\{(\mathit{m}_{1},\mathit{m}_{2},o,o_{1},o_{2}):

\displaystyle\mathit{com}=\mathit{g}^{o}\prod_{j=1}^{2}\gamma_{j}^{\mathit{m}_{j}}\ \land\ \mathit{pk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}}\leftarrow\mathit{g}^{\mathit{m}_{1}}\land\

\displaystyle\{\mathit{com}_{j}=\mathit{g}^{o_{j}}\mathit{h}^{\mathit{m}_{j}}\}_{\forall j\in[1,2]}\}

•:

Set
\mathit{reqinfo}\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak(\mathit{h},\allowbreak o_{1},\allowbreak o_{2},\allowbreak\mathit{v})
.

•:

Set
\mathit{req}\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak(\mathit{h},\allowbreak\mathit{com},\allowbreak\mathit{com}_{1},\allowbreak\mathit{com}_{2},\allowbreak\pi_{s})
.

•:

Output
\mathit{req}
and
\mathit{reqinfo}
.

\mathsf{RequestVf}(\mathit{params}_{u},\mathit{req},\mathit{pk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}})
.:

Execute the following steps:

•:

Parse
\mathit{req}
as
(\mathit{h},\allowbreak\mathit{com},\allowbreak\mathit{com}_{1},\allowbreak\mathit{com}_{2},\allowbreak\pi_{s})
.

•:

Compute
\mathit{h}^{\prime}\leftarrow H(\mathit{com})
, where
H
is modeled as a random oracle. Output
0
if
\mathit{h}\neq\mathit{h}^{\prime}
.

•:

Verify
\pi_{s}
by using the tuple
(\mathit{params}_{u},\allowbreak\mathit{h},\allowbreak\mathit{com},\allowbreak\mathit{com}_{1},\allowbreak\mathit{com}_{2})
. Output
0
if the proof
\pi_{s}
is not correct, else output
1
.

\mathsf{Issue}(\mathit{params}_{u},\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{V}_{i}},\mathit{req})
.:

Execute the following steps:

•:

Parse
\mathit{req}
as
(\mathit{h},\allowbreak\mathit{com},\allowbreak\mathit{com}_{1},\allowbreak\mathit{com}_{2},\allowbreak\pi_{s})
.

•:

Parse
\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{V}_{i}}
as
(x_{i},\allowbreak y_{i,1},\allowbreak y_{i,2})
.

•:

Compute
c=\mathit{h}^{x_{i}}\prod_{j=1}^{2}\mathit{com}_{j}^{y_{i,j}}
.

•:

Set the blinded signature share
\hat{\sigma}_{i}\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak(\mathit{h},\allowbreak c)
.

•:

Output
\mathit{res}\leftarrow\hat{\sigma}_{i}
.

\mathsf{IssueVf}(\mathit{params}_{u},\mathit{pk}_{\mathcal{V}_{i}},\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}},\mathit{res},\mathit{reqinfo})
.:

Execute the following steps:

•:

Parse
\mathit{reqinfo}
as
(\mathit{h}^{\prime},\allowbreak o_{1},\allowbreak o_{2},\allowbreak\mathit{v})
.

•:

Parse
\mathit{res}
as
\hat{\sigma}_{i}\allowbreak=\allowbreak(\mathit{h},\allowbreak c)
. Output
0
if
\mathit{h}\allowbreak\neq\allowbreak\mathit{h}^{\prime}
.

•:

Parse
\mathit{pk}_{\mathcal{V}_{i}}
as
(\tilde{\alpha}_{i},\allowbreak\beta_{i,1},\allowbreak\tilde{\beta}_{i,1},\allowbreak\beta_{i,2},\allowbreak\tilde{\beta}_{i,2})
.

•:

Compute
\sigma_{i}\allowbreak=\allowbreak(\mathit{h},\allowbreak s)\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak(\mathit{h},\allowbreak c\prod_{j=1}^{2}\beta_{i,j}^{-o_{j}})
.

•:

Set
(\mathit{m}_{1},\mathit{m}_{2})=(\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}},\mathit{v})
. Output
0
if
\mathit{e}(\mathit{h},\tilde{\alpha}_{i}\prod_{j=1}^{2}\tilde{\beta}_{i,j}^{\mathit{m}_{j}})=\mathit{e}(s,\mathit{\tilde{g}})
does not hold.

•:

Output
\mathit{W}_{i}\leftarrow(i,\sigma_{i},\mathit{v})
.

\mathsf{AggrWallet}(\mathit{pk},\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}},\mathit{\mathbb{S}},\langle\mathit{W}_{i}\rangle_{i\in\mathit{\mathbb{S}}})
.:

Execute the following steps:

•:

If
|\mathit{\mathbb{S}}|\neq t
, output
0
.

•:

For all
i\in\mathit{\mathbb{S}}
, evaluate at 0 the Lagrange basis polynomials

l_{i}=[\prod_{j\in\mathit{\mathbb{S}},j\neq i}(0-j)][\prod_{j\in\mathit{\mathbb{S}},j\neq i}(i-j)]^{-1}\ \mathrm{mod}\ \mathit{p}

•:

For all
i\in\mathit{\mathbb{S}}
, parse
\mathit{W}_{i}
as
(i,\sigma_{i},\mathit{v})
and
\sigma_{i}
as
(\mathit{h},\allowbreak s_{i})
.

•:

Compute the signature
\sigma=(\mathit{h},s)\leftarrow(\mathit{h},\allowbreak\prod_{i\in\mathit{\mathbb{S}}}s_{i}^{l_{i}})
.

•:

Parse
\mathit{pk}
as
(\mathit{params}_{u},\allowbreak\tilde{\alpha},\allowbreak\beta_{1},\allowbreak\tilde{\beta}_{1},\beta_{2},\tilde{\beta}_{2})
.

•:

Set
(\mathit{m}_{1},\allowbreak\mathit{m}_{2})\allowbreak=\allowbreak(\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}},\allowbreak\mathit{v})
and output
0
if
\mathit{e}(\mathit{h},\tilde{\alpha}\prod_{j=1}^{2}\tilde{\beta}_{j}^{\mathit{m}_{j}})=\mathit{e}(s,\mathit{\tilde{g}})
does not hold, else output
\mathit{W}\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak(\sigma,\allowbreak\mathit{v},l)
, where
l
is a counter from
1
to
L
initialized to
1
.

\mathsf{Spend}(\mathit{pk},\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}},\mathit{W},\mathit{payinfo},V)
.:

Execute the following steps:

•:

Parse
\mathit{W}
as
(\sigma,\allowbreak\mathit{v},\allowbreak l)
. If
l+V\geq L
, output
0
.

•:

Parse
\sigma
as
(\mathit{h},\allowbreak s)
.

•:

Parse
\mathit{pk}
as
(\mathit{params}_{u},\allowbreak\tilde{\alpha},\allowbreak\beta_{1},\allowbreak\tilde{\beta}_{1},\beta_{2},\tilde{\beta}_{2})
.

•:

Pick random scalars
r\leftarrow\mathbb{Z}_{p}
and
r^{\prime}\leftarrow\mathbb{Z}_{p}
.

•:

Compute
\sigma^{\prime}=(\mathit{h}^{\prime},s^{\prime})\leftarrow(\mathit{h}^{r^{\prime}},s^{r^{\prime}}(\mathit{h}^{\prime})^{r})
.

•:

Compute
\kappa\leftarrow\tilde{\alpha}\tilde{\beta}_{1}^{\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}}}\tilde{\beta}_{2}^{\mathit{v}}\mathit{\tilde{g}}^{r}
.

•:

Pick random scalars
r_{1},r_{2}\leftarrow\mathbb{Z}_{p}
.

•:

Compute
\phi_{V,l}=(\phi_{V,l}[1],\phi_{V,l}[2])\leftarrow(\mathit{g}^{r_{1}},\varsigma_{l}^{\mathit{v}}\eta_{V}^{r_{1}})
.

•:

Set
R\leftarrow H(\mathit{payinfo})
, where
H
is a collision-resistant hash function, and set
\varphi_{V,l}=(\varphi_{V,l}[1],\varphi_{V,l}[2])\leftarrow(\mathit{g}^{r_{2}},(\mathit{g}^{R})^{\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}}}\theta_{l}^{\mathit{v}}\eta_{V}^{r_{2}})
.

•:

Take
\mathit{params}_{u}
from
\mathit{pk}
. Take the public key
\mathit{pk}_{sps}\allowbreak=\allowbreak(Y,\allowbreak W_{1},\allowbreak W_{2},\allowbreak Z)
and the signature
\tau_{l+V-1}\allowbreak=\allowbreak(R_{l+V-1},\allowbreak S_{l+V-1},\allowbreak T_{l+V-1})
.

•:

Pick random
(\rho_{\varsigma_{l}},\rho_{\theta_{l}},\rho_{\varsigma_{l+V-1}},\rho_{\theta_{l+V-1}},\rho_{R_{l+V-1}},\rho_{S_{l+V-1}},\rho_{T_{l+V-1}})\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak\mathbb{Z}_{p}
.

•:

Compute the blinded bases
\varsigma^{\prime}_{l}\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak\varsigma_{l}\psi^{\rho_{\varsigma_{l}}}
,
\theta^{\prime}_{l}\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak\theta_{l}\psi^{\rho_{\theta_{l}}}
,
\varsigma^{\prime}_{l+V-1}\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak\varsigma_{l+V-1}\psi^{\rho_{\varsigma_{l+V-1}}}
and
\theta^{\prime}_{l+V-1}\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak\theta_{l+V-1}\psi^{\rho_{\theta_{l+V-1}}}
, and the blinded bases for the signature
R^{\prime}_{l+V-1}\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak R_{l+V-1}\psi^{\rho_{R_{l+V-1}}}
,
S^{\prime}_{l+V-1}\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak S_{l+V-1}\psi^{\rho_{S_{l+V-1}}}
and
T^{\prime}_{l+V-1}\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak T_{l+V-1}\tilde{\psi}^{\rho_{T_{l+V-1}}}
.

•:

Compute
\rho_{1}\leftarrow-\mathit{v}\rho_{\varsigma_{l}}
,
\rho_{2}\leftarrow-\mathit{v}\rho_{\theta_{l}}
and
\rho_{3}\leftarrow\rho_{R_{l+V-1}}\rho_{T_{l+V-1}}
.

•:

Compute a ZK argument of knowledge
\pi_{v}
via the Fiat-Shamir heuristic for the following relation:

\displaystyle\pi_{v}=

\displaystyle\mathrm{NIZK}\{(\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}},\mathit{v},r,r_{1},r_{2},\rho_{\varsigma_{l}},\rho_{\theta_{l}},\rho_{\varsigma_{l+V-1}},\rho_{\theta_{l+V-1}},

\displaystyle\rho_{R_{l+V-1}},\rho_{S_{l+V-1}},\rho_{T_{l+V-1}},\rho_{1},\rho_{2},\rho_{3}):

(4)

\displaystyle\kappa=\tilde{\alpha}\tilde{\beta}_{1}^{\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}}}\tilde{\beta}_{2}^{\mathit{v}}\mathit{\tilde{g}}^{r}\ \land\

(5)

\displaystyle\phi_{V,l}[1]=\mathit{g}^{r_{1}}\ \land\ \phi_{V,l}[2]=(\varsigma^{\prime}_{l})^{\mathit{v}}\psi^{\rho_{1}}\eta_{V}^{r_{1}}\ \land\

(6)

\displaystyle\varphi_{V,l}[1]=\mathit{g}^{r_{2}}\ \land\ \varphi_{V,l}[2]=(\mathit{g}^{R})^{\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}}}(\theta^{\prime}_{l})^{\mathit{v}}\psi^{\rho_{2}}\eta_{V}^{r_{2}}\ \land\

(7)

\displaystyle\mathit{e}(\varsigma^{\prime}_{l},\tilde{\delta}_{V-1})\mathit{e}(\psi,\tilde{\delta}_{V-1})^{-\rho_{\varsigma_{l}}}=\mathit{e}(\varsigma^{\prime}_{l+V-1},\tilde{g})\mathit{e}(\psi,\tilde{g})^{-\rho_{\varsigma_{l+V-1}}}\ \land\

(8)

\displaystyle\mathit{e}(\theta^{\prime}_{l},\tilde{\delta}_{V-1})\mathit{e}(\psi,\tilde{\delta}_{V-1})^{-\rho_{\theta_{l}}}=\mathit{e}(\theta^{\prime}_{l+V-1},\tilde{g})\mathit{e}(\psi,\tilde{g})^{-\rho_{\theta_{l+V-1}}}\ \land\

\displaystyle\mathit{e}(R^{\prime}_{l+V-1},Y)\mathit{e}(\psi,Y)^{-\rho_{R_{l+V-1}}}\mathit{e}(S^{\prime}_{l+V-1},\mathit{\tilde{g}})\mathit{e}(\psi,\mathit{\tilde{g}})^{-\rho_{S_{l+V-1}}}\cdot

\displaystyle e(\varsigma^{\prime}_{l+V-1},W_{1})e(\psi,W_{1})^{-\rho_{\varsigma_{l+V-1}}}e(\theta^{\prime}_{l+V-1},W_{2})e(\psi,W_{2})^{-\rho_{\theta_{l+V-1}}}\cdot

(9)

\displaystyle\mathit{e}(\mathit{g},Z)^{-1}=1\ \land

\displaystyle\mathit{e}(R^{\prime}_{l+V-1},T^{\prime}_{l+V-1})\mathit{e}(R^{\prime}_{l+V-1},\tilde{\psi})^{-\rho_{T_{l+V-1}}}\cdot

(10)

\displaystyle\mathit{e}(\psi,T^{\prime}_{l+V-1})^{-\rho_{R_{l+V-1}}}\mathit{e}(\psi,\tilde{\psi})^{\rho_{3}}\mathit{e}(\mathit{g},\mathit{\tilde{g}})^{-1}=1

Equation
4
is part of the proof of possession of the wallet signature that signs
(\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}},\allowbreak\mathit{v})
. Equation
5
and
6
prove that
\phi_{V,l}
and the security tag
\varphi_{V,l}
are well-formed and are computed on input
(\mathit{sk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}},\allowbreak\mathit{v})
. Equation
7
,
8
,
9
and
10
prove that the values
\varsigma_{l}
and
\theta_{l}
, which were used to compute
\phi_{V,l}
and
\varphi_{V,l}
respectively, are part of the public parameters and fulfill
l\leq L-V+1
. This is accomplished by proving possession of a signature on
\varsigma_{l+V-1}
and
\theta_{l+V-1}
in equations
9
and
10
, and proving that the indices of
\varsigma_{l+V-1}
and
\theta_{l+V-1}
and
\varsigma_{l}
and
\theta_{l}
are related by a difference of
V-1
is equation
7
and
8
respectively. This non-interactive argument signs the payment information
\mathit{payinfo}
.

•:

Output a payment
\mathit{pay}\leftarrow(\kappa,\sigma^{\prime},\phi_{V,l},\varphi_{V,l},\allowbreak\varsigma^{\prime}_{l},\allowbreak\theta^{\prime}_{l},\allowbreak\varsigma^{\prime}_{l+V-1},\allowbreak\theta^{\prime}_{l+V-1},\allowbreak R^{\prime}_{l+V-1},\allowbreak S^{\prime}_{l+V-1},\allowbreak T^{\prime}_{l+V-1},\allowbreak R,\allowbreak\pi_{v},\allowbreak V)
and an updated wallet
\mathit{W}^{\prime}\leftarrow(\sigma,\allowbreak\mathit{v},\allowbreak l+V)
.

\mathsf{SpendVf}(\mathit{pk},\mathit{pay},\mathit{payinfo})
.:

Execute the following steps:

•:

Parse
\mathit{pay}
as
(\kappa,\sigma^{\prime},\phi_{V,l},\varphi_{V,l},\varsigma^{\prime}_{l},\allowbreak\theta^{\prime}_{l},\allowbreak\varsigma^{\prime}_{l+V-1},\allowbreak\theta^{\prime}_{l+V-1},\allowbreak R^{\prime}_{l+V-1},\allowbreak S^{\prime}_{l+V-1},\allowbreak T^{\prime}_{l+V-1},\allowbreak R,\allowbreak\pi_{v},\allowbreak V)
.

•:

Parse
\sigma^{\prime}
as
(\mathit{h}^{\prime},\allowbreak s^{\prime})
and output
0
if
\mathit{h}^{\prime}=1
or if
\mathit{e}(\mathit{h}^{\prime},\kappa)\allowbreak=\allowbreak\mathit{e}(s^{\prime},\mathit{\tilde{g}})
does not hold.

•:

Output
0
if
R\neq H(\mathit{payinfo})
or if
\mathit{payinfo}
does not contain the identifier of the merchant.

•:

Verify
\pi_{v}
by using
\mathit{payinfo}
,
\mathit{pk}
,
\phi_{V,l}
,
\varphi_{V,l}
,
\varsigma^{\prime}_{l}
,
\theta^{\prime}_{l}
,
\varsigma^{\prime}_{l+V-1}
,
\theta^{\prime}_{l+V-1}
,
R^{\prime}_{l+V-1}
,
S^{\prime}_{l+V-1}
,
T^{\prime}_{l+V-1}
,
V
,
R
and
\kappa
. Output
0
if the proof is not correct, else output
V
.

\mathsf{Identify}(\mathit{params},\mathit{PK},\mathit{pay}_{1},\mathit{pay}_{2},\mathit{payinfo}_{1},\mathit{payinfo}_{2})
.:

Execute these steps:

•:

Parse
\mathit{pay}_{1}
as
(\kappa_{1},\allowbreak\sigma^{\prime}_{1},\allowbreak\phi_{V_{1},l_{1},1},\allowbreak\varphi_{V_{1},l_{1},1},\varsigma^{\prime}_{l_{1},1},\allowbreak\theta^{\prime}_{l_{1},1},\allowbreak\varsigma^{\prime}_{l_{1}+V_{1}-1,1},\allowbreak\theta^{\prime}_{l_{1}+V_{1}-1,1},\allowbreak\\
R^{\prime}_{l_{1}+V_{1}-1,1},\allowbreak S^{\prime}_{l_{1}+V_{1}-1,1},\allowbreak T^{\prime}_{l_{1}+V_{1}-1,1},\allowbreak R_{1},\allowbreak\pi_{v,1},\allowbreak V_{1})
.

•:

Parse
\mathit{pay}_{2}
as
(\kappa_{2},\allowbreak\sigma^{\prime}_{2},\allowbreak\phi_{V_{2},l_{2},2},\allowbreak\varphi_{V_{2},l_{2},2},\varsigma^{\prime}_{l_{2},2},\allowbreak\theta^{\prime}_{l_{2},2},\allowbreak\varsigma^{\prime}_{l_{2}+V_{2}-1,2},\allowbreak\theta^{\prime}_{l_{2}+V_{2}-1,2},\allowbreak\\
R^{\prime}_{l_{2}+V_{2}-1,2},\allowbreak S^{\prime}_{l_{2}+V_{2}-1,2},\allowbreak T^{\prime}_{l_{2}+V_{2}-1,2},\allowbreak R_{2},\allowbreak\pi_{v,2},\allowbreak V_{2})
.

•:

For
k\in[0,V_{1}-1]
, compute the serial numbers
\mathrm{SN}_{k,1}\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak\mathit{e}(\phi_{V_{1},l_{1},1}[2],\tilde{\delta}_{k})\allowbreak\mathit{e}(\phi_{V_{1},l_{1},1}[1],\tilde{\eta}_{V_{1},k})
. For
k\in[0,V_{2}-1]
, compute
\mathrm{SN}_{k,2}\allowbreak\leftarrow\allowbreak\mathit{e}(\phi_{V_{2},l_{2},2}[2],\tilde{\delta}_{k})\allowbreak\mathit{e}(\phi_{V_{2},l_{2},2}[1],\tilde{\eta}_{V_{2},k})
.

•:

Output
1
if none of the serial numbers
\mathrm{SN}_{k_{1},1}
, for
k_{1}\in[0,V_{1}-1]
, is equal to
\mathrm{SN}_{k_{2},2}
, for
k_{2}\in[0,V_{2}-1]
.

•:

Else, output
\mathit{payinfo}_{1}
if
\mathit{payinfo}_{1}=\mathit{payinfo}_{2}
.

•:

Else, let
k_{1}\in[0,V_{1}-1]
and
k_{2}\in[0,V_{2}-1]
be two indices such that
\mathrm{SN}_{k_{1},1}=\mathrm{SN}_{k_{2},2}
. Compute

\displaystyle T_{1}\leftarrow\mathit{e}(\varphi_{V_{1},l_{1},1}[2],\tilde{\delta}_{k_{1}})\mathit{e}(\varphi_{V_{1},l_{1},1}[1],\tilde{\eta}_{V_{1},k_{1}}),

and

\displaystyle T_{2}\leftarrow\mathit{e}(\varphi_{V_{2},l_{2},2}[2],\tilde{\delta}_{k_{2}})\mathit{e}(\varphi_{V_{2},l_{2},2}[1],\tilde{\eta}_{V_{2},k_{2}}).

For each
\mathit{pk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}}\in\mathrm{PK}
, check whether
T_{1}T_{2}^{-1}=\mathit{e}(\mathit{pk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}},\tilde{\delta}_{k_{1}}^{R_{1}}\tilde{\delta}_{k_{2}}^{-R_{2}})
and output
\mathit{pk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}}
if the equality holds. Output
\bot
if the equality does not hold for any
\mathit{pk}_{\mathcal{U}_{j}}\in\mathrm{PK}
.
